# Exercise program — 30 September 2026

Vetted exercise proposals by notebook. Each was checked for solvability and self-checking; where the anchor notebook runs quickly the vetter executed the check (the "check" line says so). Kept and revised exercises only; the public repository cannot hold answer keys, so every exercise ends in an assertion or a checkable expected result.

## Principles

- Every assert must be able to fail; nothing guaranteed by a brentq bracket or a library identity.
- Predict first, then run, then assert the sign or direction.
- Planted truth plus a Monte Carlo coverage table with MC s.e. = sqrt(p(1-p)/R).
- Invariance checks: country order, numeraire, units, re-referencing.
- Oracle design: write the check that would have caught a real library defect.

## 00_whats_new_in_puremacro_2_0

**[intermediate] Recover the planted state-dependent multipliers, and see why the instrument matters** (revise)

Your turn (insert after the section-2 cell, notebooks/00_whats_new_in_puremacro_2_0.py:107). The DGP at lines 83-91 plants a permanent effect of spending on the level of GDP: 0.9 when unemployment is above 6.5 at date t and 0.4 otherwise. lp_state_dep_iv regresses y_{t+h}-y_{t-1}, so the true beta_H = 0.9 and beta_L = 0.4 at every horizon. (1) Wrap the DGP in simulate_rz(seed, T=200, rho=0.0), drawing from a fresh np.random.default_rng(seed). Add a standard-normal `demand` shock so that GDP-growth noise is 0.2*demand and spending is 0.8*news + 0.3*noise + rho*demand. Estimate lp_state_dep_iv (same arguments as the notebook, but horizon=8) for seeds 0-99, and store beta_H, beta_L, se_H, lo_H and hi_L at h=0 and h=8. (2) Precision: y_{t+h}-y_{t-1} adds h future spending shocks to the residual. Use the DGP to predict se(h=8)/se(h=0) = sqrt((0.04 + 8*(0.04 + 0.73*(0.81+0.16)/2))/0.04), which is about 8.9. Compare it with the average reported se_H. At each horizon, in what share of seeds do the two regimes' 90% bands separate (lo_H > hi_L)? (3) Bias or noise? The h=8 mean falls below 0.9. Re-run with T=800 and explain the gap. Hint: the controls include lagged levels of a random-walk y. (4) Endogeneity: at rho=0 the OLS lp_state_dep (transition='threshold', threshold=6.5) already recovers 0.9/0.4, so the instrument is not needed yet. Set rho=-1 (spending that leans against demand shocks). Predict the OLS bias, 0.2*rho/(0.73+rho^2), and compare OLS with IV at h=0.

*Check:* Ran it: I inserted a reference solution plus these asserts after line 107 of a scratch copy and executed it with the rules' jupytext command. The notebook took 13 s (7 s unmodified), printed ALL CHECKS PASSED, and left the repo untouched. Asserts: abs(bH[:,0].mean()-0.9)<0.02 and abs(bL[:,0].mean()-0.4)<0.02; 6 < seH[1]/seH[0] < 11 and bH[:,1].std(ddof=1) > 5*bH[:,0].std(ddof=1); sep[0]==1.0 and sep[1]<0.5; abs(bH8_T800-0.9) < 0.6*abs(bH[:,1].mean()-0.9); IV at rho=-1 within 0.03 of [0.9,0.4]; (OLS at rho=-1 minus [0.9,0.4]) within 0.03 of -0.2/1.73; OLS at rho=0 within 0.02 of [0.9,0.4]. Printed values: mean beta_H at h0,h8 [0.904 0.776], mean beta_L [0.402 0.322], sd of beta_H [0.024 0.256], se_H ratio 8.06 (analytic 8.93), band separation [1.0 0.22], h8 mean beta_H 0.776 at T=200 and 0.851 at T=800, rho=-1 IV [0.910 0.406] vs OLS [0.784 0.283] (predicted shift -0.116), rho=0 OLS [0.902 0.402].

## 00_whats_new_in_puremacro_3_0

**[basic] Is 30 NUTS draws enough? Split-R-hat by hand** (revise)

The NUTS cell ran 2 chains of 30 draws after 15 warm-up steps. Predict first: can 60 retained draws reach split-R-hat <= 1.01, the usual threshold? (a) Read r_hat and ess_bulk for sigma from res_nuts.summary(). (b) Re-run model_nuts.estimate(data=df_data, method='nuts', n_draws=200, n_chains=2, burn_in=100, seed=42), which takes about 15 s, and read the same two numbers. (c) Write my_rhat(ch) for an (n_chains, n_draws) array. Split each chain into two halves of length n. Let B = n times the variance (ddof=1) of the half-chain means, and W = the mean of the within-half variances (ddof=1). Then R-hat = sqrt(((n-1)/n*W + B/n)/W). Confirm that my_rhat reproduces the summary's r_hat for both runs (use res.draws[:, :, 0]). In two sentences, say whether B or W drives R-hat at 30 draws, and whether the 200-draw run meets 1.01. Optional: n_draws=1000, burn_in=500 takes about 90 s.

*Check:* r30 = res_nuts.summary().loc['sigma']; res200 = model_nuts.estimate(data=df_data, method='nuts', n_draws=200, n_chains=2, burn_in=100, seed=42); r200 = res200.summary().loc['sigma']; assert r30.r_hat > 1.1 and r200.r_hat < r30.r_hat and r200.ess_bulk > r30.ess_bulk; assert abs(my_rhat(res_nuts.draws[:, :, 0]) - r30.r_hat) < 1e-10 and abs(my_rhat(res200.draws[:, :, 0]) - r200.r_hat) < 1e-10. RAN it with the jupytext --execute command on a modified scratch copy (scratchpad/wf/vet-nb00-ex/ex_final.py; the whole notebook took 22 s). It printed '30 draws : r_hat=1.3962 ess_bulk=13.6' and '200 draws: r_hat=1.0375 ess_bulk=85.4'. The hand R-hat matched both values to machine precision (1.3961796393770762, 1.0375494622320702), and all asserts passed. Expected conclusion: 200 draws still fail 1.01. In a separate script, 1000 draws/500 burn-in gave 1.0023 in 86.6 s, and 4 chains x 200 draws gave 1.0309 in 28.5 s.

**[intermediate] Finite differences vs kalman_score: find the V** (revise)

Section 1.1 claims that the finite-difference step h faces a trade-off. Test it by replacing the rho_a**t cell. Write loglik_at(sigma_val, rho_val). It should put the values into MOD_CODE_GRAD at full precision: MOD_CODE_GRAD.replace('sigma = 1.0;', f'sigma = {sigma_val!r};'), and the same for 'rho_a = 0.8;'. It should then rebuild with load_mod -> make_state_space_from_varobs -> build_state_space_sensitivities and return the loglik from kalman_score on the same y_sim. Use kalman_score's likelihood, which starts the filter at the stationary covariance. puremacro.state_space.kalman_filter defaults to a diffuse P0 = 1e6*I, which is a different likelihood with a different gradient. For h in [1e-2, 1e-4, 1e-6, 1e-8, 1e-10], compute central differences for sigma and rho_a and store maxerr[h] = max|FD - score|. Predict first: truncation error grows like h^2 and round-off like eps*|lnL|/h, so where should the minimum be? (eps**(1/3) is about 6e-6.) Explain why the error falls by about 1e4 from h=1e-2 to 1e-4 and why it rises again below the optimum. Note that build_state_space_sensitivities itself differentiates the decision rules by central differences with step 1e-5*|theta|. The floor you find therefore bounds the error of both gradients; it is not the error against an exact gradient.

*Check:* assert min(maxerr.values()) < 1e-6; assert 5e3 < maxerr[1e-2] / maxerr[1e-4] < 2e4  # O(h^2) truncation; assert maxerr[1e-10] > 100 * maxerr[1e-6]  # round-off returns; assert min(maxerr, key=maxerr.get) == 1e-6. RAN it with the jupytext --execute command on a modified scratch copy (scratchpad/wf/vet-nb00-ex/ex_final.py; whole notebook 22 s; the 20 re-solves took 0.36 s). Printed max|FD - score| = 6.77e-01, 6.74e-05, 1.15e-08, 7.29e-07, 7.49e-05 for h = 1e-2 to 1e-10. The ratios were 10042 (1e-2/1e-4) and 6496 (1e-10/1e-6), and all asserts passed. Per-parameter sigma/rho_a errors: 5.41e-3/6.77e-1, 5.41e-7/6.74e-5, 9.05e-9/1.15e-8, 3.82e-7/7.29e-7, 7.49e-5/3.20e-5, which match the proposal. Reference score: [3.424599, -12.628586] at loglik 76.7248 (printed by notebook cell 5).

## 01_wealth_inequality

**[intermediate] Is it heterogeneity or impatience? (fixed prices)** (revise)

The mixture betas = [0.96, 0.93] changes two things at once: it spreads β out, and it also lowers the mean β to 0.945. Keep (r_star, w_star) fixed throughout. Write a helper gini_K(betas) that sets betas_you, calls solve_permanent_types(build_type_you, [0.5, 0.5]) and returns the mixture Gini (lorenz_and_gini) and the mixture capital K = sum(mu * a_grid[:, None]). (a) Sanity check: [0.96, 0.96] must reproduce gini_ai. (b) Predict the sign of each part before you compute it. Then split gini_mix - gini_ai into a mean-shift part, Gini([0.945, 0.945]) - gini_ai, and a dispersion part, gini_mix - Gini([0.945, 0.945]). (c) Compare two levers of the same size: lower every household's β by 0.005 ([0.955, 0.955]), or spread β by ±0.005 around 0.96 ([0.965, 0.955]). Why does the small spread barely move the Gini even though it raises K? (d) Compare the mixture's K with ai['K']. Are (r_star, w_star) equilibrium prices for the mixture? Given your answers, re-read prompt (1) below: [0.955, 0.945] has mean β 0.95.

*Check:* g0,_ = gini_K([0.96, 0.96]); g_hom,_ = gini_K([0.945, 0.945]); g_mx, K_mx = gini_K([0.96, 0.93]); g_shift,_ = gini_K([0.955, 0.955]); g_spread,_ = gini_K([0.965, 0.955])
assert abs(g0 - gini_ai) < 1e-6
assert g_hom > g_mx                      # at fixed prices the whole gap is lower mean patience
assert g_shift - gini_ai > 5 * (g_spread - gini_ai) > 0
assert K_mx < 0.6 * ai['K']              # not an equilibrium at these prices
RAN: I appended this as a cell to a scratch copy of notebooks/01_wealth_inequality.py and executed it with the rule-4 jupytext command. The whole notebook took 29 s and every assert passed. Printed: degenerate 0.631986 vs gini_ai 0.631986 (diff 0.0e+00); mean-shift part +0.1941, dispersion part -0.0323 (total +0.1618); -0.005 shift +0.0473 vs ±0.005 spread +0.0062 (K 9.48); K_mix 3.96 vs K_ai 7.48. A separate probe reproduced all of the proposal's numbers: [0.9625,0.9575] 0.6329, [0.965,0.955] 0.6381, homogeneous 0.955 0.6793, homogeneous 0.95 0.7555, [0.955,0.945] 0.7597.

**[stretch] Re-clear the market: the decomposition in general equilibrium** (revise)

The fixed-price comparison is not an equilibrium (K_mix is far below K_ai). Redo it in general equilibrium. (a) Find the market-clearing r for the mixture [0.96, 0.93] with stationary_equilibrium_types(build_ge, [0.5, 0.5], excess_K, bracket). Here build_ge(r, t) rebuilds type t's VFIProblem at (r, wage_of_r(r)), and excess_K(r, pt) = sum(pt.mixture_distribution() * a_grid[:, None]) - ai['L'] * (alpha/(r+delta))**(1/(1-alpha)). Use bracket = (0.005, 1/0.96 - 1 - 0.002). Why must the upper end sit below the patient type's 1/β - 1? (b) Solve the homogeneous economy with the same mean β: aiyagari_steady_state(n_z=5, n_a=120, a_max=60.0, gamma=1.0, beta=0.945). (c) Predict how r moves in each economy relative to r_star. Then recompute the mean-shift part (homogeneous GE Gini - gini_ai) and the dispersion part (mixture GE Gini - homogeneous GE Gini). Explain why the answer to 'heterogeneity or impatience?' flips once r adjusts. Also explain why holding capital (K/Y) fixed, rather than mean β, gives the fair test of whether β-dispersion fattens the tail.

*Check:* assert ge.price > r_star and ai945['r'] > ge.price
assert abs(ai945['wealth_gini'] - gini_ai) < 0.02      # mean shift almost washes out in GE
assert g_mix_ge - ai945['wealth_gini'] > 0.1          # dispersion now does the work
assert g_mix_ge < gini_mix                            # the GE rise in r compresses inequality relative to fixed prices
RAN: same scratch copy and jupytext run as the first exercise; this cell took 9.2 s and every assert passed. Printed: GE mixture r=0.0354 (r*=0.0306), resid -2.0e-02 (from the discrete asset grid; about 0.3% of K), Gini 0.7513; GE homogeneous 0.945 r=0.0451, Gini 0.6259; GE mean-shift part -0.0061, dispersion part +0.1254. Also verified in scratch: recalibrating the mean β at fixed prices so that K_mix = K_ai (brentq over the mean, spread ±0.015) gives mean 0.9500, betas [0.965, 0.935], Gini 0.749. stationary_equilibrium_types returns r=0.0306=r* for that mixture, so this is a cheap alternative route to an equilibrium comparison.

## 02_aggregate_shocks

**[intermediate] R² vs Den Haan's dynamic forecast error** (revise)

The R² ≈ 0.999 printed after solving `ks` is the usual evidence for approximate aggregation. Den Haan (2010) argues it is a weak accuracy test. Reuse `ks`, `Z_path`, `burn` and `logK` from the cells above, with x = logK[burn:-1], y = logK[burn+1:] and zt = Z_path[burn:]. (1) Predict before you compute: what per-state R² does the naive random-walk rule log K' = log K earn? That rule has no economics (b0 = 0, b1 = 1). Score it with the same formula as `ks.r_squared`, R² = 1 − SS_res/SS_tot within each Z state (see krusell_smith.py `_regress_forecast`). (2) One-step error: feed the KS rule the TRUE log K every period and compute one_err = 100·|ks.b0[zt] + ks.b1[zt]·x − y|, in percent (log points). Report the max and the mean. (3) Den Haan dynamic error: set dyn = logK[burn], then iterate dyn ← ks.b0[Z_t] + ks.b1[Z_t]·dyn using only the Z path, never the simulated K, and compute dyn_err = 100·|dyn − y|. (4) Show that e_t = dyn_t − y_t satisfies e_t = b1[Z_t]·e_{t−1} + (one-step residual) with e_{−1} = 0. Deduce that max dyn_err ≤ max one_err / (1 − max b1). Then explain three things: why R² barely separates a good rule from a naive one; why the iterated error is several times the one-step error; and why the iterated error does not explode. Compare it with the peak-to-trough swing of log K.

*Check:* Append after the existing Your-turn cell (02_aggregate_shocks.py:249-267): `assert (r2_naive > 0.95).all(), r2_naive`; `assert dyn_err.max() > 3 * one_err.max()`; `assert dyn_err.max() <= one_err.max() / (1 - ks.b1.max())`; `assert dyn_err.max() < 2.0`. RAN IT: copied the .py to scratch/wf/nb02-denhaan/02_ex.py, appended the cell, and executed it with the rules' jupytext command. The whole notebook took 38 s wall time, and all asserts passed. Printed: 'naive R² per state = [0.9726 0.9671]  (KS rule [0.9987 0.9993])', 'one-step error: max 0.365%  mean 0.097%', 'dynamic error : max 1.650%  mean 0.429%  (bound 5.18%)', 'peak-to-trough swing of log K: 14.3%'. These match the proposal's scratch numbers. A standalone probe also confirmed that the notebook's rebuilt Z_path (lines 109-116) reproduces ks.r_squared exactly, so the scoring is consistent. The margins: naive R² exceeds 0.95 by at least 0.017; the dyn/one ratio is 4.5 against the required 3; dyn max is 1.65 against the 2.0 cap; the bound assert is an identity and fails only on a wrong implementation.

## 03_life_cycle_and_demographics

**[stretch] Mortality inside the Bellman equation vs after it** (revise)

Section 2 applied mortality only after solving. The household in `lc` plans as if it will surely reach age 39, and we only thinned out the old cohorts (a composition effect). A household that knows it may die discounts the next age by beta*s_j. Predict first: (a) will mortality inside the solve raise or lower mean wealth at every age? (b) Will the extra fall in K be smaller or larger than the composition effect K_uniform - K_mortality? Then rebuild the problem that `life_cycle_profile` solves (see puremacro/vfi/examples.py, `life_cycle_profile`). Import with `from puremacro.vfi import FiniteHorizonProblem, tauchen, life_cycle_distribution, age_profile` and set it up as follows: `z_grid, P = tauchen(n=5, rho=0.95, sigma=0.15)`; `a_grid = np.linspace(0, 40, 120)`; `kappa = np.exp(0.1*age - 0.0025*age**2)`; a return function `rf(ap, a, z, age, r_, xp=np)` with c = (1+r_)*a + kappa[age]*exp(z) - ap, CRRA utility with gamma=2, and -inf when c <= 0. Solve `FiniteHorizonProblem(a_grid=..., z_grid=..., P_z=P, return_fn=rf, beta=0.96, horizon=J, params={'r': 0.04}, survival=...).solve()` and get mean assets with `age_profile(life_cycle_distribution(sol, P), a_grid)`. First check that survival=None reproduces `assets_by_age`. Then solve with survival=surv and compute K_insolve = sum(w_mortality * assets_insolve). Explain the result with the Euler equation u'(c_j) = beta*s_j*(1+r)*E[u'(c_{j+1})]. Note that beta*(1+r) = 0.998 already, but beta*s_j*(1+r) falls to about 0.94 by age 39. Discuss: in this partial-equilibrium model, where do the assets of those who die go (accidental bequests)? How would actuarially fair annuities that pay (1+r)/s_j (Blanchard 1985) change the Euler equation? Finally, repeat the exercise with the steeper schedule np.clip(1 - 0.003*age, 0.85, 1.0).

*Check:* assert np.allclose(solve_assets(None), assets_by_age)            # rebuild matches life_cycle_profile
assert np.all(assets_insolve <= assets_by_age + 1e-12)            # mortality lowers wealth at every age
assert 0 < assets_insolve.argmax() < J - 1                         # the hump survives
assert K_insolve < K_mortality                                     # post-hoc reweighting understates the fall
assert (K_uniform - K_mortality) < 0.5 * (K_uniform - K_insolve)   # composition is less than half the total fall

RAN IT: I appended this cell to a scratch copy of notebooks/03_life_cycle_and_demographics.py (wf/lc03-mortality/nb03_final.py) and executed it with the rules' jupytext command. The whole notebook ran in about 3.5 s with every assert passing. It printed 'peak 8.53@29 -> 4.24@30; K uniform 3.58, post-hoc 2.80, in-solve 1.19' and 'composition share of total fall: 0.329'. The oracle's max abs diff was 0.0. In an earlier scratch run (nb03_ex.py), the steeper schedule gave peak 1.30@31 and K 0.20 in-solve vs 2.09 post-hoc. There the composition effect is 1.49, below half the total fall (1.69), so the decomposition assert also holds.

## 04_firm_dynamics

**[basic] A heavier fixed cost at a fixed price vs. in general equilibrium** (revise)

The Your-turn cell raises cf to 30 but keeps the old price p*. (a) First show this can't be an equilibrium: compute the entry value float(nu @ V_you) and compare it with ce. (b) Restore free entry. Write make_profit(cf_val), a copy of profit_at whose fixed cost is cf_val instead of the global cf. Re-solve free_entry_price(make_profit(30.0), nu, ce, (0.5, 30.0), P_z=P, beta=beta), then recompute the survival mask, the cutoff s[survive][0], the stationary g and the exit rate g @ (~survive). (c) Compare three cases: the baseline, fixed price (PE, from the cell above, with exit rate g_you @ (~survive_you)) and general equilibrium (GE). Explain in two sentences why the price rises and why the GE cutoff and exit rate fall between the baseline and PE values.

These numbers are for the maintainer only; publish just the asserts.

**[intermediate] Entry barriers shelter incumbents: turnover and productivity** (revise)

Using make_profit from the previous exercise, re-solve the free-entry equilibrium at cf = 20 for ce in [20, 40, 60, 80]. For each case record the price, the exit cutoff, the exit rate g @ (~survive) and the mean incumbent productivity g @ s. Show that it equals the exit rate, and explain why a stationary distribution requires this (sum both sides of g = g S + M nu). **[stretch] Selection depends only on ce/cf: a homogeneity check** (revise)

(a) With alpha = 2/3, show on paper that pi(s;p) = p^3 * alpha^2 (1-alpha) s^3 - cf. Deduce that scaling (cf, ce, p^3) by lambda > 0 scales W(s) by lambda and leaves the survival mask unchanged, so the free-entry price scales by lambda^(1/3). More generally the price scales by lambda^(1-alpha). (b) Verify this numerically. Solve the equilibrium at (cf, ce) = (10, 40) and at (20, 80). Assert that the two survival masks and exit rates are identical and that the price ratio is 2^(1/3). Which case from the previous exercise has the same cutoff as (10, 40), and why?

## 05_portfolios_and_preferences

**[intermediate] Euler errors: which solver is more accurate, and at what rate?** (revise)

Section 3 says the EGM-VFI gap comes from VFI's grid. Test that claim with the standard accuracy measure. At the true policy the Euler equation c^-gamma = beta(1+r) E[c'^-gamma] holds exactly, so any residual is solver error. Write euler_errors(a, c, aprime) that works at every node where the constraint does not bind (a[0] < a' < a[-1]). For each such node, interpolate next-period consumption c(a', z') linearly with np.interp for every z', weight by Pz[z], form c_impl = (beta*(1+r)*E[c'^-gamma])^(-1/gamma), and return ee = log10|1 - c_impl/c|. Use section 3's gamma=2 setup (rf, beta, r, income, Pz). Solve with solve_egm and with VFIProblem for n_a in {60, 120, 240} on np.linspace(0.01, 30, n_a), and record the mean ee for each solver. Before you run it, predict how much the mean should drop each time n_a doubles. VFI picks a' from the grid, so its error is O(h): log10 2 = 0.3 per doubling at most. EGM is exact on its endogenous grid and only linearly interpolated, so its error is O(h^2): about log10 4 = 0.6 per doubling. Stretch: find where EGM's worst errors sit (read the solve_egm docstring about the top of a_grid). Compare the worst ee on 25 <= a < 30 with a_max=30 (n_a=120) and with a_max=60 (n_a=240, same spacing). Then revisit prompt (2) above: at gamma=10, how much of the 'flattening' of egm.c[:, mid] near a=30 is still there with a_max=120?

*Check:* assert all(ee_egm[n] < ee_vfi[n] - 3 for n in (60,120,240)); gain_egm, gain_vfi = ee_egm[60]-ee_egm[240], ee_vfi[60]-ee_vfi[240]; assert gain_vfi > 0.3; assert gain_egm > 0.9 and gain_egm > gain_vfi + 0.4; stretch: assert worst_ee_band(a_max=30, n_a=120) > -1.0 and worst_ee_band(a_max=60, n_a=240) < -3.0. I ran this: I appended the cell to a scratch copy of 05_portfolios_and_preferences.py and ran it with jupytext --execute. The whole notebook took about 6 s and every assert passed. It printed: n_a=60 EGM -6.28 VFI -1.78; n_a=120 EGM -6.82 VFI -2.09; n_a=240 EGM -7.41 VFI -2.27. Gain over two doublings: EGM 1.13, VFI 0.49. ## 06_svar_identification

**[intermediate] Which identification recovers the planted monetary shock?** (revise)

The synthetic data were generated with a known structural shock: column 0 of B0_true (cell at lines 86-101). (a) Compute the true IRF of every variable to that shock, Psi_h @ B0_true[:, 0], with the VAR(2) recursion Psi_0 = I, Psi_1 = A1, Psi_h = A1 Psi_{h-1} + A2 Psi_{h-2} for h = 0..H. Cross-check it against puremacro.var.irf.irf([A1, A2], B0_true, H)[:, :, 0]. (b) Before you run anything, say what the recursive ordering with the rate last forces output's and prices' impact responses to be, then compare with B0_true[:, 0]. (c) Print the impact vectors of the truth, the Cholesky rate shock (c_point[0]) and the sign-restriction median (s_med[0]), plus each scheme's 90% band for output at h=0. (d) Divide each output IRF by its own rate impact and sum it over h = 0..12, giving the cumulative output loss per unit of rate impact. Compare the truth, Cholesky and the sign median. Explain why the Cholesky shock is wrong here: the DGP lets output and prices react within the quarter, which breaks the recursive zeros, so the 'rate shock' mixes the monetary shock with the third structural shock. Also explain why the sign-restriction set contains the truth even though its median is not an estimate of it: the band covers admissible rotations at the OLS estimates only. (e) Fix the Your-turn assert so prompt (2) no longer fails. The recursive zero applies whenever output comes before the rate in the ordering, not only when the rate is last.

## 07_local_projections

**[intermediate] Do 90% LP bands cover 90% of the time?** (revise)

A 90% band is a promise about repeated samples: across many samples it should contain the true beta_h 90% of the time. Test that promise in a setting where you know the truth. Reuse the section-1 simulation loop (notebooks/07_local_projections.py:114-121), but give the shock a single impact b = -0.5 with no recession split: y_t = phi*y_{t-1} + b*x_{t-1} + 0.3*b*x_t + 0.35*e_t, with phi = 0.55 and T = 360.
(a) Derive the true IRF by hand: psi_0 = 0.3b, psi_1 = phi*psi_0 + b, psi_h = phi*psi_{h-1}. lp_hac's outcome is y_{t+h} - y_{t-1}, but its slope on x_t is the same because y_{t-1} is already a control. Before you simulate, predict whether coverage will be exactly 0.90 at every h.
(b) Run R = 200 replications. Use seed 1000+r, and draw x and then e from the same generator. On each replication call lp_hac(d, y='y', x='shock', horizons=range(17), n_lags=2, alpha=0.10) and store beta, se and hit_h = (lo_h <= psi_h <= hi_h). Report coverage by h. The Monte Carlo standard error of one horizon's coverage is sqrt(0.09/R), about 0.021, so do not over-read any single horizon.
(c) Diagnose any shortfall. Compute ratio_h = mean(se_h)/sd(beta_h) and bias_h/sd(beta_h). Show that erf(z_0.95 * mean(ratio)/sqrt(2)) reproduces the mean coverage. That formula is the coverage of a normal band whose SE is off by that ratio.
(d) Stretch. First, rerun at phi = 0.95. Does the long-horizon bias grow, and can the SE ratio alone still explain coverage? Second, on the same phi = 0.55 draws, run la_lp(d, y='y', x='shock', horizons=range(17), n_lags=2, alpha=0.10). This is lag-augmented LP (Montiel Olea & Plagborg-Møller 2021). Are its bands wider? Do they cover better? Explain what lag augmentation buys and what it costs in this design: i.i.d. shock, T = 360, p_aug = 18, so 38 regressors with HC0 SEs.

*Check:* Asserts (student adds): `assert 0.80 < cov.mean() < 0.95`; `assert cov[0] >= 0.85 and cov[1] >= 0.85` (with a wrong psi_0 = 0 coverage at h=0 is 0.000; with psi_1 = b it is 0.020 at h=1, so this also checks the derivation); `ratio = ses.mean(0)/betas.std(0); assert abs(cov.mean() - math.erf(1.6449*ratio.mean()/math.sqrt(2))) < 0.015`. Stretch: `assert bias_h8(0.95) > bias_h8(0.55)`, where bias_h8 = ((betas.mean(0)-psi)/betas.std(0))[8:].mean(); `assert 0.80 < cov_la.mean() < 0.95`. Do NOT assert that la_lp covers better. RAN (conda work python, puremacro from the repo): I appended the cell to a scratch copy of 07_local_projections.py and ran it with the jupytext --execute build command. Whole notebook took 32.1 s: the lp_hac Monte Carlo 4.0 s, the la_lp one 24.2 s. Printed output: coverage 0.915 0.900 0.885 0.890 0.865 0.855 0.905 0.880 0.870 0.840 0.855 0.870 0.860 0.905 0.890 0.880 0.850, mean 0.877; se/sd ratio 0.89-1.03, mean 0.955; erf prediction 0.884 (diff -0.006); bias/sd at most 0.17; la_lp coverage mean 0.861 (range 0.830-0.905) with bands 2-8% wider; p_aug = 18. Robustness runs with R in {100, 200, 400}: phi=0.55 mean coverage 0.877-0.884 and erf diff -0.005 to -0.006. phi=0.95: mean coverage 0.851-0.868, h>=8 coverage 0.839-0.868, erf diff about -0.02, bias/sd over h>=8 0.15-0.23 vs 0.07-0.09 at phi=0.55. cov[0] and cov[1] were at least 0.870 in every run. All asserts passed.

## 08_garch_volatility

**[intermediate] Spurious GARCH persistence from a one-time variance break** (revise)

Each half of a sample can be an ordinary GARCH(1,1) with alpha+beta = 0.95, yet one permanent shift in the level of volatility can make the pooled fit look close to integrated. Predict first: will the full-sample alpha_hat+beta_hat come out above, below or equal to 0.95? (a) Build `r_b = r.copy(); r_b.iloc[1000:] *= 2.0`, so volatility doubles for good at t = 1000. Fit `garch11_fit` to `r_b`, to `r_b.iloc[:1000]` and to `r_b.iloc[1000:]`. Convert each persistence p into a volatility half-life `np.log(0.5) / np.log(p)`, the number of periods a variance shock takes to halve. Explain why the second half gives exactly the same alpha_hat and beta_hat as the unscaled `r.iloc[1000:]` (hint: scaling the data multiplies omega by c^2 and leaves alpha and beta unchanged). Then explain why a single-regime GARCH has to push beta_hat up to fit a sample whose variance level moves once and never comes back. (b) Stretch: repeat with pure iid noise, `z = pd.Series(np.random.default_rng(0).standard_normal(2000), index=r.index)`, with and without the same x2 break. Without the break alpha_hat is about 0, and beta_hat is then unidentified, so ignore it. With the break, GARCH 'effects' appear out of nothing and alpha_hat+beta_hat hits the optimiser's 0.999 ceiling. What does this imply for high estimated volatility persistence in long real samples that span a regime change, such as the Great Moderation?

*Check:* I ran it on a modified copy of 08_garch_volatility.py with the rules' jupytext --execute command. The notebook plus the check took 5.2 s, and all asserts passed. Code: `half_life = lambda p: np.log(0.5)/np.log(p)`; `r_b = r.copy(); r_b.iloc[1000:] *= 2.0`; `fit_b = garch11_fit(r_b)`; `fit_h1, fit_h2 = garch11_fit(r_b.iloc[:1000]), garch11_fit(r_b.iloc[1000:])`; `hl_full = half_life(fit_b.persistence)`; `hl_half = half_life(max(fit_h1.persistence, fit_h2.persistence))`; `assert abs(fit_h1.persistence-(ALPHA+BETA)) < 0.05 and abs(fit_h2.persistence-(ALPHA+BETA)) < 0.05`; `assert fit_b.persistence > ALPHA+BETA+0.03`; `assert hl_full > 3*hl_half`; (b) `z = pd.Series(np.random.default_rng(0).standard_normal(2000), index=r.index); z_b = z.copy(); z_b.iloc[1000:] *= 2.0; fz, fz_b = garch11_fit(z), garch11_fit(z_b)`; `assert fz.alpha < 0.02`; `assert fz_b.alpha > 0.01 and fz_b.persistence > 0.99`. Printed: full a+b = 0.9912 (half-life 78.3); halves 0.9451 and 0.9525 (half-life 14.3); DGP 0.95 (half-life 13.5); iid without break alpha = 0.001, beta = 0.976; iid with break alpha = 0.030, beta = 0.969, a+b = 0.9990.

## 09_growth_at_risk

**[stretch] Is the skew-t fit trustworthy in the tail?** (revise)

The third assert in the Your-turn cell, |cdf(GaR) - 0.05| < 0.02, can never fail. `downside_quantile` is found by root-finding on that same cdf, so the check only confirms that the cdf and ppf agree with each other. Test the fit against its targets instead. (a) For fci_pct in (10, 50, 90, 95, 98), set qd = qdict_at(np.percentile(FCI, fci_pct)) and fit = fit_skewt_to_quantiles(qd). Print four numbers: gap5 = |fit.ppf(0.05) - qd[0.05]|, the fit loss sum_tau (fit.ppf(tau) - qd[tau])^2, fit.alpha, and the model-free Kelley skewness of the targets, (Q95 + Q05 - 2*Q50)/(Q95 - Q05). Where does the gap jump? At that point, does alpha still have the same sign as the Kelley skewness? Print cdf(GaR) as well: does it catch the problem? (b) Is the flip in alpha economics or an optimizer failure? Refit at pct 95 starting from the pct-90 fit, init=(fit_tight.mu, fit_tight.sigma, fit_tight.alpha, fit_tight.nu). If you have time, also try maxiter=2000 from the default start (it is slower). Compare the losses and the alphas. (c) Look at the fitted nu. What can five quantiles between 5% and 95% tell you about tail thickness?

*Check:* def fit_loss(ft, qd): return sum((ft.ppf(t) - qd[t])**2 for t in qd)
def kelley(qd): return (qd[0.95] + qd[0.05] - 2*qd[0.50]) / (qd[0.95] - qd[0.05])
# audit[pct] = (qd, default fit) for pct in (10, 50, 90, 95, 98)
qd95, fit95 = audit[95]
fit95_w = fit_skewt_to_quantiles(qd95, init=(fit_tight.mu, fit_tight.sigma, fit_tight.alpha, fit_tight.nu))
gap95_w = abs(fit95_w.ppf(0.05) - qd95[0.05])
assert all(kelley(audit[p][0]) > kelley(audit[q][0]) for p, q in [(10,50),(50,90),(90,95),(95,98)])
assert kelley(qd95) < 0
assert fit_loss(fit95_w, qd95) < fit_loss(fit95, qd95)
assert fit95_w.alpha < 0 and gap95_w < 0.1

Printed for pct 10/50/90/95/98: gap5 0.005/0.010/0.061/0.227/0.250; loss 0.003/0.003/0.042/0.334/0.410; alpha -0.07/-1.47/-2.51/+0.07/+0.13; Kelley +0.015/-0.104/-0.209/-0.228/-0.240; cdf(GaR) = 0.050 at every pct, including the bad fits. The pct-95 refit started from the pct-90 fit gave gap5 0.073, loss 0.060 and alpha -2.71. In a separate scratch run, maxiter=2000 from the default start also reached loss 0.059 and alpha -2.72, with nu around 7.5e13; success stayed False. Starting from the pct-90 fit repairs pct 98 and pct 100 too: alpha -2.88 and -3.32, gap5 0.089 and 0.105.

## 10_staggered_did

**[intermediate] When does TWFE break? Effects that grow with exposure** (revise)

Section 1 plants a constant kick. That is the one case where static TWFE works (the notebook says so at section 2). Now let the effect grow with exposure: tau(e) = 1 + slope*e for e = t - g >= 0. Copy `_simulate_panel` into `_simulate_dynamic(slope)`. Keep the same seed and also store the columns `D` (treated indicator) and `tau`. For slope in {0, 0.2, 0.4}, compute three numbers. (i) Static TWFE: the OLS coefficient on D in a regression of y on D plus unit and time dummies (use `pd.get_dummies` and `np.linalg.lstsq`; puremacro has no TWFE helper). (ii) `callaway_santanna(p, n_boot=0).att_overall`. (iii) The truth: the mean of tau over the post-treatment (g,t) cells, which is the same unweighted cell mean that CS's overall ATT averages. Before you run anything, predict whether TWFE tracks the truth as slope rises. Then re-run both estimators on the noise-free outcome y = tau. This separates bias in what each estimator targets from sampling noise. Use Goodman-Bacon's forbidden comparisons to explain the direction of the TWFE gap: an already-treated cohort serves as the control while its own effect keeps growing.

*Check:* Ran it: executed a scratch copy of the whole notebook with the exercise cell appended, using jupytext --execute (5.0 s wall). Printed: slope 0/0.2/0.4 -> TWFE 1.058/1.214/1.371, truth 1.000/1.686/2.371 (= 1 + slope*72/21; 21 post cells, mean e 3.43), CS 1.129/1.815/2.501. Noise-free at slope 0.4: CS 2.3714 and TWFE 1.3136. All of these passed at slope 0.4: `assert truth - tw > 0.8`; `assert abs(cs_val - truth) < 0.3`; `assert np.isclose(cs_val - truth, cs.att_overall - TRUE_ATT)` (CS is linear in y, so its error is the baseline's slope-invariant +0.129 sampling error, the same logic as the notebook assert at 10_staggered_did.py:342); noise-free `assert np.isclose(cs_nf, truth) and truth - tw_nf > 1.0`.

**[stretch] No never-treated group: TWFE's negative weights and a sign flip** (revise)

Drop the never-treated group by setting cohorts to (5, 8, 11), and keep tau(e) = 1 + slope*e. (a) Run `callaway_santanna` with the default control and explain why it fails. Then switch to `control='not_yet_treated'`. (b) Compute TWFE's implicit weights on treated observations with Frisch-Waugh. Residualize D on the unit and time dummies to get D~, then set w_it = D~_it*D_it / sum(D~*D). Verify three things: the weights sum to 1; sum(w*tau) reproduces TWFE on the noise-free outcome y = tau exactly; and some (cohort, e) cells get negative weight. Say which cells, and why the long-exposure cells of the earliest cohort are penalized. (c) Predict, then sweep slope over {0, 0.4, 2.5}. What happens to TWFE as every unit's effect gets larger? At slope 2.5, compare TWFE's sign to min(tau). (d) Which post-treatment (g,t) cells can CS with not-yet-treated controls identify? What does its overall ATT estimate on y = tau? Compare it with the full-cell truth 1 + slope*72/21, and state what a missing clean control group costs you.

Weights sum to 1.0, and sum(w*tau) = noise-free TWFE = 0.7822 at slope 0.4. 148 of 666 treated observations have negative weight: each cohort-5 cell with e >= 6 carries -0.085. TWFE was 1.068 at slope 0, 0.850 at 0.4 and -0.294 at 2.5 (noise-free -0.3615), while min tau = 1. CS with not_yet_treated on y = tau gave exactly 1.8 at slope 0.4. That is the mean of tau over its 9 identified post cells (cohort 5 e=0..5, cohort 8 e=0..2, cohort 11 none), against a full-cell truth of 2.371. Asserts passed: sumw close to 1; wtau close to tw_nf; nneg > 0; tw(0.4) < tw(0); tw(2.5) < 0 < min_tau; cs_nf close to cs_target and ncells == 9.

It should equal the mean over segments of sum(seg^2)/sum(w^2), where seg is each Hann-windowed, demeaned segment of length 64 with step 32. Show that this identity catches both scale bugs but not the reversal. Say what kind of reference would catch a permuted frequency axis.

## 13_build_your_own_index

**[intermediate] Concentration vs comovement: why the premium falls while risk rises** (revise)

Recipe 4's covariance premium is a ratio: the cross-sector covariance terms divided by the no-covariance (idiosyncratic) variance. (a) Show that when R is equicorrelated, Pi(rho, w) = rho * [(sum_i w_i sigma_i)^2 - sum_i (w_i sigma_i)^2] / sum_i (w_i sigma_i)^2, so the premium is linear in rho. Code this closed form and assert that it matches SigmaObject(...).cov_premium_var to within 1e-12 for rho in {-0.2, 0, 0.3, 0.6, 0.85} and w in {equal weights, [0.5, 0.2, 0.1, 0.1, 0.1]}. Why must rho stay at or above -1/(N-1) = -0.25? (Try rho = -0.3 and see what SigmaObject does.) (b) Use linearity to find rho*, the correlation at which the concentrated portfolio shows the same premium as the equal-weight portfolio at rho = 0.6, and assert it. (c) Before running anything, predict: at rho = 0.6, if you tilt towards energy, the most volatile sector (w_en = [0.1, 0.1, 0.1, 0.1, 0.6]), do the premium and the total variance S.var(w) go up or down? Check your prediction. Then use S.var_no_cov and S.diag_contrib to say which term causes the change. Is the premium a measure of aggregate risk?

*Check:* Put this after the Your-turn cell. It reuses sig, labels, S (rho=0.6), w (equal) and SigmaObject.
def prem(r, ww):
    R_ = (1-r)*np.eye(5) + r*np.ones((5,5)); return SigmaObject(sig, R_, labels).cov_premium_var(ww)
def closed(r, ww):
    ws = np.asarray(ww)*sig; return r*(ws.sum()**2 - (ws**2).sum())/(ws**2).sum()
w_conc = np.array([.5,.2,.1,.1,.1])
for r in (-0.2,0,0.3,0.6,0.85):
    for ww in (w, w_conc): assert abs(prem(r,ww)-closed(r,ww)) < 1e-12
rho_star = 0.6*closed(1,w)/closed(1,w_conc)
assert abs(prem(rho_star,w_conc) - S.cov_premium_var(w)) < 1e-12
w_en = np.array([.1,.1,.1,.1,.6])
assert S.cov_premium_var(w_en) < S.cov_premium_var(w) and S.var(w_en) > S.var(w)
assert S.diag_contrib(w_en)[4]/S.var_no_cov(w_en) > 0.9
I RAN this in a modified copy through the jupytext --execute command (whole notebook about 3.5 s). All asserts passed. Output: max |lib - closed form| = 3.11e-15. Premium at 0.6: equal 1.9125, concentrated 1.3704. rho* = 0.8373. Energy tilt: premium 1.9125 -> 0.4173, var 3.8084 -> 8.4001, var_no_cov 1.3076 -> 5.9269, covariance part (var - var_no_cov) 2.5008 -> 2.4732, energy's share of var_no_cov 0.972. At rho = -0.3 the PSD clipping takes over: library -0.8601 vs closed form -0.9563. I also tested the proposed replacement for line 246, assert (premium_user > premium(0.6, weights_user)) == (rho_user > 0.6). At the shipped rho_user = 0.85 it passes (1.9414 vs 1.3704), and it passes at rho_user = 0.5, 0.62, 0.65 and 0.7. The current 'premium_user > 1.5' fails at 0.62 (1.4161), 0.65 (1.4846) and 0.5 (1.1420).

Predict first: which single event month shrinks the h = 12 IP band the most? Then run the Your-turn cell once for each event alone, keeping n_draws=600 and seed=42 so every run filters the same 159 sign-admissible rotations. For each event record three things: (a) the unit-effect h = 12 band width; (b) how many sign-admissible rotations the event rejects, which is r.n_traditional_accepted - r.n_narrative_accepted; (c) the event month's standardized reduced-form residuals, r.residuals[dates.get_loc(pd.Timestamp(ev)) - P] / np.sqrt(np.diag(r.Sigma)), which gives z(u) and z(ip). Then answer four questions. (1) Lehman and COVID are the most famous uncertainty months, so why do they barely bind? (2) What do the residuals of the months that do bind show that makes 'the shock was positive' a real restriction and not one that is already satisfied? (3) Compare the winning month alone with the default three-event EVENTS_TRY. What do the other two events add? (4) The band is measured after unit-effect normalization. Compare the median impact on u, r.irf_median[0, 0, 0], and the raw width, r.irf_upper[12, 1, 0] - r.irf_lower[12, 1, 0], across events. How much of the tightening comes from discarding rotations whose 'uncertainty shock' barely moves uncertainty? Bonus: rerun with seed=5 or seed=7. Which ranking survives, the band-width ranking or the rejection ranking?

It uses only names the notebook already defines: narrative_sign_svar :108, P/H/CI :113, dates :136, SIGN_PATTERN :148, unit_effect :153. It also uses NarrativeSignResult.residuals and .Sigma (puremacro/var/identify/_results.py:418,429).
CANDS=["1987-10-01","2001-09-01","2008-09-01","2020-03-01","2011-08-01"]; width,rej,zip_={},{},{}
for ev in CANDS: r1=narrative_sign_svar(Y,p=P,horizon=H,sign_matrix=SIGN_PATTERN,restrictions=[(ev,0,+1)],dates=dates,n_draws=600,ci=CI,seed=42); _,lo1,hi1=unit_effect(r1.irf_median[0,0,0],r1.irf_median[:,1,0],r1.irf_lower[:,1,0],r1.irf_upper[:,1,0]); width[ev]=hi1[12]-lo1[12]; rej[ev]=r1.n_traditional_accepted-r1.n_narrative_accepted; zip_[ev]=(r1.residuals[dates.get_loc(pd.Timestamp(ev))-P]/np.sqrt(np.diag(r1.Sigma)))[1]; assert r1.n_narrative_accepted>0

## 19_model_confidence_set

**[intermediate] Pairwise DM vs MCS when all 8 models are equally good** (revise)

Prompt (2) asks you to count how many pairwise DM tests 'falsely' reject. On real data you never know which rejections are false, so build a world where you do: a Monte Carlo in which every model is equally good by construction. First, predict three numbers: with M=8 models there are 28 pairwise DM tests at 10%. On average, how many reject? How often does at least one reject? How often does the 90% MCS keep all 8 models? Then simulate R=200 samples. For r in range(200): rng = np.random.default_rng(500 + r); common = rng.standard_normal((180, 1)); E = 0.7*common + 0.7*rng.standard_normal((180, 8)). This gives 8 forecast-error series, each with variance 0.98 and pairwise correlation 0.5. For every pair (a, b), run diebold_mariano(E[:, a], E[:, b], h=1, loss='mse') and count p < 0.10. Also run model_confidence_set(E**2, alpha=0.10, n_boot=500, seed=r) and record whether len(included) == 8. Report three numbers: the mean count of false rejections, P(at least one rejection) and P(MCS keeps all 8). Then explain two things. (i) Why is P(at least one) well below the independent-tests bound 1 - 0.9**28 ≈ 0.95? Hint: the 28 tests share models, so their outcomes are positively dependent. (ii) Why does the MCS keep the whole set about 1 - alpha of the time, while the pairwise race almost always 'finds' a winner? The MCS controls the family-wise probability of eliminating a best model, not the error rate of each pair.

*Check:* assert 2.0 < mean_false_rej < 3.6; assert p_any > 0.5; assert p_any < 1 - 0.9**28; assert p_mcs_all >= 0.85. I RAN this. I appended the cell to a scratch copy of notebooks/19_model_confidence_set.py and executed it with the jupytext command (whole notebook 4.7 s wall; the simulation alone 0.7 s). It printed: 'mean false DM rejections: 2.79 of 28 (28*0.10 = 2.8)', 'P(at least one DM rejection) = 0.750 (independent-test bound 1-0.9^28 = 0.948)', 'P(MCS keeps all 8) = 0.925; mean MCS size = 7.92'. All four asserts passed. Robustness: with seed bases 1500 and 7000 instead of 500, the numbers were (2.96, 0.75, 0.92) and (3.10, 0.76, 0.89), so the bands are not knife-edge.

## 20_unit_roots_with_power

**[stretch] Size at the unit root, and the negative-MA trap** (revise)

The power figure only means something if the tests are correctly sized, meaning they reject a TRUE unit root about 5% of the time. Copy `_rejection_rates` into `_size_rates(phi, theta, T=200, n_rep=300, seed0=0, lags="auto")` and change three things. (i) Use MA(1) errors: set `rng = np.random.default_rng(seed0 + r)`, draw `e = rng.standard_normal(T + 1)`, set `v = e[1:] + theta * e[:-1]`, and use `u[t] = phi * u[t-1] + v[t]`. (ii) Also run `ng_perron_test(y, regression="ct")`, and count an MZt rejection when `res["stat"] < res["crit_values"]["5%"]`. ADF and DF-GLS keep `p_value < 0.05`. (iii) Pass `lags` through to all three tests and return (ADF, DF-GLS, MZt) rejection rates. At phi=1 the null is true, so each rate is the test's size. Before you run anything, predict which test holds its size best when theta<0. Compute size at theta = 0, -0.5 and -0.8. (a) Does the theta=0 row back up the '~5%' claim under the power figure? (b) How much does a negative MA root inflate size? Does MZt remove the problem or only reduce it? (c) Re-run theta=-0.8 with `lags=8`. Why does ADF's size fall back toward 5%? What does that tell you about SIC choosing too few augmenting lags when an MA root nearly cancels the unit root? Then compare phi=0.9, theta=0 with auto lags and with `lags=8` to see how much power the fix costs.

*Check:* Paste after your function:
s0, s5, s8 = (_size_rates(1.0, th) for th in (0.0, -0.5, -0.8))  # each = (ADF, DF-GLS, MZt)
assert max(s0) < 0.08                     # all three near nominal size with iid errors
assert s5[0] > 2 * s0[0]                  # MA(-0.5) at least doubles ADF's size
assert s5[2] <= s5[0] and s8[2] <= s8[0]  # MZt is less distorted than ADF
assert s8[2] > 0.25                       # ...but far from fixed at theta=-0.8
s8_l8 = _size_rates(1.0, -0.8, lags=8)
assert s8_l8[0] < 0.5 * s8[0]             # more lags absorb the MA root
assert _size_rates(0.9, 0.0, lags=8)[0] < _size_rates(0.9, 0.0)[0]  # and cost power

## 21_dynare_vfi_dsl

**[basic] No risk, no gap: recover the deterministic steady state** (revise)

Read set_budget in the setup cell. The constraint the code solves is c + k' = e^z k^alpha + (1-delta) k, so z scales the return on the household's own capital. That is capital-income risk, not the labor-income budget c + a' = (1+r)a + wz written in the math cell. (a) From the Euler equation 1 = beta(alpha k^(alpha-1) + 1 - delta), compute k_det from model.params. (b) Re-solve the notebook model with an innovation std of 0.01 instead of 0.15 (same grid; reuse model.utility_fn and model.budget_fn) and compare its mean capital with k_det. (c) By what percentage does the baseline mean_assets exceed k_det? Before going on, predict whether that whole gap is precautionary saving.

*Check:* p = model.params; k_det = (p['alpha']/(1/p['beta']-1+p['delta']))**(1/(1-p['alpha'])); res0 = <model with add_shock('z', 0.90, 0.01, 5)>.solve(howard_steps=15, tol=1e-6); step = a_grid[1]-a_grid[0]; assert abs(k_det-4.294) < 1e-3; assert abs(res0['mean_assets']-k_det) < step; assert mean_assets > k_det + 1.0. I ran it with jupytext on a modified copy (scratchpad/wf/ex21vet/nb21_ex.py). The whole notebook took 28.7 s and every assert passed. It printed: k_det=4.2940, mean(sigma=0.01)=4.4447, step=0.2525, baseline=6.6767, ratio=1.555.

**[intermediate] How much of the gap is precaution? A risk-neutral benchmark and a gamma sweep** (revise)

Is the 55% gap all precautionary saving? Build a risk-neutral benchmark. With linear utility the Euler equation is 1 = beta E[alpha e^{z'} k'^(alpha-1) + 1 - delta | z], so k_rn(z) = k_det * (E[e^{z'}|z])^(1/(1-alpha)). Average it over pi = markov_stationary(results['P_z']) (from puremacro.vfi), using results['z_grid'], and ignore the c>0 constraint. Then solve the model for gamma in {0.1, 0.5, 2, 3.5, 5} on a grid with a_max=45 and n=180. The spacing matches the notebook grid, but the ceiling no longer binds. Avoid gamma=1, where the CRRA lambda divides by zero. Split mean(gamma=2) - k_det into two parts. The first, k_rn - k_det, comes from investing more while persistent productivity is high: E[e^z] > 1 and k_rn(z) is convex. The second, mean(gamma=2) - k_rn, is precautionary saving against capital-income risk. Why does the second part grow with gamma? Note that gamma also sets the EIS = 1/gamma.

*Check:* k_rn = float(pi @ (k_det*(P @ np.exp(z))**(1/(1-alpha)))); means = [solve(gamma=g, a_max=45, n=180)['mean_assets'] for g in (0.1,0.5,2,3.5,5)]; assert np.all(np.diff(means) > 0); assert abs(means[0]/k_rn - 1) < 0.05; assert means[2] > k_rn > k_det. I ran it with jupytext in the same modified copy and every assert passed. It printed: k_det=4.294, k_rn=5.352, means=[5.318, 5.435, 7.486, 11.576, 16.043] (10.3 s for the five solves).

**[stretch] The Gini trap: a grid ceiling and a shifted Gini** (revise)

The notebook's stretch prompt says that raising sigma_z from 0.15 to 0.25 widens the Gini. Run it: results['gini'] falls from 0.392 to 0.370. Find out why before believing either answer. (a) Compute the exact stationary distribution with mu = stationary_distribution(res['policy_idx'], res['P_z']) (from puremacro.vfi) and the mass at the top grid node, mu[-1].sum(). Compare a_max with k_rn(z_max) from the previous exercise. (b) Compute the standard wealth Gini with lorenz_and_gini(mu, np.broadcast_to(res['a_grid'][:, None], mu.shape))[2]. Compare it with res['gini'], which is a Gini of a - min(a) over the final period of a 2000-agent panel. Try sigma=0.01 to see how differently the two measures behave. (c) Repeat (a) and (b) with a_max=60 and n=240, then state what more capital-income risk does to wealth inequality.

*Check:* assert top_mass(a_max=15, sigma=0.25) > 0.10; assert top_mass(a_max=60, sigma=0.25) < 0.03; assert exact_gini(a_max=60, sigma=0.25) > exact_gini(a_max=60, sigma=0.15) + 0.05; assert exact_gini(a_max=15, sigma=0.01) < 0.05. I ran it with jupytext in the same modified copy and every assert passed. It printed: a_max=15: reported 0.392 -> 0.370, exact 0.349 -> 0.362, mass at a_max 0.082 -> 0.180. a_max=60: reported 0.450 -> 0.529, exact 0.412 -> 0.528, mass at a_max 0.000 -> 0.017. sigma=0.01: reported 0.897, exact 0.012 (9.7 s). The sigma=0.25 level has not converged in the grid (exact Gini 0.508/0.528/0.549 at a_max 45/60/80), so the checks test only the direction.

**[stretch] When does the asset grid stop being innocent? Truncation near and at r = rho** (revise)

In the first exercise, mass piled up on a_max as r approached rho. Now test whether the truncation is what drives E[a]. ## 23_aiyagari_endogenous_labor

**[basic] Prices from the interest rate, then factor shares** (revise)

The firm's first-order conditions pin down K/L and w from r alone: r = alpha (K/L)^(alpha-1) - delta and w = (1-alpha)(K/L)^alpha, with A = 1. Using only `r_star` from the setup cell (alpha = 0.36, delta = 0.08), compute K/L and w. Compare them with `K_star / L_star` and `w_star`. Then compute Y = K_star**alpha * L_star**(1-alpha) and the two factor shares, (r_star + delta) * K_star / Y and w_star * L_star / Y. Why must these shares equal alpha and 1 - alpha? Why does matching K/L test market clearing, when the solver computes w_star directly from r_star?

*Check:* alpha, delta = 0.36, 0.08; kl = ((r_star+delta)/alpha)**(1/(alpha-1)); w = (1-alpha)*kl**alpha; Y = K_star**alpha * L_star**(1-alpha); assert abs(kl/(K_star/L_star)-1) < 1e-4; assert abs(w/w_star-1) < 1e-4; assert abs((r_star+delta)*K_star/Y - alpha) < 1e-4; assert abs(w_star*L_star/Y - (1-alpha)) < 1e-4. The title promises placebo inference, so do it. ## 25_frequency_connectedness

**[basic] White noise has no frequency: spillover from correlated shocks alone** (revise)

Before running anything, predict: if the VAR has no dynamics (A1 = 0) but keeps the notebook's correlated Sigma, which band shows more spillover, high (pi/4, pi) or low (0, pi/4)? Write a helper simulate(A, S, T, seed=123) that repeats the notebook's simulation loop with a fresh np.random.default_rng(seed). Simulate T=5000 with A=np.zeros((N, N)) and the notebook Sigma, then compute both band indices with barunik_krehlik(..., var_lags=1). Next, derive the answer on paper. With A = 0, Psi(e^{-i w}) = I at every w, so theta_jk(w) = Sigma_jk^2 / (Sigma_kk * Sigma_jj) is the same at every frequency. Row-normalize that matrix and compute 100/N times its off-diagonal sum from Sigma alone. Explain why a flat spectrum makes every band look the same. Optional: rerun with T=300 and a few seeds to see how far sampling noise moves each band.

*Check:* d = np.diag(Sigma); theta0 = Sigma**2/np.outer(d, d); theta0 /= theta0.sum(1, keepdims=True); closed_form = 100*(theta0.sum()-np.trace(theta0))/N; assert abs(h_wn - l_wn) < 1.0; assert abs(h_wn - closed_form) < 1.5 and abs(l_wn - closed_form) < 1.5. I ran this with jupytext on a modified copy of the notebook in scratch/wf/vet25freq/nb_ex.py (the whole notebook runs in about 3 s). It printed 'EX1 closed form 11.60  high 11.32  low 11.64', and all asserts passed.

**[intermediate] Two channels: correlated shocks vs cross-lag propagation** (revise)

Now switch off correlated shocks (S = np.eye(N)) so that only lag dynamics can create connectedness. (a) Use A = np.diag([0.9, 0.5, 0.2, 0.7]). The variables differ a lot in persistence but have no cross-lags. Predict both band indices, then compute them at T=5000 with your simulate helper. (b) Repeat with the notebook's A1 and S = I. Which band carries the spillover now, and why? Use the moduli of A1's eigenvalues (np.abs(np.linalg.eigvals(A1))) in your answer. (c) Compare with the notebook's 9.60 (high) and 60.31 (low) and with your basic-exercise result. Which channel produces most of the notebook's high-band connectedness, and which produces most of its low-band connectedness?

*Check:* assert max(h_diag, l_diag) < 1.0; assert l_a1 > 40 and h_a1 < 5 and l_a1 > 10*h_a1. I ran this in the same modified copy. It printed 'EX2 diag A: high 0.11 low 0.43 | notebook A1: high 2.80 low 60.29', and the asserts passed. Over seeds 0-5 at T=5000, the diagonal case never went above 0.41, and A1 with S = I always gave high 2.65-3.06 and low 58.3-63.1. Expected answers: (a) own persistence changes each variable's spectrum but moves no variance between variables. (b) Cross-lags pass shocks through a near-unit-root system (largest |eig| = 0.985), so connectedness builds up at low frequencies. (c) The notebook's high band mostly reflects contemporaneous correlation (about 11.6 under white noise), while its low band comes from cross-lag dynamics.

(a) The notebook's current intermediate prompt says var_lags=2 'enriches low-frequency connectedness'. Test that claim: recompute the low band (0, pi/4) with var_lags=2 on the notebook's data. Explain the result from the process that generated the data. (b) Compute the full-band index with freq_bounds=(0.0, np.pi). Is it the average of the high (9.60) and low (60.31) indices? Read puremacro/connectedness/bk2018.py lines 37-45 to see what each band's theta is divided by. Then explain why band indices do not add up and why the full-band number sits close to the low band. (c) Compute the time-domain Diebold-Yilmaz generalized index with spillover_index(pd.DataFrame(data), var_lags=1, fevd_horizon=200) from puremacro.connectedness. Compare it with the full band. Why should the two agree, and what in bk2018.py (the frequency grid) could explain the small gap that remains?

*Check:* assert abs(low2 - total_low) < 0.5; assert min(total_high, total_low) < full < max(total_high, total_low); assert abs(full - total_low) < abs(full - (total_high + total_low)/2); assert abs(full - dy) < 1.5. I ran this in the same modified copy. It printed 'EX3 low p=1 60.31 p=2 60.32 | high 9.60 low 60.31 full 57.76 avg 34.95 | DY H=200 56.91', and all asserts passed. Expected answers: (a) the data come from a VAR(1), so the lag-2 estimates are near zero and the spectrum barely changes. (b) Each band is normalized by its own integrated spectral variance and then row-normalized (bk2018.py:40-45), so the indices are shares within each band. In the estimated VAR, 89-96% of each variable's variance lies in [0, pi/4], so the full index follows the low band. ## 26_cycles_and_bandpass

**[basic] Filters on known inputs (1): Hamilton on a pure random walk** (revise)

A series with no cycle at all: rng = np.random.default_rng(7); y_rw = np.cumsum(0.006 + rng.normal(0, 0.008, 4000)). Before you run anything, work out what hamilton_filter(y_rw, h=8, p=4) should return. For a random walk with drift, the best linear forecast of y_{t+8} given (y_t, ..., y_{t-3}) is y_t + 8*0.006, so the Hamilton 'cycle' is the sum of the next 8 shocks. Predict its standard deviation in closed form. Then check your prediction, and check that the cycle is almost exactly the 8-quarter change y_{t+8} - y_t. How does this change the way you read the Hamilton row (ratio > 1) in the Your Turn table? The notebook's trend shocks have the same sigma, 0.008.

*Check:* pred = np.sqrt(8)*0.008  # 0.02263
ham_rw, _ = hamilton_filter(y_rw, h=8, p=4)
assert abs(np.nanstd(ham_rw)/pred - 1) < 0.05
d8 = np.full(4000, np.nan); d8[8:] = y_rw[8:] - y_rw[:-8]
ok = np.isfinite(ham_rw) & np.isfinite(d8)
assert np.corrcoef(ham_rw[ok], d8[ok])[0,1] > 0.99
RAN via the jupytext command on a modified copy (scratch nb26_ex.py, whole notebook 2.98 s). It printed std=0.02294, pred=0.02263, relerr=0.0137, corr(8-diff)=0.9958. In a separate probe, seeds 0-7 gave corr 0.9958-0.9997 and seeds {7,42,0,1,2} gave relerr of 3% or less.

**[intermediate] Filters on known inputs (2): Baxter-King weights and white noise** (revise)

The library does not return Baxter-King weights, but you can read them off a unit impulse. Build imp = np.zeros(49) with imp[24] = 1 and run baxter_king_filter(imp, low=6, high=32, K=12). The 25 finite outputs are the filter weights. (1) Check that they sum to zero, which is the zero-frequency constraint in the Methods cell, and that they match a_k - theta computed from the formula there. (2) For unit white noise, the variance of the filtered series equals the sum of squared weights. An ideal 6-32 quarter bandpass would pass exactly the share (w2 - w1)/pi = 2/6 - 2/32 of the variance. Compute both numbers and confirm the first by filtering 20,000 standard-normal draws (seed 0). (3) Explain, using Parseval (var = (1/pi) * integral from 0 to pi of G(w)^2 dw), why the truncated filter passes less than the ideal one. Repeat with K=36 and watch the gap shrink.

*Check:* imp = np.zeros(49); imp[24] = 1.0
c, _ = baxter_king_filter(imp, low=6, high=32, K=12)
w = c[np.isfinite(c)]
assert len(w) == 25 and abs(w.sum()) < 1e-10
ss = np.sum(w**2); ideal = 2/6 - 2/32
assert abs(ss - 0.2562) < 1e-3 and ss < ideal
e = np.random.default_rng(0).standard_normal(20000)
bk_e, _ = baxter_king_filter(e, low=6, high=32, K=12)
assert abs(np.nanvar(bk_e) - ss) < 0.02
RAN in the same jupytext execution. It printed sum w=6.77e-17, sumsq=0.2562, ideal=0.2708, simvar=0.2602. The l.31-32 formula also gives 0.25623 exactly. A probe over seeds 0-9 at T=20000 gave mean 0.2545, sd 0.0051, range [0.2466, 0.2628], all within the tolerance. K=36 gives 0.2652 and K=60 gives 0.2675 (probe).

**[stretch] Filters on known inputs (3): the spurious cycle in a random walk** (revise)

A random walk has no cycle, yet HP and Baxter-King both find one (Cogley & Nason 1995; Hamilton 2018). Get each filter's weights from a unit impulse in the middle of a long vector: N=801 with the impulse at index 400. For HP use np.asarray(hp_filter(imp, lamb=1600).cycle); for BK use baxter_king_filter(imp, 6, 32, 12) with NaNs set to 0. On a fine grid of w in (0, pi], compute the gain G(w) = sum_k w_k cos(w k). The spectrum of a filtered random walk is proportional to S(w) = G(w)^2 / (2(1 - cos w)). Find the period 2*pi/w* at which S peaks for each filter. For HP, start from the closed-form cycle gain 4*lam*(1-cos w)^2 / (1 + 4*lam*(1-cos w)^2), show that the peak satisfies 4*lam*(1-cos w)^2 = 3, and compare this with your numerical peak. Both peaks fall inside the 6-32 quarter band. Explain in terms of the gain and the 1/(2(1-cos w)) random-walk spectrum why the filter manufactures business-cycle periodicity. Optional: confirm the result by averaging periodograms of 200 filtered simulated random walks.

*Check:* per_hp_analytic = 2*np.pi/np.arccos(1 - np.sqrt(3/(4*1600)))  # about 30.14 quarters
assert abs(per_hp - per_hp_analytic) < 0.5
assert 6 <= per_hp <= 32 and 6 <= per_bk <= 32
RAN in the same jupytext execution. It printed the peak period HP=30.15 (analytic 30.14) and BK=23.48. A probe also found that the HP impulse-response gain matches the closed form to 1.5e-13. Averaged periodograms of 200 random walks with T=400 peak at 28.6 raw and 30.8 after 3-bin smoothing for HP, and at 23.5 raw and 22.1 smoothed for BK.

## 28_weak_iv_anderson_rubin

**[basic] Who covers the truth? Wald vs Anderson-Rubin coverage with a weak instrument** (revise)

Turn the setup cell into a Monte Carlo. (1) Write simulate(rng, z_load, z_sd) that draws a new sample of the setup DGP: shock ~ N(0,1), nu ~ N(0,1.2), x = 0.8*shock + nu, y = np.convolve(shock, beta_true)[:T] + N(0,0.5), z = z_load*shock + N(0, z_sd). (2) Write mc(z_load, z_sd, h, n_rep, seed) that runs lp_iv(df, y='y', x='x', z='z', horizons=[h], n_lags=2, anderson_rubin=True) on each draw and records three things: whether the Wald interval [lo, hi] contains the IV estimand beta_true[h]/0.8, whether the AR set contains it, and whether the AR set is unbounded. Test AR membership by ar_set_type. For 'bounded', check ar_lo <= b <= ar_hi. For 'unbounded_rays' the set is (-inf, ar_hi] U [ar_lo, inf), so check b <= ar_hi or b >= ar_lo. 'all_real' always covers and 'empty' never does. (3) Before you run it, write down your prediction for each 90% set's coverage at h=0 with the weak instrument (z_load=0.12, z_sd=1.0) over 1000 replications. Then run it and report the Wald coverage, the AR coverage, the share of AR sets that are unbounded and the median first-stage F. (4) The Reading-the-Output cell says Wald 'can drop to below 50%'. Explain why Wald does not undercover here. Hint: beta_true[0] = 0, so is x_t correlated with the h=0 structural error? What does the unbounded share tell you about the cost of robust inference when F is about 1?

*Check:* assert abs(cov_ar - 0.90) < 0.035 and cov_wald > 0.95 and share_unbounded > 0.5. RAN: I appended the solution to a scratch copy of the notebook (scratchpad/wf/nb28-weakiv/nb28_mod.py) and executed it with the jupytext command. With seed 2028 and 1000 reps it printed Wald 0.996, AR 0.903, unbounded 0.735, median F 1.18, and the assert passed. Across a 20-seed scan (1000 reps each) AR ranged 0.872-0.910, Wald 0.987-0.996 and the unbounded share 0.688-0.737, with 0 failures. A 4000-rep run gave Wald 0.993 and AR 0.894.

The notebook says AR is 'exact regardless of instrument strength'. Test that claim along a different axis. **[intermediate] Where lambda-hat looks: the time-weight moment that cancels the factor bias** (revise)

lambda-hat is fitted on donor data only (puremacro/did/synthetic_did.py:119). Compare the lambda printed in the Your Turn cell at loadings 1.4 and 3.0: they are identical. Donors differ only through alpha_i, which the lambda intercept absorbs, and through l_i*f_t. So the lambda problem is solved by weights that satisfy sum_t lambda_t*f_t = mean post-period f. Verify this moment with f = macro_factor. To see why it matters, note that raising the treated loading by dl only adds dl*macro_factor to treated_traj, and both estimators below are linear in that path. Write did_at(l), the 2x2 DiD on treated_traj + (l - factor_loadings[0])*macro_factor. Write tau_lam_at(l), which uses uniform donor weights plus the SDID time weights: gap = y - unweighted_control_path and tau = gap[T_treat:].mean() - lam @ gap[:T_treat]. Derive each slope in l on paper (f_post_bar - f_pre_bar for DiD, f_post_bar - lam'f_pre for the lambda-weighted version) and evaluate both at l in {0.5, 1.4, 3.0, 5.0}. Use this to explain why SDID barely pays for its leftover loading gap (l0 - omega'l) while DiD pays fully.

*Check:* lam = res_sdid.lambda_w.to_numpy(); f_pre, f_post = macro_factor[:T_treat], macro_factor[T_treat:].mean(); assert abs(lam @ f_pre - f_post) < 0.05; assert np.isclose(did_at(3.0) - did_at(1.4), 1.6*(f_post - f_pre.mean())); assert np.isclose(tau_lam_at(3.0) - tau_lam_at(1.4), 1.6*(f_post - lam @ f_pre)); assert abs(tau_lam_at(3.0) - tau_true) < 0.5 < 2.0 < abs(did_at(3.0) - tau_true). RAN via jupytext; all asserts pass. Output: lam'f_pre 1.3773 vs f_post 1.3798, so the DiD slope is 1.3125 and the lambda slope is 0.0025. did_at gives -5.098/-3.917/-1.817/0.808 and tau_lam_at gives -4.082/-4.080/-4.076/-4.071 at l=0.5/1.4/3.0/5.0. The top lambda periods are 2, 1, 0 (0.335, 0.157, 0.153), identical across loadings.

## 30_narrative_bursts_and_transcripts

**[basic] Narratives can be wrong: a false bound excludes the truth** (keep)

The DGP (cell at l.176-181) is u_t = B0 e_t with B0 = [[1,0],[0.5,1]], so the true impact response of inflation to the policy shock is B0[1,0] = 0.5 (irf[0,1,0]). (a) The notebook's narrative (l.184-192) says the policy shock on row 21 was positive and at least 0.5. An integer date is a row of Y, and Y[21] is driven by e[21]. Look up e[21,0]: is the narrative true? (b) Re-run narrative_sign_svar with restrictions=[] and the same sign_matrix, p, horizon, bayes_draws, n_draws=500 and seed=42. That gives plain sign restrictions on the same Haar draws. Call the result sign_only. (c) Compare the 90% impact bands for inflation, irf_lower/irf_upper[0,1,0], from sign_only and svar_res. Does a narrower band mean a more accurate one? Explain why a lower bound on the date-21 shock becomes a lower bound on the inflation impact, given that the restriction is checked on eps = resid @ inv(B).T.

*Check:* B0_true = np.array([[1.0,0.0],[0.5,1.0]]); lo_s, hi_s = sign_only.irf_lower[0,1,0], sign_only.irf_upper[0,1,0]; lo_n, hi_n = svar_res.irf_lower[0,1,0], svar_res.irf_upper[0,1,0]; assert e[21,0] < 0.5; assert lo_s <= B0_true[1,0] <= hi_s; assert hi_n - lo_n < 0.6*(hi_s - lo_s); assert lo_n > B0_true[1,0]. RAN with the jupytext command on modified copies of both the EN and ES sources; all asserts passed. Printed: e[21,0]=0.481, sign-only [0.020,1.193], narrative [0.569,1.187]. Robustness (scratch script, seeds 0/1/2/3/42 x n_draws 500/4000): the narrative lower band was always 0.569-0.747 (>0.5), and the sign-only band always covered 0.5.

**[intermediate] True narratives about big events pull the posterior toward the truth** (revise)

Ludvigson, Ma and Ng lean on large, well-documented episodes. (a) Find t_star = 1 + argmax(e[1:,0]), the largest true contractionary shock in the sample. Impose the true restriction NarrativeRestriction(kind='shock_bound', date=t_star, shock=0, min_magnitude=1.5, sign=+1), with the same sign_matrix, p=1, horizon=10, bayes_draws=True, n_draws=500 and seed=42. Call the result true_res. (b) Compare its impact band and median for inflation with sign_only from the previous exercise. Does the band cover 0.5? Is the median closer to 0.5? (c) The library tests restrictions on estimated shocks (eps = resid @ inv(B).T). Compute the shock an econometrician would recover at the true B0: eps_hat = estimate_var(Y, 1)[3] @ inv(B0_true).T, where eps_hat row r is Y row r+1. Evaluate it at t_star and at date 21. Use the slack between eps_hat and each bound to explain why the big-event bound is safe and the date-21 bound is not. (Import estimate_var from puremacro.var.estimate.)

*Check:* assert e[t_star,0] >= 1.5 and eps_hat[t_star-1,0] >= 1.5; assert eps_hat[20,0] < 0.5; lo_t, med_t, hi_t = true_res.irf_lower[0,1,0], true_res.irf_median[0,1,0], true_res.irf_upper[0,1,0]; assert lo_t <= 0.5 <= hi_t; assert abs(med_t - 0.5) < abs(sign_only.irf_median[0,1,0] - 0.5). RAN with jupytext on both EN and ES copies; all asserts passed. Printed: t_star=101, e=2.905, eps_hat(t*)=2.771, eps_hat(21)=0.423, true-narrative band [0.011, median 0.644, 0.980] against a sign-only median of 0.922, 48/88 draws accepted. Robustness (seeds 0/1/2/3/42 x 500/4000 draws): the band always covered 0.5 (lower 0.011-0.083), and the median was always 0.58-0.70 against 0.91-0.98 for sign-only.

By construction, every month outside 2023-03..06 is pure random-template noise (l.110-117). ## 31_sequence_space_hank

**[basic] The budget constraint inside J_C_Y** (revise)

Column s of res.jacobian_c_y is the consumption path after aggregate income rises by 1% of Y in quarter s only. The rise reaches households through the wage; the labour tax only pays interest on the debt. Before computing anything, use the households' intertemporal budget constraint to predict x = sum_t (1+r_ss)^(-t) J_C_Y[t,0]. Compute x for the notebook's res (T=40). Then re-solve with T=200, keeping every other argument from section 1. Where did the missing 22% go at T=40? Check that column s=10, divided by q_10 = 1.01^-10, gives the same answer.

*Check:* def pv_col(r, s=0):
    T = r.jacobian_c_y.shape[0]; q = (1 + r.r_ss) ** -np.arange(T)
    return float(q @ r.jacobian_c_y[:, s]) / q[s]
kw = dict(beta=0.985, gamma=1.0, r_ss=0.01, phi_pi=1.5, kappa=0.1, shock_magnitude=0.0025, shock_rho=0.7, n_a=60)
res200 = solve_hank_sequence_space(T=200, **kw)
assert pv_col(res) < 0.8
assert abs(pv_col(res200) - 1) < 0.01
RAN it: I appended it to a scratch copy of notebooks/31_sequence_space_hank.py and ran the jupytext command. The whole notebook plus all exercises took 4.9 s. It printed T=40: 0.7745, T=200: 0.9934, s=10 at T=200: 0.9939. All asserts passed.

**[intermediate] Truncation or leak? Auditing the asset-grid cap** (revise)

Push T to 800. Does x from the previous exercise reach 1? It should not, which shows the remaining ~0.6% gap is not truncation. Find the leak. Look at res200.steady_state_wealth_dist[-1], the mass at the top grid point a_max=30 (the default). Compare res200.steady_state_consumption with w_ss*N = 1, and res.policy_a[-1] with res.asset_grid[-1]. Explain why a saving choice above a_max makes resources vanish from the simulated economy. Then re-solve with a_max=60.0 and confirm the budget identity holds. Lesson: accounting identities are free accuracy tests for any heterogeneous-agent Jacobian.

*Check:* x200 = pv_col(res200)
x800 = pv_col(solve_hank_sequence_space(T=800, **kw))
assert abs(x800 - x200) < 0.002 and 1 - x800 > 0.003   # more T does not help
x_big = pv_col(solve_hank_sequence_space(T=800, a_max=60.0, **kw))
assert abs(x_big - 1) < 1e-3
RAN it in the same scratch notebook. It printed: T=800 0.99435, top-grid mass 0.00289, C_ss 0.999892. With a_max=60 and T=800: 1.000000, top-grid mass 0.00e+00, C_ss 1.000000. A separate probe showed policy_a at a=30 is [29.20, 30.055], and 0.196% of households choose a' > a_max. All asserts passed.

**[intermediate] One Jacobian, many monetary rules** (revise)

The household Jacobians are partial-equilibrium objects. They never see the Taylor rule. Using only res.jacobian_c_y and res.jacobian_c_r from section 1, build the GE block from section 4's equations with kappa=0.1 and beta=0.985, truncating pi_T = 0. K[t,s] = kappa*beta^(s-t) for s >= t, else 0. M_rY = phi_pi*K - (K shifted up one row). Then solve (I - J_CY - J_Cr M_rY) dY = J_Cr eps with eps_t = 0.0025*0.7^t, for phi_pi in {1.25, 1.5, 2.5}, without recomputing any Jacobian. Verify against the library re-solve. Explain why a more hawkish inflation response makes the output contraction from the same rate shock smaller.

*Check:* for phi in (1.25, 1.5, 2.5):
    M = phi*K - K_next
    dY = np.linalg.solve(np.eye(T) - Jy - Jr @ M, Jr @ eps)
    ref = solve_hank_sequence_space(T=40, **{**kw, 'phi_pi': phi})
    assert np.allclose(ref.jacobian_c_y, Jy) and np.allclose(ref.jacobian_c_r, Jr)
    assert np.allclose(dY, ref.irf_output, atol=1e-12)
    dY0[phi] = dY[0]
assert dY0[1.25] < dY0[1.5] < dY0[2.5] < 0
RAN it in the same scratch notebook. It printed dY0 (%) = {1.25: -0.511, 1.5: -0.434, 2.5: -0.272}, matching the proposal. The hand-built solve matched irf_output to 1e-12, and the Jacobians were identical (array_equal True).

**[stretch] Whose MPC does J_C_Y[0,0] measure?** (revise)

The summary reports an aggregate MPC of 0.102, but J_C_Y[0,0] = 0.068. Both measure spending out of a surprise one-quarter income gain, so why do they differ? Compute each household's quarterly MPC from res.policy_c and res.asset_grid: mpc = diff(c)/((1+r_ss)*diff(a)), repeat the last row, clip to [0,1]. Productivity is s = (0.5, 1.5) (puremacro/models/hank_sequence_space.py:54). Compute three things: the plain mass-weighted average, the average weighted by D*s (each household's share of a wage increase), and the mean MPC within each s. Explain the gap in terms of who receives the income. Explain the remaining ~2% difference too: a forward difference on the grid versus the exact EGM derivative.

*Check:* mpc = np.diff(res.policy_c, axis=0) / ((1 + res.r_ss) * np.diff(res.asset_grid))[:, None]
mpc = np.clip(np.vstack([mpc, mpc[-1:]]), 0, 1)
mpc_plain = (res.distribution * mpc).sum()
mpc_earn = (res.distribution * mpc * s_grid).sum() / (res.distribution * s_grid).sum()
assert abs(mpc_plain - res.steady_state_mpc) < 1e-10
assert abs(mpc_earn - Jy[0,0]) / Jy[0,0] < 0.05
assert abs(mpc_plain - Jy[0,0]) > 0.03
RAN it in the same scratch notebook. It printed plain 0.1022 (library 0.1022), earnings-weighted 0.0665, J00 0.0679, MPC by s [0.1736, 0.0309]. All asserts passed.

## 32_climate_macro_dice

**[basic] Read the model you run: damages and net output** (revise)

Before you trust a simulator, check that it computes the equations you think it does. Use `res_base.trajectories` and `a2 = res_base.parameters['damage_coef']`. (a) Verify that `damage_fraction` equals a2*T^2, where T = `temperature_anomaly`. (b) Verify that `output_net` equals (1 - damage_fraction - abatement_fraction) * output_gross, which means damages and abatement spending are subtracted additively. (c) Show on paper that the multiplicative form (1-Omega)(1-Lambda)Y_gross exceeds the additive form by exactly Omega*Lambda*Y_gross. Confirm this numerically, and report the largest cross term Omega*Lambda in both `res_base` and `res_policy`. Why does the choice between the two forms not matter at DICE-sized damage and abatement shares?

*Check:* I ran this. I appended the solution to a scratch copy of the notebook and executed it with jupytext; the whole notebook took 7.5 s. The checks are: `assert np.allclose(D, a2*T**2, rtol=0, atol=1e-12)`, `assert np.allclose(df['output_net'], (1-D-Lam)*Yg, rtol=1e-12, atol=0)`, `assert np.allclose((1-D)*(1-Lam)*Yg - df['output_net'], D*Lam*Yg, rtol=1e-8, atol=1e-12)`, and, for both runs, `assert (D*Lam).max() < 1e-3`. Output: largest cross term 3.168e-4 in base and 3.361e-4 in policy; EX1 OK. The largest damage error was 6.9e-18 and the net-output error was 0.0 exactly.

**[intermediate] Is $40 a Pigouvian tax? The tax = SCC fixed point** (revise)

A Pigouvian tax equals the social cost of carbon. In this simulator, though, the SCC is computed along the path that the tax itself induces. So with tax growth held at 2%/yr, the Pigouvian starting tax solves tau0 = SCC_2020(tau0). Write `scc0(tau0, g=0.02)`, which returns `simulate_dice_model(n_periods=30, time_step_years=5, start_year=2020, carbon_tax_initial=tau0, carbon_tax_growth=g).scc_initial`. Starting from tau = 40, iterate tau <- scc0(tau) until the change is below 1e-6. Also evaluate SCC_2020 at tau0 = 0, 40 and 80. Then explain three things: (i) why the SCC falls as the tax rises; (ii) why the iteration converges in a few steps (estimate dSCC_2020/dtau0); (iii) whether the baseline $40 over- or under-prices carbon in 2020, and by roughly how much.

*Check:* I ran this in the same scratch notebook run. The checks are `assert abs(scc0(tau_star) - tau_star) < 1e-4`, `assert tau_star < 40.0` and `assert scc0(0.0) > scc0(40.0) > scc0(80.0)`. The iterates printed were 40 -> 34.705441 -> 34.786633 -> 34.785411 -> 34.785430 -> 34.785429 (converged at k=4). SCC_2020 at tau0 = 0, 40, 80 was 35.2247, 34.7054, 34.0299, and tau* = 34.7854. The finite-difference slope dSCC/dtau0 was about -0.0151 (scratch probe2.py). EX2 OK.
