## 35_empirical_benchmark_replications

**[basic] Galí's specification on Galí's sample: differences vs levels** (revise)

Galí (1999) estimates his bivariate VAR on US data for 1948:1-1994:4 with hours in first differences, because ADF tests could not reject a unit root in the level of hours. The cell above does something different: it feeds hours in LEVELS over the whole bundled sample (1948Q2-2026Q1). (1) Keep df_gali up to '1994Q4', build dhours = hours.diff(), and run bq(..., p=4, horizon=20) twice: once on [dlprod, dhours] (Galí's specification) and once on [dlprod, hours] (hours in levels). (2) Careful with units: bq cumulates the IRF of every variable. For dhours the cumulated response is the response of the level of hours, which is what you want. For hours already in levels you must undo the cumulation with np.diff(irf, prepend=0.0) before you read any horizon h>0. The impact response (h=0) is the same either way. (3) Compare the impact response of hours to a positive technology shock, with its 90% band, across the two specifications. Which one lets you reject 'hours do not fall on impact'? Check that the shock you identified really raises productivity in the long run.

*Check:* g = df_gali.copy(); g['dhours'] = g['hours'].diff(); g94 = g.loc[:'1994Q4']
r_d = bq(g94[['dlprod','dhours']].dropna().to_numpy(float), p=4, horizon=20)
r_l = bq(g94[['dlprod','hours']].to_numpy(float), p=4, horizon=20)
lvl = np.diff(r_l.irf_point[:,1,0], prepend=0.0)
assert r_d.irf_point[-1,0,0] > 0
assert r_d.irf_point[0,1,0] < 0 and r_d.irf_upper[0,1,0] < 0
assert r_l.irf_lower[0,1,0] < 0 < r_l.irf_upper[0,1,0]
assert np.isclose(lvl[0], r_l.irf_point[0,1,0])
I ran this with the rule-4 jupytext command on a modified copy (scratchpad/wf/ex35-gali/nb35_ex.py). The whole notebook ran in about 11 s and every assert passed. Printed: differences 1948-94 impact -0.215 [-0.393,-0.007]; levels 1948-94 impact -0.161 [-0.493,+0.204]; levels hours after de-cumulation, h=0..6: -0.161 -0.172 -0.062 +0.067 +0.152 +0.199 +0.214; long-run productivity +0.769. With seeds 0-4 the upper band in differences ranges from -0.049 to -0.004, so it is always below zero but close to it.

**[intermediate] Does the sample choice decide the sign?** (revise)

Estimate both specifications from the previous exercise (hours in differences and hours in levels, p=4) on three samples: data ending 1994Q4 (Galí's), ending 2019Q4 (before the pandemic), and the full bundled sample (the notebook's default). Put the impact response of hours and its 90% band in a 3x2 table. (a) In which specification does the sign of the impact response survive every sample? In which does it flip? (b) The full-sample estimate in differences is much larger in magnitude than the other two. Find the quarters that drive it (plot dhours after 2019) and explain, using the reduced-form residuals, why one extreme episode can dominate a VAR estimate. (c) Read the impact response of hours from the notebook's own bq_res. Is it consistent with the notebook's text claiming that hours 'plunge by -0.4% on impact'?

*Check:* imp = {}
for end in ['1994Q4','2019Q4',None]:
    s = g.loc[:end] if end else g
    for spec,col in [('diff','dhours'),('level','hours')]:
        r = bq(s[['dlprod',col]].dropna().to_numpy(float), p=4, horizon=20)
        imp[(end,spec)] = (r.irf_point[0,1,0], r.irf_lower[0,1,0], r.irf_upper[0,1,0])
E = ['1994Q4','2019Q4',None]
assert all(imp[(e,'diff')][0] < 0 and imp[(e,'diff')][2] < 0 for e in E)
assert all(imp[(e,'level')][1] < 0 < imp[(e,'level')][2] for e in E)
assert imp[('1994Q4','level')][0] < 0 < imp[(None,'level')][0]
assert imp[(None,'diff')][0] < 3*imp[('1994Q4','diff')][0]
assert abs(imp[('2019Q4','diff')][0]) < 0.5*abs(imp[(None,'diff')][0])
I ran this in the same jupytext run and every assert passed. Printed impact [lo,hi]: differences -0.215 [-0.393,-0.007] / -0.276 [-0.397,-0.121] / -0.834 [-1.335,-0.262]; levels -0.161 [-0.493,+0.204] / +0.300 [-0.126,+0.456] / +0.209 [-0.629,+0.625]. The notebook's default bq_res gives an impact of +0.209, not -0.4.

**[stretch] Low-frequency hours: what the levels VAR is really fitting** (revise)

Christiano, Eichenbaum and Vigfusson (2003) find that hours RISE after a technology shock. They argue that the disagreement with Galí comes from how the low-frequency component of hours is modelled. (1) Plot df_gali['hours'] and describe its slow, decade-long swings. A levels VAR with p=4 has to treat these swings as stationary dynamics; differencing removes them. (2) Replace hours in levels with the HP cycle of hours, puremacro.cycles.hp_filter(hours, lamb=1600.0).cycle, computed separately within each sample. Re-estimate the BQ model on the three samples from the previous exercise. Does removing the low-frequency component restore Galí's sign and significance in every sample? (3) Optional: remove only a linear trend (np.polyfit, degree 1) and explain why the result differs from the HP version. (4) Critique the approach: the HP filter is two-sided, so the filtered series uses future data. What does that do to the timing assumptions behind a VAR?

*Check:* from puremacro.cycles import hp_filter
hp_imp = {}
for end in ['1994Q4','2019Q4',None]:
    s = (g.loc[:end] if end else g).copy()
    s['h_hp'] = np.asarray(hp_filter(s['hours'].to_numpy(), lamb=1600.0).cycle)
    r = bq(s[['dlprod','h_hp']].to_numpy(float), p=4, horizon=20)
    hp_imp[end] = (r.irf_point[0,1,0], r.irf_lower[0,1,0], r.irf_upper[0,1,0])
assert all(v[0] < 0 and v[2] < 0 for v in hp_imp.values())
I ran this in the same jupytext run and the assert passed. Printed: HP cycle 1994Q4 -0.306 [-0.453,-0.133]; 2019Q4 -0.194 [-0.349,-0.035]; full -0.532 [-1.105,-0.041]. With seeds 0-4 the upper bands stay below zero (2019Q4: -0.050 to -0.035; full: -0.069 to -0.034). Linear-detrend contrast (probe script, not asserted): 1994Q4 -0.221 [-0.523,+0.149]; full +0.037 [-0.774,+0.464].

**[basic] Is the narrative shock a shock?** (keep)

`narrative_shock` is Banxico's decision direction (+1 hike, 0 hold, -1 cut). It is not a measured surprise. Then use `from puremacro.regress import ols, add_constant` to regress `narrative_shock` on a constant and the four lags. Test that all four lag coefficients are zero with `m.f_test('inf_L1 = 0, inf_L2 = 0, rate_L1 = 0, rate_L2 = 0')`. Is the indicator predictable? rate_L1 comes out positive and rate_L2 negative, with about the same size. What does that say about how the 3-month interbank rate moves in the month before a hike?

The simulation cell in section 2 builds a pure-noise first release, y0 = y* + v, where y* ~ N(2.2, 1.5^2) (line 97) and v ~ N(0, 0.6^2) (line 100). v is independent of y*, and later vintages remove it at the rate e^{-lag/3}. You can ignore the small amount of noise left in the latest vintage, at most e^{-3} ≈ 5% for the last quarter.

## 39_multilingual_narrative_harvesting

**[basic] Negation stress test: predict, then score** (revise)

Section 2 used `infer_policy_stance` to sign each excerpt. It checks which lexicon phrases appear in the text and nothing else. Before you run anything, predict the sign it will give each sentence below. The hand labels are +1 expansionary, -1 contractionary and 0 no policy change. Cancelling planned spending counts as a cut, so the Parliament sentence gets -1; a label of 0 would also be defensible and would not change your count.

```python
tests = [
    ("The government announced a fiscal stimulus package.", +1),
    ("Parliament rejected the fiscal stimulus and cancelled the planned infrastructure investment and cash transfers.", -1),
    ("The ministry has no plans for fiscal stimulus.", 0),
    ("The Federal Reserve decided not to hike rates.", 0),
]
```
Score each sentence with `infer_policy_stance(text, language="en")` and count how many signs are wrong. What confidence does the scorer report for the Parliament sentence? The confidence rule is min(0.95, 0.5 + 0.15 x the difference in matched phrases). What does that confidence actually measure? And why do narrative shock series in the Romer & Romer tradition read what was enacted instead of counting words?

*Check:* wrong = sum(infer_policy_stance(t, language="en")[0] != l for t, l in tests); assert wrong == 3; assert infer_policy_stance(tests[1][0], language="en")[1] == 0.95. I ran this on a copy of 39_multilingual_narrative_harvesting.py with the jupytext command, and the whole notebook took about 2.6 s. Output: +1 conf=0.80 for the correct sentence ['fiscal stimulus','stimulus package']; +1 conf=0.95 for Parliament ['fiscal stimulus','infrastructure investment','cash transfers']; +1 conf=0.65 for 'no plans' ['fiscal stimulus']; -1 conf=0.65 for 'not to hike' ['hike rates']; wrong: 3. Both asserts passed.

**[intermediate] A 3-token negation window, and what it cannot fix** (revise)

Use the `tests` list from the previous exercise and repair the scorer's negation blindness.

1. Write `negated(text, term, lang="en", window=3)`. It lowercases `text` and finds the first occurrence of `term`. It tokenises everything before that point with `re.findall(r"\w+", ...)`. It returns True if any of the last `window` tokens is in NEG[lang], where NEG = {"en": {"not","no","never","without","nor"}, "es": {"no","sin","nunca","ni"}}.
2. Write `stance_neg(text, lang="en", window=3)`. It calls `infer_policy_stance` and drops every negated phrase from `details["matched_expansion"]` and `details["matched_contraction"]`. It returns `int(np.sign(n_exp - n_con))`.
3. Count the wrong signs for window = 1, 2, 3 and 4. What is the smallest window that fixes both syntactic negations? Which sentence is still wrong at every window? Why can no token window fix a verb like 'rejected' or 'cancelled'?

The library's uncertainty indices already suppress a hit that follows a negation marker, using a 4-token window (`_NEG_WINDOW` in puremacro/narrative/indices/_kernels.py). The stance scorer does not.

*Check:* assert [sum(stance_neg(t, "en", w) != l for t, l in tests) for w in (1, 2, 3, 4)] == [3, 2, 1, 1]; still_wrong = [t for t, l in tests if stance_neg(t, "en", 3) != l]; assert len(still_wrong) == 1 and "rejected" in still_wrong[0]. I ran this in the same executed scratch copy. Signs by window: w=1 [1,1,1,-1] with 3 wrong; w=2 [1,1,1,0] with 2 wrong; w=3 [1,1,0,0] with 1 wrong; w=4 [1,1,0,0] with 1 wrong. The only sentence left wrong is the Parliament 'rejected ... cancelled' one. The asserts passed.

**[intermediate] Which identity residuals can a re-reference move?** (revise)

Prompt 2, made checkable. Build ident15 = qna_identity(qna_rebase(panel, 2015)) and compare it with ident.
(a) Assert that every column not starting with 'real_' is identical, NaNs included. That covers nominal_*, output_*, income_*, crossflow_* and n_obs.
(b) Assert that each of real_mean, real_absmax and real_last moves by more than 0.1 pp for at least one country.
(c) Now explain (b). On the rebased panel, compute the chain-linking gap: the real expenditure gap minus the nominal one, both as % of GDP. Use IDENTITY_TERMS from puremacro.fetch. Assert that its mean over the 2015 quarters is below 0.05 pp for every country.
Where is the chain-linking gap anchored? What does that tell you about how much real_last depends on the choice of reference year rather than on the data?

*Check:* from puremacro.fetch import IDENTITY_TERMS
ident15 = qna_identity(qna_rebase(panel, 2015))
nom = [c for c in ident.columns if not c.startswith('real_')]
pd.testing.assert_frame_equal(ident15[nom], ident[nom])
real = ['real_mean', 'real_absmax', 'real_last']
assert ((ident15[real] - ident[real]).abs().max() > 0.1).all()
reb = qna_rebase(panel, 2015)
def gap(p, s):
    tot = sum(sg * p[f'{n}{s}'] for n, sg in IDENTITY_TERMS.items())
    return 100 * (p[f'gdp{s}'] - tot) / p[f'gdp{s}']
chain = gap(reb, '_real') - gap(reb, '')
in15 = chain.index.get_level_values('date').year == 2015
assert (chain[in15].groupby(level='code').mean().abs() < 0.05).all()
RAN (same scratch-copy execution, all asserts passed):
- Current-price columns: identical.
- Real columns moved by at most: real_mean 0.783, real_absmax 0.364, real_last 1.107 (MEX real_last went from 3.895 to 2.788).
- 2015 anchor means: 0.0006 to 0.0024 pp (largest DEU). Over the full sample the gap reaches 0.277 (ITA) to 5.345 (MEX) pp.
- A separate probe found the same anchoring for base years 2010 and 2020 (largest 0.028 pp, MEX 2020).

(a) Assert that the annualised gdp in contrib is the compounded quarterly rate, 100*((1+q/100)**4 - 1). For example, USA 2020Q2 goes from -7.877 q/q to -27.976 annualised.

(c) Annualise each quarterly contribution on its own in the same way, and sum the results. Assert that this sum misses the annualised GDP by more than 0.5 pp in USA 2020Q2. Then compare the median miss by country.
Explain three things: why compounding is not additive, how qna_contributions keeps the parts additive, and which convention (quarterly or annualised) you would use for the 2020 bars in a paper.

*Check:* qtr = qna_contributions(panel, annualise=False)

full = contrib[parts].notna().all(axis=1)
assert (contrib.loc[full, parts].sum(axis=1) - contrib.loc[full, 'gdp']).abs().max() < 1e-10

RAN (same scratch-copy execution, all asserts passed):

- The separately annualised parts sum to -28.596, a miss of -0.620 pp.
- Median miss by country: 0.012 (USA) to 0.140 (MEX) pp. Largest miss: 0.767 (USA) to 5.35 (MEX) pp, in the 2020 quarters.

## 41_dynare_frontier_showcase

**[basic] Where does the ZLB start to bind?** (revise)

The small NK block (notebooks/41_dynare_frontier_showcase.py:153-185) has one state variable, the demand shock g_t, with E_t g_{t+1} = rho_g g_t. Guess y_t = psi_y g_t and pi_t = psi_pi g_t and solve for the coefficients by undetermined coefficients. Write your closed forms in Python using params_nk (do not type in numbers), set psi_r = phi_pi*psi_pi + phi_y*psi_y, and compute g_star = -r_ss/psi_r, the smallest one-time demand shock that pushes the unconstrained policy rate below the floor on impact. Then confirm with OccBin that a shock 3% smaller than g_star never hits the ZLB, and one 3% larger binds for exactly one quarter. Why is it exactly one quarter?

*Check:* psi_y, psi_pi = ..., ...  # your closed forms
psi_r = params_nk['phi_pi']*psi_pi + params_nk['phi_y']*psi_y
np.testing.assert_allclose(ref_mod.decision_rules().ghu.loc[['y','pi','r'],'eps_g'].to_numpy(), [psi_y, psi_pi, psi_r], atol=1e-8)
g_star = -params_nk['r_ss']/psi_r
occ_at = lambda g0: solve_occbin(ref_mod, cons_mod, constraint, shock_sequence=np.array([0.0, g0]), horizon=40)
assert occ_at(0.97*g_star).binding_periods == 0
assert occ_at(1.03*g_star).binding_periods == 1

The OccBin sweep matches: 0 binding quarters at -0.0075 and -0.0078, 1 at -0.00782, -0.0079 and -0.0080.

At g0 = -0.020 the ZLB makes the output trough much deeper than in the unconstrained model, yet the episode lasts exactly as long as the unconstrained rate predicts. (a) Show that from the exit quarter onward, the OccBin path of y coincides with the unconstrained rule psi_y*g_t, while inside the spell y is strictly lower. Copy nk_ref into nk_ref_smooth with interest-rate smoothing, r_t = rho_i*r_{t-1} + (1-rho_i)*(phi_pi*pi_t + phi_y*y_t) + eps_r with rho_i = 0.5. y_unc = psi_y*p['g'].to_numpy()

assert (p['y'].to_numpy()[:T] < y_unc[:T]).all()

## 43_dsge_nuts_and_analytic_gradients

**[basic] Is the analytic score wrong, or is the finite-difference check wrong?** (revise)

It then builds ssm and sens for the rebuilt model with make_state_space_from_varobs and build_state_space_sensitivities, and returns the first output of kalman_score(Y, ssm, sens), where Y = data.to_numpy(). Take central differences with h = 1e-6 and compare them with score_exact. (b) Compare ll_exact with kalman_filter(Y, ssm)['loglik']. Read both docstrings: which initial state covariance P0 does each routine use? Find the P0 that makes kalman_filter reproduce ll_exact. Explain why verify_numerical_gradient reports WARN even though the analytic score is correct, and say which initialisation suits a stationary DSGE model.

Log-likelihoods: kalman_score -275.280824; kalman_filter with its default P0 -288.449192; kalman_filter with P0 = scipy.linalg.solve_discrete_lyapunov(ssm.T, ssm.R @ ssm.Q @ ssm.R.T) -275.280824. The notebook's own 5-point FD (ssm_evaluator, step 1e-5) with that P0 gives rel 6.5e-11 and 3.2e-10, both PASS. All asserts passed.

**[intermediate] Race NUTS against random-walk Metropolis in two dimensions** (revise)

Predict before running: in this two-parameter model, which sampler gives more effective draws per draw, and which gives more per second? Then re-estimate the same data with random-walk Metropolis: res_rw = model.estimate(data, method='kalman', n_draws=n_draws, n_chains=n_chains, burn_in=burn_in, seed=seed_nuts). **[stretch] How far does a badly centred prior drag the posterior?** (revise)

sigma is the inverse IES: in the IS curve, output responds to the real rate with coefficient 1/sigma. The data were generated with sigma = 1. Suppose a researcher uses a prior centred at 2 with s.d. 0.2, five prior s.d. away from the truth. Rebuild the model with load_mod(NK_3EQ_MOD.replace('sigma, gamma_pdf, 1.0, 0.20;', 'sigma, gamma_pdf, 2.0, 0.20;')). Estimate it on the same data by random-walk Metropolis with .estimate(data, method='kalman', n_draws=2000, n_chains=2, seed=7), keeping the default burn_in; this takes about a minute. Check convergence with compute_split_rhat. Report three things: the posterior mean of sigma, what fraction of the distance from the baseline posterior mean to the prior mean it moved, and the shift measured in baseline posterior s.d. Do 120 quarters identify sigma well? Would you trust inference under this prior?

*Check:* m_tight = float(res_tight.draws[:, :, 0].mean()); m_base = float(res.summary().loc['sigma', 'mean']); assert max(compute_split_rhat(res_tight.draws[:, :, i]) for i in range(2)) < 1.05; assert m_base < m_tight < 1.25. I ran this on a modified copy with the jupytext command (52.7 s for this cell). It printed m_tight = 1.1100 (mode 1.112), m_base = 0.9348, kappa mean 0.0961, split R-hat 1.004/1.002, bulk ESS 541/506. The shift of 0.175 is 16% of the way to the prior mean, about 3.2 baseline posterior s.d. (0.055). Asserts passed.

Predict first: the linear SSJ path is a first-order approximation. If the rate cut is 4 times larger, by what factor should the gap to the nonlinear Broyden path grow? Use the Section 4 settings (rho=0.5, horizon=30). For cuts of -10, -25, -50 and -100 bp, compute max_t |Y_nonlinear - Y_linear| in bp, then the ratio of the -100 bp gap to the -25 bp gap. It printed max gaps of 0.0114, 0.0520, 0.1921 and 0.7929 bp at -10, -25, -50 and -100 bp, and a ratio of 15.23. The t=0 gap was +0.793 bp for the cut and +0.670 bp for the hike. The ratios between neighbouring shocks were -25/-10 = 4.56 (quadratic predicts 6.25) and -50/-25 = 3.69 (predicts 4). So the smallest shock sits near the Broyden floor of tol=1e-6 on ||H||_inf, which is 0.01 bp.

**[stretch] The budget constraint as a Jacobian diagnostic** (revise)

Each household's budget constraint means that a one-unit rise in aggregate income at date 0 must, in present value, all be consumed eventually: sum_t (1+r_ss)^(-t) J_C_Y[t,0] = 1. Write pv_col0(m, T), which computes this sum from m.compute_jacobians(T=T)["J_C_Y"]. Evaluate it for T = 30, 200 and 400. Explain why the T=30 value falls well short of 1. Then ask whether the shortfall at T=200 disappears at T=400. If it does not, it is not truncation: find where income leaks out. Inspect model.asset_distribution[-1], the mass at the top of the asset grid, and think about what happens to a rich, high-income household that wants to save past a_max. Rebuild the model with a wider grid via load_hank_mod(mod_text.replace("a_max = 30.0", "a_max = 60.0")) and recompute. Relate what you find to the steady-state Y printed in Section 1.

*Check:* def pv_col0(m, T): J = m.compute_jacobians(T=T)["J_C_Y"]; q = (1 + m.r_ss)**(-np.arange(T)); return float(q @ J[:, 0])

assert 0.97 < pv_col0(model, 200) < 0.995
assert abs(pv_col0(model, 400) - pv_col0(model, 200)) < 0.005   # plateau, so this is not truncation
wide = load_hank_mod(mod_text.replace("a_max = 30.0", "a_max = 60.0"))
assert abs(pv_col0(wide, 400) - 1.0) < 1e-3   # wider grid closes the leak
I RAN this in the same scratch-copy execution and all asserts passed. PV was 0.6728 at T=30, 0.9884 at T=200 and 0.9895 at T=400. Mass at a_max was 0.0058. The high-income household's a'(a_max) is 30.06 > 30, so the grid clips its saving. With a_max=60 the PV at T=400 is 1.00000 and Y_ss is 1.000000, against 0.9998 at a_max=30. In a separate scratch script, a_max=30 with n_a=100 gave 0.9992, and a_max=60 with n_a=50 gave 0.99995 at T=300. Each Jacobian computation takes under 0.1 s.

**[intermediate] Does the Taylor principle matter here? Anchored vs accelerationist Phillips curve** (revise)

The Phillips curve inside `simulate_nk_counterfactual` hard-codes `rho_pi = 0.5` (line 221). Its right-hand side, `(1 - rho_pi) * pi_star + rho_pi * pi_prev`, pulls inflation back toward 2% every quarter, whatever the central bank does. (1) Add `rho_pi: float = 0.5` to the function's signature and remove `rho_pi` from the hard-coded line, keeping `kappa = 0.25`. With this default every earlier result in the notebook stays the same. (2) Before you run anything, predict inflation in quarter 40 for `phi_pi` in {0.8, 0.9, 1.5, 2.0}, using `phi_y=0.25, rho_i=0.7, pi_0=6.0, T=40`. (3) Run those four cases twice: once with `rho_pi=0.5` and once with `rho_pi=1.0`, the accelerationist Phillips curve. (4) Write the model as x_t = A x_{t-1} + c, where x = (y, pi, i), A = M^{-1} diag(rho_y, rho_pi, rho_i) and M is the matrix built in the function. Compute the spectral radius of A as a function of `phi_pi` for both values of `rho_pi`. Then explain three things. Why does `phi_pi < 1` still bring inflation back to 2% when `rho_pi=0.5`? Why does `phi_pi > 1`, a rise in the real rate, decide stability once `rho_pi=1`? What happens at exactly `phi_pi = 1`? Hint: compute det(B - M). The method section appeals to determinacy under rational expectations, but this simulation contains no expectations at all, since every right-hand-side term is lagged. What does the Taylor principle mean in this model?

## 49_applied_macroprudential_gar_and_stress

**[intermediate] Is the fitted skewness real? Check convergence before reading alpha** (revise)

The Read-the-output cell says the skew-t shape alpha 'plunges' under stress. Test that claim. For fci in [0.0, 1.0, 2.5], build q = predict_quantiles(fci) and compute a model-free quantile skewness, (q[0.95] + q[0.05] - 2*q[0.50]) / (q[0.95] - q[0.05]). Fit the skew-t twice: once with fit_skewt_to_quantiles(q), which uses the default maxiter=200 as the notebook does, and once with fit_skewt_to_quantiles(q, maxiter=2000). For each fit, record .success, .alpha, .nu and the 5% GaR .ppf(0.05). Also record the fit error SSE = sum over fit.target_quantiles of (fit.ppf(tau) - q_tau)^2. Before you run it, predict how alpha should move with FCI if tighter conditions stretch the left tail. Then answer four questions. (i) Why is the default alpha sequence not monotone, and what does .success tell you? (ii) Which fits should the statement 'alpha falls under stress' rest on, and do they agree with the model-free skewness? (iii) The degrees of freedom nu reach the thousands or more. What does that say about identifying tail thickness from seven quantiles (hint: the skew-normal limit)? (iv) Use compute_stress_loss on the converged GaR at FCI = 2.5. How much does the stress-scenario CET1 depletion change? The rule to take away: never interpret estimated parameters before you check the optimizer's convergence flag. The refit takes about 50 s.

*Check:* fci_grid=[0.0,1.0,2.5]; qskew=[]; f200=[]; f2000=[]
for f in fci_grid:
    q=predict_quantiles(f); qskew.append((q[0.95]+q[0.05]-2*q[0.50])/(q[0.95]-q[0.05]))
    f200.append(fit_skewt_to_quantiles(q)); f2000.append(fit_skewt_to_quantiles(q, maxiter=2000))
sse=lambda ft: sum((ft.ppf(t)-v)**2 for t,v in ft.target_quantiles.items())
a=np.array([ft.alpha for ft in f2000]); g=np.array([ft.ppf(0.05) for ft in f2000])
assert all(ft.success for ft in f2000)
assert np.all(np.diff(qskew)<0) and np.all(np.diff(a)<0) and np.all(np.diff(g)<0)
assert all(sse(n)<=sse(o)+1e-9 for o,n in zip(f200,f2000))

- FCI 1.0: qskew -0.173. maxiter=200: ok=False, alpha -1.68, nu 9.78e3, GaR -3.88, SSE 0.291. maxiter=2000: ok=True, alpha -2.51, nu 1.46e4, GaR -3.91, SSE 0.036.
- FCI 2.5: qskew -0.245, raw QAR q05 -6.17. maxiter=200: ok=False, alpha -0.68, nu 3.13e4, GaR -5.48, SSE 4.274. maxiter=2000: ok=True, alpha -4.38, nu 2.23e5, GaR -6.57, SSE 0.871.
- Stress CET1: 3.49 pp in the notebook, 4.03 pp with the converged fit.
Why the SSE assert holds: Nelder-Mead is deterministic, so the 2000-iteration run follows the same path as the 200-iteration run and its best vertex can only improve.

## 50_applied_fiscal_multipliers_and_debt_sustainability

**[stretch] When is fiscal consolidation self-defeating?** (revise)

The calibrated DSA holds growth fixed, so a higher primary balance always lowers debt (the Your-turn assert at 50:280-284). Add the missing growth channel. (a) Write `simulate_dsa_growth(d0=0.65, horizon_quarters=20, n_sims=1000, seed=42, pb_shift=0.0, m=0.0, shocks=True)`. Make it a copy of `simulate_stochastic_dsa` (50:142-168) with two changes. First, the debt equation uses `g_eff = g_t - m*pb_shift` in quarters t=1..4 and `g_t` afterwards. Annual growth is then m*pb_shift points lower for one year, so the GDP level ends m*pb_shift % lower, which makes m a level multiplier. Second, the shock draw is multiplied by 0 when `shocks=False`. The growth hit enters only the debt equation, not the VAR state, so there is no automatic-stabilizer feedback. Predict first: relative to the no-consolidation path with the same d0, the year-1 debt-ratio change is roughly (m*d0 - 1)*pb_shift percentage points. The primary surplus cuts the numerator; the smaller GDP raises the ratio by about m*pb_shift*d0. (b) With `n_sims=1, shocks=False`, compute dd(d0, m, q) = 100*(path with pb_shift=1 minus path with pb_shift=0) at quarter q. Find the break-even multiplier m* at d0=0.65 and d0=1.0 and compare it with 1/d0. (c) Compare dd at q=8 and q=20 with dd at q=4. Is self-defeat permanent when the growth hit lasts only one year? What kind of growth hit would make it permanent? (d) Plug in m_hat = -m_bp[4], -m_rr[4] and -m_iv[4]. Which point estimates imply self-defeating consolidation at d0=1.0, and which at d0=0.65? Then check whether the RR 90% interval (`lp_rr.iloc[4][['lo','hi']]`) or the LP-IV Anderson-Rubin set (`res_iv.iloc[4][['ar_lo','ar_hi','ar_set_type']]`, times SCALE, sign flipped) can rule out m = 1/d0. Finally, print `irfs_bp[:5,0,0]*c_bp/SCALE`, the tax change behind m_bp[4] in % of GDP. Given that path, does m(4) under- or overstate the output cost of a sustained 1 pp consolidation?

*Check:* Asserts (no answer key):
```
from scipy.optimize import brentq
assert np.allclose(simulate_dsa_growth(d0=0.65, pb_shift=1.0, m=0.0), simulate_stochastic_dsa(d0=0.65, pb_shift=1.0))
dd = lambda d0, m, q=4: 100*(simulate_dsa_growth(d0=d0, n_sims=1, pb_shift=1.0, m=m, shocks=False)[0, q] - simulate_dsa_growth(d0=d0, n_sims=1, pb_shift=0.0, shocks=False)[0, q])
for d0 in (0.65, 1.0):
    assert abs(brentq(lambda mm: dd(d0, mm), 0.1, 5.0) - 1/d0) < 0.1
    assert dd(d0, 0.9/d0) < 0 < dd(d0, 1.1/d0)
    assert max(abs(dd(d0, m) - (m*d0 - 1)) for m in (0, 0.5, 1, 1.5, 2)) < 0.02
    assert dd(d0, 2.0, q=20) < 0
    for est in (m_bp[4], m_rr[4], m_iv[4]):
        if abs(-est - 1/d0) > 0.1: assert (dd(d0, -est) > 0) == (-est > 1/d0)
```

- The RR 90% interval for m at h=4 is [-0.18, 2.42]. The IV Anderson-Rubin set is two unbounded rays, m >= 0.72 or m <= -16.3. Both contain 1/d0 at both debt levels.
- BP tax path in % of GDP, h=0..4: 1.00, 0.62, 0.71, 0.56, 0.46.

The proposal's numbers (chk50.py) match exactly.

## 51_continuous_projection_collocation_and_fem

**[intermediate] Measure the convergence orders yourself, then find what stalls the spectral error** (revise)

The notebook says FEM converges at O(h^2) and Chebyshev collocation converges spectrally (O(c^-N)). Check both claims with numbers. (a) FEM: re-solve the smooth Brock-Mirman model with the Experiment 1 FEMProblem settings for E in {20, 40, 80, 160}. For each E, compute the max relative policy error against g*(k)=alpha*beta*k^alpha on eval_k. Estimate the algebraic order p as minus the slope of log(err) on log(E), and also compute the pairwise orders log2(err_E / err_2E). One pairwise order will look wrong. Recompute the errors on np.linspace(*domain, 20001) and explain the gap. Hint: where in the mesh is the FEM error largest? (b) Chebyshev: for N in {4, 6, 8, 10, 12}, fit log10(err) on N. How many decades of accuracy does each extra degree buy? Why is a semilog plot, not a log-log plot, the right test for spectral convergence? (c) Solve N=16 with the default options. The error stops falling and stays near its N=12 level. Is this floor a limit of the Chebyshev basis (round-off or conditioning), or is it the nonlinear solver's stopping rule? Test this by re-solving with CollocationProblem(..., options={"tol": 1e-12}). Then explain what the default tol controls in puremacro/vfi/collocation.py.

*Check:* I ran this. I appended an assertion cell to a scratch copy of notebooks/51_continuous_projection_collocation_and_fem.py and executed it with the rule-4 jupytext command. The whole notebook took about 7 s. It printed VET51CC_ALL_ASSERTS_PASSED. The assertions and their printed values: `assert 1.7 < p_fem < 2.3`: fit slope on eval_k = 1.953 (errors 1.755e-4 / 4.504e-5 / 1.049e-5 / 3.126e-6). `assert all(1.8 < o < 2.2 for o in pairwise_orders_on_20001_grid)`: 1.954 / 1.907 / 1.950. On eval_k the pairwise orders are 1.962 / 2.102 / 1.747. `assert 0.5 < decades_per_degree < 0.8`: 0.643 (errors 2.195e-4, 9.462e-6, 4.646e-7, 2.659e-8, 1.539e-9). `assert err16_default > 1e-10 and err16_default > 0.5*err12`: 1.651e-9 against 1.539e-9. `assert err16_tol1e12 < 1e-10`: 5.423e-12, converged True. Expected explanations: (a) The max FEM error sits at the mesh nodes; on a 20001-point grid the nodal error equals the grid max. The 1000-point eval_k grid misses the worst node at E=80 (it reports 1.049e-5, the true value is 1.208e-5), which produces the spurious 1.75. (c) The default options tol=1e-8 (collocation.py:853) goes to scipy root(method='hybr', tol=tol) (collocation.py:912). Scipy maps it to xtol, a relative step tolerance on the coefficients (scipy/optimize/_root.py:241-242), so Powell stops at about 1e-8 relative coefficient change and leaves about 1e-9 policy error. Caveats for the key: do not assert sol.converged at tight tolerances for arbitrary N (N=14 with tol=1e-12 returned converged=False with residual 1.97e-12, while tol=1e-11 and tol=1e-13 converged). Use N=16 for the floor: N=20 with the default tol happened to reach 1.16e-12.

Next, change user_persistence to 0.6 and then 0.92. Should the impact response move? Prompt 1 says the rate jump scales 'near-linearly'. Is that the right word?

With sigma_eps=0, each country's transition and Euler equation involve only its own capital (deep_macro.py:512-549). Explain why sol.converged (test MSE < 1e-3 on a trajectory started within ±15% of k_ss, deep_macro.py:965, 1057, 1074) cannot see these errors, and why a deterministic model's 'ergodic set' is a point. ## 57_multiconstraint_occbin_and_dml

**[intermediate] Which constraint amplifies the recession? ZLB vs borrowing cap** (revise)

The introduction (and the `chi` comment, 'financial accelerator') says the borrowing limit compounds the contraction. Test that claim. (1) Before you run anything, rewrite the IS row of `ref_eqs` as y_t = E_t y_{t+1} - (r_t - E_t pi_{t+1})/sigma + g_t - chi*b_t. (c) Run linear 2SLS of net_tfa_iv on p401, with e401 as the instrument and a constant plus all 17 df_controls columns as exogenous regressors. Is the 1.3k miss regularization bias, confounding leaking through U, or an unlucky draw?

## 62_flexible_trade_cge

**[basic] Hicksian own-wage elasticity of labour demand in the nested CES** (keep)

Reuse experiment 1 (factor_ones, ones, tau_ones, compute_nested_ces_costs, compute_nested_factor_demands with normalized=True). Hold output, rental rates and intermediate prices fixed. Move all wages by +/-h in logs (h = 1e-6, central difference) to get eps = d log L / d log w in every sector and country, for (rho_va, sigma_y) in {(1.4, 0), (1.4, 0.2), (1.4, 1.0), (0.5, 2.0)}. Derive the answer before you compute it. Write L ∝ y·(c_Y/c_VA)^sigma_y·(c_VA/w)^rho and use d log c_VA/d log w = 1 - alpha and d log c_Y/d log w = theta_VA·(1 - alpha). Here alpha = calib.alpha is capital's share of value added, and theta_M = 1 - theta_VA is the intermediate share of the unit cost net of the production tax (build it from calib.a and calib.tax). Show that eps = -[alpha·rho + sigma_y·theta_M·(1 - alpha)]. Explain why the sigma_y = 0 elasticity is capital's share times rho rather than rho itself, and why sigma_y adds a term weighted by the intermediate share.

*Check:* h = 1e-6; theta_M = calib.a.sum(axis=0)[None] / (1 - calib.tax)

    tech = FlexibleTechnologyConfig(rho_va=rho, sigma_y=sy); logl = []
    for sgn in (1, -1):
        w = factor_ones * np.exp(sgn * h)

        logl.append(np.log(l))
    eps = (logl[0] - logl[1]) / (2 * h)
    np.testing.assert_allclose(eps, -(calib.alpha * rho + sy * theta_M * (1 - calib.alpha)), rtol=1e-6)

**[intermediate] Smooth subsistence versus a fixed-floor LES: when is the budget feasible?** (revise)

Experiment 2 scales the subsistence quantity by g(u) = tanh(3u)/tanh(3) (smooth_subsistence_scaling), where u = income/income0. Work at unit prices (P = ones) for country A, with food subsistence mu. (1) Compute A's benchmark household food share s0 from compute_stone_geary_final_demand(income0, ones, calib, FlexiblePreferenceConfig()). Compute the marginal budget share b = compute_les_marginal_budget_shares(calib, pref)[0, 0, 0]. (2) A textbook LES with a fixed floor c_bar = mu·c_food,0 runs out of supernumerary income at u = mu·s0. Show that the smooth rule overspends only where u < mu·s0·g(u). Use g(0) = 0 and concavity (g(u)/u <= g'(0) = 3/tanh 3) to derive the threshold mu* below which the budget holds at every income, and the worst-case overspend above mu*. (3) For mu = 0.9, find the root u_r of u = mu·s0·g(u) with scipy.optimize.brentq. Report the budget gap (demand.sum - budget)/budget just above and just below u_r. Explain where the gap comes from (supernumerary income is clipped at zero, flexible.py:1695) and state the several-sector condition sum_s mu_s·s_s < tanh(3)/3. Discuss why a subsistence level that moves with income leaves this demand without an expenditure function (docs/trade_household.md:140-146).

*Check:* s0 = compute_stone_geary_final_demand(income0, ones, calib, FlexiblePreferenceConfig())[0,0,0] / (calib.theta[0,0,0]*income0[0,0,0])

    d = compute_stone_geary_final_demand(u*income0, ones, calib, FlexiblePreferenceConfig(subsistence_shares={'FOOD': mu}))
    m = (calib.theta[:, :1, :]*u*income0)[0,0,0]
    return d[0,0,0]/m, (d.sum(axis=1)[0,0] - m)/m

mu_star = np.tanh(3)/(3*s0); worst = lambda mu: max(share_gap(mu, x)[1] for x in np.linspace(1e-3, 2, 2001))

**[intermediate] When does the capital-labour elasticity matter in general equilibrium?** (revise)

Experiment 1 shows that rho_va governs K/L at given prices. Does it change the tariff equilibrium? Use solve_flexible_trade_equilibrium(calib, tol=1e-10, rho_va=rho, ...) with and without tau = tau_fd = np.array([.10, 0.]), and assert converged and metadata['flexible_settings_applied']. Two warnings. First, this route (routing 'auto', trade_balance_valuation 'ppfd_legacy') uses the legacy closure with Leontief intermediate sourcing by default, so compare results only within this route, never with section 4's consistent benchmark. Second, compare against rho_va = 1+1e-9 or method='quasi_condensed', not the default call, which uses a different Cobb-Douglas precedence convention. (a) With default sourcing, compare rho in {0.5, 2}. Show that log outputs and log(w/r) do not move and that the whole state change is identical. Explain why: at unchanged w/r, CES and Cobb-Douglas factor demands coincide, so rho matters only if w/r moves, and w/r moves only if the output mix changes. Why can't the mix change here? Look at final_weights (cell lines 80-81): both sectors of each origin country have identical final-use weights, so every final basket is collinear within an origin, and with Leontief intermediates baseline outputs still clear both factor markets. Change A-MANU's row to [.30, .25, .05, .30, .08, .02] and confirm that rho then matters. (b) With default final_weights, switch on sigma_trade=5, then sigma_inter=2, keeping sigma_y = 0. Start from the two factor-clearing conditions, with d log L_s = d log y_s - rho·alpha_s·z and d log K_s = d log y_s + rho·(1 - alpha_s)·z, where z = d log(w/r). Derive z = sum_s (lambda_L,s - lambda_K,s)·d log y_s / (rho·D), with D = sum_s lambda_L,s·alpha_s + sum_s lambda_K,s·(1 - alpha_s) and lambda the baseline factor shares from factor_allocation_frame(). Verify it for rho in {0.5, 1+1e-9, 2} and explain the approximate 1/rho scaling. Contrast this with exercise 1's partial-equilibrium elasticity.

*Check:* (a) for rho in (.5, 2.): assert max|log(ff.y_sol/fb.y_sol)| < 1e-10 and max|dlog(w/r)| < 1e-12; assert max|dx(.5) - dx(2.)| < 1e-9 (dx = ff.x_sol - fb.x_sol); assert max|x(rho_va=1+1e-9) - x(method='quasi_condensed')| < 1e-10 at the 10% tariff.
(b) fa = fb.factor_allocation_frame(); L0, K0 = fa['labor'].to_numpy(), fa['capital'].to_numpy(); alpha = calib.alpha.ravel(order='F'). For each country n with i = slice(2n, 2n+2), lL = L0[i]/L0[i].sum(), lK = K0[i]/K0[i].sum() and pred_n = ((lL - lK)*dly[i]).sum()/(rho*((lL*alpha[i]).sum() + (lK*(1 - alpha[i])).sum())); np.testing.assert_allclose(z, pred, rtol=1e-2) for sigma_trade=5 and sigma_inter=2 at rho in {0.5, 1+1e-9, 2}.
RAN in a modified notebook copy; all asserts pass. The dx difference is 1.3e-11 and the continuity gap 6.2e-14. For sigma_trade=5, z_A = -0.004944 / -0.002686 / -0.001405 against predictions -0.004951 / -0.002690 / -0.001407. For sigma_inter=2, z_A = -0.003406 / -0.001769 / -0.000902 against -0.003409 / -0.001771 / -0.000903. The perturbed-row case was run in a standalone scratch script: z_A = -1.126e-3 (rho 0.5) and -2.82e-4 (rho 2), and the formula matches to 3e-4 relative.

**[stretch] Who pays A's tariff? Incidence, reversal and a singular benchmark** (revise)

Section 4 finds that B's producer prices fall about 9% under A's 10% tariff at sigma = 2. Define foreign incidence as inc = -log(p_B'/p_B)/log(1.1) for B's two sectors (p_sol[0, :, 1]), and read A's terms of trade from terms_of_trade[0]. (a) Wrap section 4's two solves in a function of sigma (ge_options with sigma replaced; assert convergence and the tolerance) and evaluate sigma in {0, 0.5, 2, 5, 10}. Explain sigma = 0: with Leontief sourcing and fixed final baskets nothing substitutes, so the tariff is a transfer rebated lump-sum. Explain why incidence falls as sigma rises above 2, and what inc > 1 means for the tariff-inclusive price of B goods in A (Metzler). Then impose the tariff on intermediate use only (tau) and on final use only (tau_fd), and explain the gap using the fact that final-demand sourcing is fixed-coefficient. (b) At sigma = 0.5, B's prices rise. Find the cause. Build the benchmark Jacobian J(sigma) of compute_equilibrium_residuals(x, calib, tau=..., tau_fd=..., sigma=sigma, accounting='consistent') at base.x_sol by central differences; the benchmark state does not depend on sigma. Locate sigma*, where det J changes sign, with brentq on [0.9, 1.05]. Compute the implicit-function slope d x/dt = -J^{-1} dF/dt. Check it against a t = 1e-3 solve at sigma 0.5 and 2, and evaluate it on both sides of sigma* (0.98 and 1.0). Relate the sign flip to Samuelson's correspondence principle and to a Marshall-Lerner-type heuristic: eps_i ≈ sigma × (intermediate share of country i's imports, computed from Z and F), and d log ToT_A/d log(1+t) ≈ eps_A/(eps_A + eps_B - 1). Discuss why the heuristic's singular sigma differs from sigma*. Do not assert on 10% solves near sigma*: Newton fails there, which is a symptom and not a check.

assert np.all((inc[2.] > .95) & (inc[2.] < 1.05)) and abs(np.log(tot[2.])/np.log(1.1) - 1) < .01
assert np.all(inc[2.] > inc[5.]) and np.all(inc[5.] > inc[10.])
assert np.all(np.abs(inc_final_only) < .05) and np.all(inc_intermediate_only > .9)

assert np.all(np.sign(slope(.98)[2:]) != np.sign(slope(1.)[2:]))
for s in (.5, 2.): np.testing.assert_allclose(np.log(c.p_sol/b.p_sol).ravel(order='F')[2:]/1e-3, slope(s)[2:], rtol=2e-3)  # b, c = t=1e-3 solve pair at sigma s

The linear slope at sigma 2 is [-0.9498, -1.0026] against the t = 1e-3 finite difference [-0.9495, -1.0023]. ## 64_singularities_and_keller_pac

**[basic] Did PAC really turn the fold? An honest fold test** (revise)

The fold flag in Experiment 2 (`if tau_lam <= 0.0 or ...`) is already True after the first step, before the path reaches the fold: tau_lambda is negative from the start because the path is heading toward the fold. (a) Wrap the Experiment 2 loop in a function that records the normalised tau_lambda (including the initial value), x1 and lambda at every step. Show that tau_lambda starts negative, ends positive and changes sign exactly once. Show that every point with x1 < 0 lies on x1 = -sqrt(lambda), and that lambda never goes below 0. (b) Natural-parameter continuation: warm-start Newton on F_fold at lambda = 0.03, 0.02, 0.01, 0.0 and -0.01 (at most 50 iterations, stopping when max|F| < 1e-10). Explain why it converges at the first four, why convergence at lambda = 0 is only linear (the error halves at each step), and why it cannot converge at -0.01. (c) Stretch, an economic fold: adapt the loop to the Laffer revenue equation F(t, Rbar) = t(1-t)^eps - Rbar with eps = 2, starting at t = 0.05 with ds = 0.05, and stop once t > 0.8. Derive t* = 1/(1+eps) and R_max = R(t*). Show that PAC turns at R_max onto the high-tax branch, while Newton in t at Rbar = 1.01*R_max has no root to find.

*Check:* Asserts: `s=np.sign(tl_hist); assert s[0]<0 and s[-1]>0 and np.count_nonzero(np.diff(s))==1`; `neg=xs<0; assert neg.sum()>=10 and np.max(np.abs(xs[neg]+np.sqrt(lams[neg])))<1e-10 and lams.min()>=0`; `assert ok_hist[:4]==[True]*4 and ok_hist[-1] is False`; `assert np.allclose(ratios_at_lam0, 0.5, atol=1e-12)`; Laffer: `k=np.nonzero(np.diff(np.sign(vR_hist)))[0]; assert len(k)==1 and T[k[0]]<t_star<T[k[0]+1]`; every point with T>t_star matches brentq(R-Rb, t_star, 1) to 1e-10 and every point with T<t_star matches brentq on [0, t_star] to 1e-10; `assert Rb.max()<=R_max+1e-12`; Newton at 1.01*R_max `assert not conv`. RAN via jupytext on a modified copy (whole notebook about 4 s). Printed: tau_lam [-0.3714 -0.2002 -0.1244 -0.0455 0.0345 ... 0.608], with the sign change between entries 3 and 4; the notebook flag was already true after step 1 (True); 11 lower-branch points; min lambda 5.95e-4. Natural Newton converged at 0.03, 0.02 and 0.01 in 3-4 iterations and at 0.0 in 14 iterations with ratios exactly 0.5; it failed at -0.01 with residual 1.47e-2 after 50 iterations. Laffer: t*=1/3, R_max=0.148148; the sign change falls between t=0.326 and t=0.376; the high branch matches brentq to 2.2e-16 and the low branch to 2.0e-13; Newton at 1.01*R_max did not converge.

**[intermediate] Continuation versus a cold Newton start: a basin-of-attraction story** (revise)

Experiment 3 finds no fold (fold_points is empty), yet continuation can still matter. (a) On calib_cge with sigma=0, solve tau=1.0 twice. First use solve_trade_equilibrium(calib_cge, tau=1.0, tau_fd=1.0, sigma=0.0, tol=1e-9), which starts from the no-tariff baseline. Then use solve_keller_pac(calib_cge, tau_target=1.0, sigma=0.0, tol=1e-9). PAC's scalar tariff also applies to final demand, which is why tau_fd is passed to Newton. Confirm that the two solutions agree. (b) Loop the tariff over 1.0, 1.1, ..., 1.5. Find the first tariff at which cold Newton fails while PAC converges with no fold points. (c) At 1.5, warm-start Newton from PAC's x_sol (x0=rp.x_sol). Explain the result using the basin of attraction and the distance of the target equilibrium from the baseline guess (compare max|x_sol - build_initial_guess(calib_cge)| across tariffs). Why is this a homotopy argument and not a singularity? Note: with accounting='consistent', cold Newton converges at 1.5, so the basin is a property of the residual system and the starting point, not of the tariff alone.

*Check:* `assert rn1.converged and rp1.converged and np.max(np.abs(rn1.x_sol-rp1.x_sol))<1e-8`; `assert first_fail == 1.4`; `assert (not rn.converged) and rp.converged and np.max(np.abs(rp.residuals))<1e-9 and rp.metadata['fold_points']==[]`; `assert rw.converged and np.max(np.abs(rw.x_sol-rp.x_sol))<1e-8`. Cold Newton converged at 1.0, 1.1, 1.2 and 1.3 and failed at 1.4 and 1.5 (max residual 25.04 after 50 iterations at 1.5). PAC's residual at 1.5 is 2.4e-12 with fold_points []; w=[0.721, 1.549] against the baseline [1, 1]; warm-started Newton took 0 iterations. The distance max|x*-x0| grows smoothly: 0.34 at 1.0, 0.52 at 1.3, 0.59 at 1.4, 0.66 at 1.5. With accounting='consistent', cold Newton and PAC both converge at 1.5.

**[intermediate] What does a clamped Newton step buy?** (revise)

Experiment 4 reports that every step is 'bounded to 0.30' and that the manifold solve 'converged', but it never checks whether the steps solve S_ww dw = rhs_w. Define rel(d) = ||S_ww d - rhs_w|| / ||rhs_w||. Compute it for six steps: dw_unreg; dw_svd; the same SVD step with max_disp=None; the coordinatewise clamp clamp_wage_displacement(dw_unreg, 0.30); the truncated-SVD step, which drops the smallest singular value; and dw_cyp. (a) Show that dw_cyp is parallel to the exact solve (cosine = 1). Using the source of solve_cyprus_manifold_step, explain why res_cyp ≈ 0 and converged=True describe the step before its uniform rescaling. (b) Explain why the truncated-SVD residual equals exactly the component of rhs_w along the dropped left singular vector. Explain why coordinatewise clipping is worse than taking no step at all (rel > 1). (c) Treat S_ww w = rhs_w as the equation to solve. Iterate w <- w + clamp_wage_displacement(la.solve(S_ww, rhs_w - S_ww @ w), 0.30, mode='uniform') 100 times starting from w = 0. Show that the maximum error falls by exactly 0.30 per iteration, and compute how many iterations this capped exact-Newton scheme would need.

*Check:* `assert rel(dw_unreg) < 1e-12`; `assert rel(dw_svd) > 0.98 and rel(dw_cyp) > 0.999 and cos > 1-1e-12 and res_cyp < 1e-12 and conv_cyp`; `assert abs(rel(dw_tsvd) - abs(U[:,-1]@rhs_w)/np.linalg.norm(rhs_w)) < 1e-12`; `assert rel(dw_coord) > 1.0`; `assert abs((e0 - np.max(np.abs(dw_unreg - w))) - 30.0) < 1e-3` after 100 steps. RAN (jupytext copy, with the notebook's planted idx_cyp=14). Printed: rel unreg 1.34e-13, svd 0.9861, svd with max_disp=None 0.2723, truncated SVD 0.2723, manifold 0.99999989, coordinate clamp 23.516, cos 1.0000000000000004, res_cyp 4.4e-16. The error dropped by 30.0 after 100 capped steps, so about 9.33e6 steps would be needed.

**[stretch] Hawkins-Simon viability versus existence of a positive-wage equilibrium** (revise)

check_hawkins_simon_viability certifies that a positive Leontief price system exists (rho(B_tau) < 1). Is that enough for a general equilibrium with positive factor prices? Where does the solver stall (effective tariff = T*metadata['final_lambda'])? Compare that with the Hawkins-Simon threshold and with the certificate's upper bound at 2.32. Extrapolate w_A linearly to zero. (c) Rerun T=2.32 with sigma=1.0. Explain economically why substitution away from tariffed imported inputs removes the corner. Explain why the hundreds of tau_lambda <= 0 entries in fold_points at 2.32 do not show an economic fold: no branch turns back, and log w_A goes to minus infinity. ## 65_gvc_cascades_and_welfare_decomposition

**[basic] Cascade rounds, systemic nodes and the tax gross-up** (revise)

Use the notebook's OECD-layout calibration: `cal = calibrations['oecd']; n = cal.n_countries*cal.n_sectors; A = cal.a.reshape(n, n, order='F')`. Nodes are country-major: 0-2 are the first country's sectors and 3-8 are foreign. (a) Compute max|A_oecd - A_figaro| and max|A_oecd - A_exiobase|. What does this say about the three identical rows of the `network` table? (b) For a 1% unit-cost shock at node 0, build the cascade round by round, S_K = sum_{k<=K} (A^T)^k dv, and plot max|S_K - dp| against K on a log scale. Compare the decay ratio with rho from `compute_spectral_radius`. (c) Shock each node in turn and record the total cascade dp.sum()/0.01. Which node is most systemic? Show that these totals are the row sums of the Leontief inverse (I - A)^{-1} and explain why. (d) What share of node 0's cascade lands on foreign nodes? (e) The library's price equation is pp = (unit_va + inter_cost)/(1 - tax) (puremacro/trade/_accounting.py:129). Let D = diag(1/(1 - t)) with t = cal.tax.ravel(order='F'). Recompute the node-0 cascade as dp_tax = (I - D A^T)^{-1} D dv and explain why ad valorem taxes on output revenue compound along the supply chain.

(d) Optional: why does Tauchen with m = 3 held fixed not converge as N grows?

**[stretch] An error budget for the corner gap: chain identification vs 10,000 simulated draws** (revise)

The notebook names Aiyagari's simulation as a likely source of the Table II gaps. Aiyagari (QJE p. 677) simulates the chain for 10,000 draws, feeds them through the asset demand function and takes the sample mean as Ea; his footnote 35 says 20,000 draws changed results only slightly. Test this at the corner sigma = 0.4, rho = 0.9, mu = 5, where the notebook's gap is +0.256 pp. (a) Chain identification: sweep grid_width from 2.90 to 3.10 in steps of 0.005 and keep the widths whose seven-state Tauchen chain reproduces all eight Table I cells at two decimals. Simulate R = 400 independent households, starting at Ea with z drawn from markov_stationary(P), for T = 10,000 and 20,000 periods. Convert each time-average error into a rate error with -(mean - Ea)/slope, where slope is a centered finite difference of Ea(r) - K(r) with h = 2e-4. Compare the s.d. of these rate errors with the gap, the chain spread and the grid-refinement error printed in section 2. Explain why the s.d. falls like 1/sqrt(T), and why highly autocorrelated assets make 10,000 draws worth far fewer independent observations. (c) Discuss: the printed gaps are small and negative in the calmest sigma = 0.2 cells and grow positive toward the corner. Could independent, mean-zero sampling errors produce that pattern? What would a single random seed reused across all 24 cells change?

## 68_third_order_perturbation_trust

**[basic] Which risk term flips sign?** (keep)

Set rho_claim = -0.8 in the cell below and keep beta_claim = 0.95 and vol_claim = 0.1. The solver's ghuss changes sign but ghxss does not. Use B = 3*beta*A*rho*s^2/(1-beta*rho) from section 1 to explain why. News e_t moves next period's x by rho*e_t, and the convex payoff x^3 turns that into a price response driven by risk, which carries the sign of rho. Then predict the ratio ghxss/ghuss as a function of rho_claim and assert your prediction. This replaces the old Basic prompt, which said wrongly that both terms change sign.

**[intermediate] A symmetry test sees what a path check cannot** (revise)

Section 3 showed that a path check misses an antisymmetric g_xxu error. Build the check that catches it. Start with _, T_rbc = labelled_tensors(rbc). (a) Write symmetry_defect(field, extra=0). It reshapes T_rbc[field] + extra to (variables, index1, index2, ...) and returns the largest gap between entries that differ only by permuting state indices among themselves or shock indices among themselves. Assert that the gap is at rounding level for ghxx, ghuu, ghxxx, ghxxu, ghxuu and ghuuu. (b) Apply symmetry_defect to ghxxu plus each of section 3's planted errors. Which error does the symmetry test flag, and which does the path check flag? (c) Take D = np.random.default_rng(1).normal(size=(2,2,2))*1e-3 as a g_xxx error, and let Dsym be its average over the 6 index permutations. Contract both with x1⊗x1⊗x1, where x1 is the lagged first-order states from rbc.simulate(periods=2500, seed=0, burn=0).states_1st. Show that paths cannot tell D from Dsym. How many of the 8 directions are invisible, and why? (d) Is an antisymmetric g_xx error such as [[0,1e-3],[-1e-3,0]] also harmless? Contract it with x1⊗x1, the order-2 term, and with x1⊗x2, the order-3 term H_xx·(x1⊗x2), using states_2nd from the same simulation.

*Check:* assert max(defects.values()) < 1e-12; assert abs(defect_antisym - 2*size) < 1e-12 and defect_sym < 1e-12; assert np.max(np.abs(kron3@D.ravel() - kron3@Dsym.ravel())) < 1e-15 and np.max(np.abs(D-Dsym)) > 1e-4. Expected answer: 8 - 4 = 4 invisible directions, since there are C(4,3) = 4 symmetric 3-tensors in 2 dimensions. assert eff2 < 1e-15 and eff3 > 1e-8. Ran on a modified copy with jupytext; every assert passed. Printed: defects of the correct tensors at most 4.5e-17; antisymmetric plant 2.5857766e-3 = 2*size, symmetric plant 4.5e-17; path gap 2.6e-18 against tensor gap 9.1e-4. In scratch, the symmetrizer had rank 4. The antisymmetric g_xx error moved paths by 1.3e-20 at order 2 and by 7.0e-6 at order 3.

**[stretch] Is informality countercyclical, and is it a buffer?** (revise)

§3.2 and §4.3 use informality as a level: a correction to the labour share and a cross-country regressor. This exercise looks at its cycle. (b) Tabulate corr(DF, Dlog y) by country, with and without 2020-21. Then estimate one pooled slope with country fixed effects: demean both variables within country and run `ols(dF_w, dy_w[:, None], cov_type='hac-groupsum', cov_kwds={'maxlags': 4, 'time': <integer quarter codes>})`, which gives Driscoll-Kraay errors. Report it for both samples and for the Latin American subsample. What does 2020 do to the answer, and why would lockdowns act that way on informal jobs? Is dropping 2020-21 the same judgement call exercise 17 asks you to defend? (c) A countercyclical rate F = I/E does not by itself mean informal jobs rise in slumps; formal jobs may simply fall faster. Where `panel` also has `emp`, set I = F/100 * emp and split the slope of Dlog F on Dlog y into the slope of Dlog I minus the slope of Dlog E. Which economies look like a buffer, with informal employment rising when output falls? Which are merely less cyclical? Caveat: `emp` is domestic employment from the accounts while F comes from resident labour-force surveys, the mismatch §4.3 flags.

## T02_A_neoclassical_growth_local

**[intermediate] Two routes to the half-life: how the gap between them grows with the shock** (revise)

Your turn. The section 'How Fast Does Capital Converge?' compares the Klein half-life ln(0.5)/ln(g_k) (`hl_base`) with a single non-linear transition that starts from k0 = 0.5 k_ss. Here you turn that single comparison into a function of the shock size. (1) `hl_nonlinear` interpolates the capital gap linearly between years. Write `half_life(gap)` that interpolates log(gap) instead. On the exact geometric sequence `gk_base**np.arange(80)`, show that your function returns `hl_base` and that linear interpolation does not. (2) Write `nl_gap(frac)`: call `solve_perfect_foresight` on `neoclassical_stacked_equations` from k0 = frac*k_ss (T=200, tol=1e-11, max_iter=80, as in that section) and return the normalised gap (k_t - k_ss)/(k0 - k_ss) with k0 prepended. For d in {0.10, 0.05, 0.02, 0.01}, compute the discrepancies `disc_below` = half_life(nl_gap(1-d)) - hl_base and `disc_above` = half_life(nl_gap(1+d)) - hl_base. (3) Starting from 0.5 k_ss, compute the one-year contraction ratio gap[61]/gap[60]. Questions: Which economy converges more slowly than the linear rule predicts, the capital-poor one or the capital-rich one? What does that say about the true capital policy k'(k) compared with its tangent at k_ss? Why is the discrepancy first order in d, when the level errors in 'How Large Can a Shock Be...' scale as d^2? What is left after you average the two sides? Why does the stable eigenvalue predict the late contraction rate exactly even from a 50% gap, but not the half-life?

*Check:* assert abs(half_life(gk_base**np.arange(80)) - hl_base) < 1e-10
assert np.all(disc_below > 0) and np.all(disc_above < 0)
assert 0.9 < np.polyfit(np.log(ds), np.log(disc_below), 1)[0] < 1.15   # first order in d
assert abs(disc_below[-1]) < 0.01                                        # d = 1%: within 0.01 years
sym = 0.5*(disc_below + disc_above)
assert 1.8 < np.polyfit(np.log(ds), np.log(np.abs(sym)), 1)[0] < 2.2     # odd term cancels, leaving the quadratic law
assert abs(gap50[61]/gap50[60] - gk_base) < 1e-5

One-sided slope 1.028, symmetric-average slope 2.002, ratio60 - g_k = 3.96e-6. All asserts passed.

## T02_C_new_keynesian_local

**[stretch] Banxico's estimated Taylor rule on the determinacy map** (revise)

Your turn (stretch). Does Banxico's estimated reaction function sit inside the determinacy region you mapped in Section 7? (1) Load these files from data_curso with ruta_dato/DATOS: the 3-month interbank rate IR3TIB01MXM156N (a market rate, not the policy rate before 2008; see T01_C section 5), 12-month CPI inflation CPALTT01MXM659N (it ends in 2024-07), Mexican log industrial production oecd_mei_mex_log_ip.csv (the gap is the cycle from puremacro.data.hp_filter with lamb=129600), and FEDFUNDS. (2) Estimate the monthly partial-adjustment rule i_t = c + rho*i_{t-1} + a*pi_t + b*gap_t [+ d*ff_t] with puremacro.regress.ols(..., cov_type='HAC', cov_kwds={'maxlags': 12}). The long-run responses are phi_pi = a/(1-rho) and phi_y = b/(1-rho). Compute the delta-method standard error of phi_pi from r.cov_params(). (3) Build a specification curve over six windows (2002-2019, 2002-2024, 2008-2019, 2001-2019, 2005-2019, 2010-2019), with and without FEDFUNDS, and with the gap HP-filtered on the full sample or inside the window. Flag every specification with rho_hat >= 1: it has no long-run response. (4) Map each usable estimate into the model. phi_pi carries over unchanged, since it is a ratio of annualised rates. phi_y_model = phi_y/4, because the model's rate is quarterly (the .mod file uses the same /4 on line 34). Set rho_i = rho_hat**3. Then solve with load_mod(nk_mod_path, params={...}, strict=False) and read solution.eu. (5) Before you trust the analytic frontier kappa*(phi_pi-1) + (1-beta)*phi_y > 0 for this INERTIAL rule, test it against the QZ verdict on a grid of (phi_pi, phi_y >= 0, rho_i in [0, 0.99]). Then try phi_pi=1.5, phi_y=-0.3, rho_i=0. Where does the frontier stop being valid? (6) Report three things: how many usable specifications are determinate, the range of phi_pi, and in how many of them the 90% interval for phi_pi contains the frontier value 1 - (1-beta)*phi_y/kappa. What does this tell you about reading the Taylor principle off a regression? Why should a window in which inflation stayed near target be the least informative? Hint: look at 1/(1-rho) when rho is close to 1.

## T03_A_aggregate_shocks

**[basic] Is the Krusell-Smith forecast rule a fixed point?** (revise)

In a Krusell-Smith equilibrium, the rule households use to forecast K has to be the rule the simulated economy produces. Use x, y and zt from the scatter-plot cell (burn = 300; they are built from ks.K_path and Z_path). For each aggregate state, re-run the OLS of log K' on a constant and log K. Store the coefficients in b_hat, with shape (2, 2): the row is the state (low, high) and the columns are (b0, b1). Store the R-squared values in r2_hat. Compare b_hat with np.c_[ks.b0, ks.b1] and report gap = max absolute difference. Why is the gap not exactly zero? Use two facts about krusell_smith. First, it stops when the new OLS estimate moves less than tol = 1e-3 from the rule that generated the simulation. Second, it returns the damped average 0.5*new + 0.5*old (damping = 0.5). What upper bound on the gap follows from these two facts? What does the fixed point say about households' beliefs?

*Check:* gap = np.abs(b_hat - np.c_[ks.b0, ks.b1]).max(); assert gap < 0.5 * 1e-3, gap  # (1-damping)*tol
assert np.allclose(r2_hat, ks.r_squared, rtol=0, atol=1e-10)
RAN IT: I appended the cell to a scratch copy of curso/notebooks/T03_A_aggregate_shocks.ipynb and ran it with jupytext --execute --run-path curso/notebooks. The whole notebook took 32 s. Output: b_hat = [[0.24315, 0.92965], [0.26802, 0.92638]], rule = [[0.24335, 0.92960], [0.26832, 0.92629]], gap = 2.92e-04 (below the 5e-4 bound). r2_hat matches ks.r_squared exactly: [0.99867, 0.99929].

**[intermediate] R-squared vs Den Haan dynamic forecast errors** (revise)

The R-squared of about 0.999 measures one-step-ahead fit: each period the rule is fed the true K_t. Households, however, forecast many periods ahead using only their rule. Following Den Haan (2010), set lk[0] = logK[burn]. Then iterate lk[j+1] = ks.b0[Z_path[t]] + ks.b1[Z_path[t]] * lk[j] for t = burn, ..., len(logK)-2, along the realized Z_path, and never feed the simulated K back in. Compute two error series in percent. The dynamic error is dyn = 100*np.abs(lk - logK[burn:]). The one-step error is one = 100*np.abs(y - (ks.b0[zt] + ks.b1[zt]*x)). Print the max and mean of each next to 1 - ks.r_squared and the standard deviation of log K. Is an unexplained variance of about 0.1% a good guide to how wrong households' multi-period forecasts are? Express the largest dynamic error as a fraction of the standard deviation of log K.

*Check:* assert dyn.shape == (ks.K_path.size - burn,) and dyn[0] == 0
assert dyn.max() > 2 * one.max(), (dyn.max(), one.max())
RAN IT (same scratch run, 32 s for the whole notebook). Dynamic error: max 1.650%, mean 0.428%. One-step error: max 0.365%, mean 0.097%. 1-R2 = [0.00133, 0.00071]. sd(log K) = 4.200%. Ratio max dyn / max one-step = 4.53.

**[stretch] Why forecast errors accumulate: persistence of the omitted distribution** (revise)

Take the one-step errors u = y - (ks.b0[zt] + ks.b1[zt]*x) and the signed dynamic error e = lk - logK[burn:] from the previous exercise. The dynamic error follows e_{t+1} = b1 e_t + u_{t+1}. If u were i.i.d., this would give std(e)/std(u) = 1/sqrt(1 - b1^2), about 2.7 for b1 of about 0.93. Compute the first-order autocorrelation rho_u = np.corrcoef(u[:-1], u[1:])[0, 1] and the ratio e.std()/u.std(). Explain why the ratio is above the i.i.d. benchmark. What does a persistent one-step error reveal about the information in the wealth distribution that log K leaves out? Optional: derive the AR(1)-residual ratio sqrt((1 + rho*b1)/((1 - rho*b1)(1 - b1^2))), compare it with the measured ratio, and say why it overshoots (u is not exactly AR(1), and b1 switches with Z).

*Check:* assert rho_u > 0.3, rho_u
assert e.std() / u.std() > 1 / np.sqrt(1 - ks.b1.max()**2)
RAN IT (same scratch run): rho_u = 0.697 and e.std()/u.std() = 4.50. The i.i.d. benchmark is 2.71 (low TFP) and 2.65 (high TFP). Computed separately: the AR(1)-residual formula with rho = 0.697 gives 5.87 and 5.72. Mean signed dynamic error is -0.008%, so the rule is unbiased but its errors persist.

## T03_C_growth_at_risk

**[intermediate] Growth-at-Risk for Mexico: is the left tail imported?** (revise)

This notebook only looks at the US. Here you ask whether Mexico's downside risk comes from US financial conditions or from home. (1) Build a quarterly panel. For growth use g = crecimiento("MEX"): the quarter-on-quarter % change of seasonally adjusted real GDP. It is NOT annualized, so multiply its slopes by 4 before comparing them with the US table in Section 4. For US conditions use fci_real from Section 4. For Mexican uncertainty use lepu, the quarterly mean of np.log(epu) from DATOS / "epu_mex_mensual.csv", and keep only quarters with all three months. Concatenate with sort=True and drop NaNs; you should get 1996Q1-2022Q4 (108 quarters). (2) Fit qar(g, quantiles=(0.05,0.25,0.50,0.75,0.95), horizons=(1,4), p=1, controls=[nfci, lepu], n_boot=20). With p=1 the lag block is g_t itself, so beta1 = g_t, beta2 = NFCI_t and beta3 = log EPU_t. (3) Tabulate beta2 and beta3 across tau for h=1 and h=4. Does a tightening of US financial conditions move Mexico's 5% quantile more than its median? Does domestic policy uncertainty? What does your answer say about where Mexico's left tail comes from? (4) Check that the fit is a true quantile regression. Rebuild the design matrix that qar used: rows t = 1..T-h-1, X = [1, g_t, nfci_t, lepu_t], target g_{t+h}. Verify the Koenker-Bassett optimality conditions for every (h, tau). Then refit each (h, tau) with quantreg on the same X and compare the two solvers.

*Check:* Code to append after Section 6 (cell 24):
y=mx['g'].to_numpy(); Z=mx[['nfci','lepu']].to_numpy(); T=len(y)
for h in (1,4):
 X=np.column_stack([np.ones(T-1-h), y[1:T-h], Z[1:T-h]]); yh=y[1+h:]
 for tau in taus:
  b = the row's [beta0..beta3]; u=yh-X@b
  assert (u<-1e-7).mean() <= tau <= (u<=1e-7).mean()
  assert (abs(u)<=1e-7).sum() >= X.shape[1]  # an LP vertex fits k points exactly
  bi=np.asarray(quantreg(yh,X,q=tau).params); rho=lambda r: np.sum(r*(tau-(r<0)))
  assert rho(u) <= rho(yh-X@bi)+1e-9  # exact LP is never beaten by IRLS
  assert np.max(abs(b-bi)) < 1e-3
RAN it: I inserted this into a scratch copy of the notebook and executed it with the jupytext command. The full notebook plus both exercises ran in about 25 s and printed 'CHECK A passed' for all 10 (h, tau) pairs. Shares below zero ranged from 0.028 to 0.932, always bracketing tau. Exactly 4 residuals were zero in every fit. The largest coefficient gap between the two solvers was 6.8e-05 (h=1, tau=0.05). The LP check loss was lower than the IRLS loss by 3e-07 to 3.9e-06. Negative control: using g_{t-1} in place of g_t, or a target of g_{t+h-1}, fails 10 of 10 pairs. So the check catches timing mistakes and does not leak any economic answer.

**[stretch] Crossing quantiles, rearrangement, and a 2008-size US shock to Mexico** (revise)

Start from your Mexico QAR (p=1, controls NFCI and log EPU). (1) For h=1 and h=4, compute the n x 5 matrix of in-sample fitted quantiles, Q = X @ B.T. Count the dates where the quantile lines cross, meaning np.diff(Q, axis=1) < -1e-9 (the tolerance ignores floating-point ties). (2) Rearrange them (Chernozhukov, Fernandez-Val and Galichon, 2010): Qr = np.sort(Q, axis=1). Write rho_tau(u) = tau*u + max(-u, 0) and use the rearrangement inequality to prove the following: for ANY realized y, the pooled loss sum_tau rho_tau(y - q_tau) can only fall when the q's are sorted in tau, and it falls strictly exactly when they cross. Then verify this date by date. (3) Set g and log EPU at their sample means. Evaluate the conditional quantiles twice: with NFCI at its sample 10th percentile, and with NFCI at its 2008Q4 value (the sample maximum). Rearrange if needed, then report Q_0.05, the median and the spread (median - Q_0.05). Why is the 90th percentile of NFCI a poor 'tight' state in 1996-2022? (4) Re-estimate on 1996-2019 (mx.loc[:'2019-12-31']). How much of the tail slope and the stress-state GaR depended on the 2020 quarters? Optional: fit fit_skewt_to_quantiles in each state. Before you interpret alpha, print fit.success and max|F(Q_tau) - tau|, and explain why 5 anchor quantiles from about 100 observations may not pin down 4 skew-t parameters.

*Check:* tau_v=np.array(taus); Q=X@B.T; Qr=np.sort(Q,axis=1)
cross=np.any(np.diff(Q,axis=1)<-1e-9,axis=1)
L=lambda M: np.sum((yh[:,None]-M)*(tau_v-((yh[:,None]-M)<0)),axis=1)
assert np.all(L(Qr) <= L(Q)+1e-12)
assert np.array_equal(L(Qr) < L(Q)-1e-12, cross)
RAN it on both samples and both horizons in the same scratch notebook copy; it printed 'CHECK B passed'. Crossing dates: 4/106 (1996-2022, h=1), 2/103 (1996-2022, h=4), 1/94 (1996-2019, h=1) and 0/91 (1996-2019, h=4). Pooled loss fell from 198.585 to 194.703 at h=1 on the full sample. With the 1996-2019 sample at h=1 and NFCI at the 2008Q4 value, the raw quantiles are non-monotone (0.50 above 0.75), so the rearrangement matters out of the typical range. Without the tolerance, a -2.2e-16 tie makes the strict-equality assert fail; the tolerance is required.

## T05_A_svar_identification

**[stretch] Galí's hours debate on Mexican data: 59 quarters and one lockdown** (revise)

T01_A found that between 2005 and 2019 Mexico grew by adding hours, not by raising productivity (GDP +1.66% a year, output per hour -0.20%). Do Mexican hours fall after a technology shock identified by a long-run restriction, as Galí (1999) found for the US?
(1) Build the data with `from _datos import mercado_laboral, volumen`: y = log volumen('gdp','MEX') and h = log mercado_laboral().loc['MEX','hours'] (total hours, seasonally adjusted with X-13 in T01_B). Set dprod = 100*diff(y-h) and dh = 100*diff(h). Population growth in dh ends up in the VAR constant.
(2) Estimate `bq_svar(Y, p=4, horizon=12, permanent_var_idx=0, n_boot=300, seed=0)` on [dprod, dh] twice: first on 2005Q2-2019Q4, then through 2026Q1. bq_svar returns cumulated IRFs. So irf_point[0] is the impact matrix B0 and irf_point[:,1,0] is already the level of hours. Do not cumsum it again.
(3) Check the identification. Take A and Sigma from estimate_var(Y, 4). Assert B0 B0' = Sigma. Assert that entry [0,1] of C(1) = (I - sum A_j)^-1 B0 is zero.
(4) Get the US benchmark from `run_gali_replication()` (puremacro.examples.gali_1999_hours).
(5) Compute the structural shocks eps_t = B0^-1 u_t and read off 2020Q2.
Explain three things. Why can't 59 quarters settle the question (Faust-Leeper, section 1)? Why does adding the lockdown quarter, when productivity rose 2.5% and hours fell 23%, produce a 'significant' Galí result, and what composition effect is behind it? Does Mexico's negative productivity trend matter for what the long-run restriction identifies?

- np.allclose(B0@B0.T, Sigma, atol=1e-10) and abs(C1[0,1]) < 1e-10, on both samples
- us['diff'].irf_upper[0,1,0] < 0
- Mexico pre-2020: irf_lower[0,1,0] < 0 < irf_upper[0,1,0]
- Mexico through 2026Q1: irf_upper[0,1,0] < 0

'pre-2020 T=59 sd(u_h)=0.51 hours impact -0.159 [-0.308, +0.029]'
'to 2026Q1 T=84 sd(u_h)=2.99 hours impact -1.273 [-2.749, -0.155] 2020Q2 shocks: tech +2.6 sd, other -7.1 sd'

## T06_B_life_cycle_and_demographics

**[intermediate] Mexico's demographic transition in the OLG age weights** (revise)

The aging experiment in Section 4 uses only US population data. Now use Mexico's. Load DATOS / "POPTOTMXA647NWDB.csv" (Mexico's total population, bundled in data_curso), compute annual growth with .pct_change(), and average it over 1960-1969 and over 2020-2029 (the data end in 2025). One model period is one year, so treat each decade mean as a steady-state growth rate n. For each n, with no mortality: (a) build w = stationary_age_weights(J, pop_growth=n); (b) check that the retiree share w[J_R_base:].sum() equals the geometric closed form (g**-J_R - g**-J)/(1 - g**-J), where g = 1+n and J_R = J_R_base = 30; (c) report OADR = share_old/(1-share_old), capital per capita K = w @ assets_by_age, capital per efficiency unit K/L with L = w @ kappa_profile, and the PAYGO balancing tax tau* = theta_base*share_old / (w[:J_R] @ kappa_profile[:J_R]); (d) sweep n over np.linspace(-0.01, 0.04, 51) and show that OADR falls monotonically in n. In two sentences, explain why K per capita rises when n falls even though no household changes its saving policy. Stretch: differentiate w_j ∝ (1+n)^(-j) to show dK/dn = -Cov_w(j, a_j)/(1+n). Confirm it with a central finite difference, and link its sign to the gap between the asset-weighted mean age and the population mean age. Optional: repeat with survival=s_base, and say why the closed form in (b) no longer holds. Caveat: these are stationary, partial-equilibrium comparisons (r fixed at 4%, a US-shaped kappa, retirement at model age 30). They are not a forecast for Mexico.

## T06_C_portfolios_and_preferences

**[basic] Epstein-Zin nests CRRA: three solvers, one policy** (revise)

Place this after the EGM vs VFI cell (curso/notebooks/T06_C_portfolios_and_preferences.ipynb:444-466). (1) On paper: set gamma = 1/psi, so rho = 1 - 1/psi = 1 - gamma. Show that the recursion V^rho = (1-beta) c^rho + beta E[V'^rho] becomes V^(1-gamma)/(1-gamma) = (1-beta) * sum_t beta^t c_t^(1-gamma)/(1-gamma), which is CRRA utility scaled by (1-beta) plus a constant. (2) Take the cell-14 economy (a_g, z_g, Pz, r=0.03, beta=0.96, gamma=2). Solve EpsteinZinProblem with felicity equal to consumption, c = (1+r)a + exp(z) - a' (return -inf when c <= 0), and psi = 1/gamma. Compare its a'-policy with vsol.policy_aprime. Check whether the value functions satisfy the transform from (1), given that vsol uses u(c) = (c^(1-gamma)-1)/(1-gamma). (3) Build the consumption policy c_EZ and measure max|c_EZ - c_EGM| at median income (column mid). Compare it with the cell-14 gap and with the grid step a_g[1]-a_g[0]. Why does EpsteinZinProblem reject gamma = 1 and psi = 1 (puremacro/vfi/epstein_zin.py:53-56)? Use the median-income column, not all z. At the top income state the gap reaches 1.26 because EGM clamps a' at a_grid[-1] (puremacro/vfi/egm.py:78-79). That is a truncation artifact, not a solver disagreement.

Cell 19 compares arc elasticities from a +2% productivity shock with a hand-typed target of '~19-20'. Replace both sides with numbers you can defend. (b) Model: differentiate the notebook's equilibrium condition (c*theta^alpha/mu)(r+s+beta*c*theta) = (1-beta)(p-z) with respect to log p. This gives eps = [p/(p-z)] / [alpha + beta*c*theta/(r+s+beta*c*theta)]. Code it as eps_point(z, beta, c) using solve_theta_nash. Check it against a central finite difference in log p with step h=1e-5, for Shimer (c_shimer) and Hagedorn-Manovskii (c_hm). Derive the Hall point elasticity from solve_theta_hall. (c) Build one table with: arc and point elasticities for the three calibrations; the data ratio for both lambdas; and the numbers other course notebooks print (lesson 11: 1.77 and 24.52 from a +1% arc; T02_C: 0.91 from simulation). **[stretch] Ins or outs? Decomposing Mexico's flow-implied unemployment** (revise)

Section 1 (cell 6) claims that in US data the job-finding rate drives most unemployment volatility. Test the analogue for Mexico on the u*_t from the previous exercise. Linearise u*(P) around the sample-mean matrix. For each of the 12 off-diagonal probabilities p_ij, compute du*/dp_ij by central finite differences, shifting p_ii by the opposite amount so rows still sum to 1. Build contributions C_ij,t = du*/dp_ij * (p_ij,t - mean p_ij). Then compute Elsby-Hobijn-Sahin-style shares, share_ij = cov(u*_t, C_ij,t)/var(u*_t), and a residual share for the non-linearity, resid = cov(u*_t, u*_t - sum C)/var(u*_t). Group the shares into ins (flows into U), outs (flows out of U) and flows among F, I and N. Is Mexico's u* an 'ins' or an 'outs' economy, and which origin state (F, I or N) dominates? Does the answer survive using only pre-2020 data, or 12-month moving averages of the matrices? Note that you are decomposing the variance of u* levels, trend included.

*Check:* assert abs(sum(share.values()) + resid - 1) < 1e-12 (an identity). assert r2 > 0.95, where r2 = 1 - var(du - sum C)/var(du) measures how well the linearisation fits. I ran this in the scratch copy and both asserts passed. r2 = 0.987. Ins 0.831 (I->U 0.41, N->U 0.28, F->U 0.14); outs 0.097; other flows 0.064; residual 0.007. Robustness from a separate scratch probe: pre-2020, ins 0.81 and outs 0.11 (r2 0.991); 12-month moving average, 0.87 and 0.10 (0.999); quarterly averages, 0.86 and 0.08 (0.979).

## curso T05_C_staggered_did (course DiD lesson); new exercise

**[intermediate] Card-Krueger (1994): the 2x2 building block on real data** (revise)

Your turn: every estimator in this notebook is built from 2x2 DiDs, so here is the best-known one. Download https://davidcard.berkeley.edu/data_sets/njmin.zip (you need internet; Colab works). Read `codebook` and parse the fixed-width `public.dat`, where '.' means missing. For each wave, build full-time-equivalent employment, FTE = EMPFT + NMGRS + 0.5*EMPPT (use the ...2 columns for wave 2). Permanently closed stores (STATUS2 = 3) already report 0. Temporarily closed stores (STATUS2 in 2, 4, 5) are missing. Check that SHEET is unique before you use it as a store id. Compute: (a) `did_all`, the NJ-minus-PA change in mean FTE using every store available in each wave; (b) on the balanced sample (FTE observed in both waves), regress dFTE on a constant and an NJ dummy with `ols(..., cov_type="HC2")`, giving `did_bal` and `se_bal`; (c) `did_closed0`, the balanced-sample DiD after setting wave-2 FTE of temporarily closed stores to 0; (d) stack the balanced sample as a two-period panel (time 0/1, D = NJ x post) and estimate `beta_fe` with the two-way within transform plus `ols` from Section 4, and `cs2 = callaway_santanna(...)` with treat_time = 1 for NJ and NaN for PA; (e) `chg_hi_nj`, the mean dFTE of balanced NJ stores whose wave-1 WAGE_ST >= 5.00, and `chg_pa_bal`, the PA mean change. Then explain: why do (b), `beta_fe` and `cs2.att_overall` agree to machine precision, and why can this design not produce the sign flip of Section 4? Why does HC2 reproduce the paper's unpooled two-sample standard error? What does comparing high-wage NJ stores with PA test? What does a positive employment DiD mean for the competitive model of the low-wage labour market?

*Check:* # Card & Krueger (1994), NBER WP 4509, Table 3 (PDF p.49) and text (PDF p.13), read this session from nber.org/system/files/working_papers/w4509/w4509.pdf

assert abs(did_bal - 2.75) < 0.005, did_bal           # row 4: balanced 2.75 (1.34)
assert abs(se_bal - 1.34) < 0.005, se_bal             # HC2 = unpooled two-sample SE
assert abs(did_closed0 - 2.51) < 0.005, did_closed0   # row 5: 2.51 (1.35)
assert abs(beta_fe - did_bal) < 1e-8                  # TWFE == 2x2 DiD
assert abs(cs2.att_overall - did_bal) < 1e-8          # CS with T=2 == 2x2 DiD
assert abs(chg_hi_nj + 2.16) < 0.005 and abs(chg_pa_bal + 2.28) < 0.005  # text p.11: -2.16 vs -2.28
