## curso/notebooks/T02_B_rbc_dynare_local.ipynb (plus a new section in notebooks/68_third_order_perturbation_trust.py)

**[basic] Dynare's default order-2 table and a claim to x^2** (revise)

A claim that pays x_t^2 has price y_t = beta*E_t y_{t+1} + x_t^2, where x_t = rho*x_{t-1} + e_t. The exact solution is y = A x^2 + C with A = 1/(1 - beta*rho^2). Build it with load_mod(..., order=2), using the model text of tests/test_dsge_order2_pruned_moments.py:50-53, at beta=0.95, rho=0.8, s=0.1. Before running anything, use Isserlis' theorem to predict Var(y) = 2A^2 v^2, with v = s^2/(1-rho^2), and corr(y_t, y_{t-k}) = rho^(2k). Then compare Var(y) from three sources: claim.theoretical_moments(), which is Dynare's default stoch_simul(order=2); claim.theoretical_moments(pruning=True); and the sample variance of a 200,000-period claim.simulate(). Why does the default report zero? Which number does the simulator agree with? Do the two conventions report the same mean?

*Check:* Asserts: abs(default Var(y)) < 1e-20; |pruned Var(y)/(2A^2v^2) - 1| < 1e-10; pruned autocorr at lags 1-3 equals rho^(2k) (rtol 1e-10); |simulated var/(2A^2v^2) - 1| < 0.1. The cell must first call warnings.filterwarnings('ignore', message='forecast-error variance is zero'). RAN: I added it as a new cell after cell 7 of a modified copy of T02_B and executed the copy with jupytext --execute (run-path curso/notebooks): exit 0, 51 s for the whole notebook. Printed: default 0.000e+00, pruning=True 0.0100428, closed form 0.0100428, 200k simulation (seed 1) 0.0099116; autocorr [0.64, 0.4096, 0.262144]. Both conventions give the mean 0.5556 = A v + beta A s^2/(1-beta). Over 6 seeds and 4 calibrations, including rho=0.95 and rho=-0.5, the simulated variance erred by at most 3.7%, so the 10% band holds for parameters students pick.

**[intermediate] Do the conventions matter in the RBC? Read them as powers of sigma** (revise)

Use sol2 from the cell above. For sigma in {1, 2, 3}, collect the Variance column from sol2.theoretical_moments(sigma=s) and from sol2.theoretical_moments(sigma=s, pruning=True). Also collect the first-order variances V1 = m_rbc.theoretical_moments().moments['Variance']. Show three things: the default variance is exactly sigma^2*V1; the pruned variance adds a term proportional to sigma^4 (by what factor does that gap grow from sigma=1 to sigma=3?); and the means are identical. Which variable has no gap at all, and why? At sigma=1 the gap is at most about 0.1% of the variance in this model, yet for the claim to x^2 it is the whole variance: why? Finally, confirm that m_rbc.stoch_simul(order=2, irf=0, pruning=True) reports the same pruned variances.

*Check:* Asserts: the largest mean difference between conventions is < 1e-14; the default variances equal outer(V1, sigma^2) (rtol 1e-12); gap(3) = 81*gap(1) (rtol 1e-8, atol 1e-20); the stoch_simul(order=2, irf=0, lags=1, pruning=True) variances equal the pruned variances at sigma=1 (rtol 1e-10). RAN in the same modified T02_B copy; all asserts passed. Printed relative gaps (pruned/default - 1) at sigma=1: y 6.46e-4, c 3.61e-4, k 7.04e-4, i 1.06e-3, l 5.9e-5, a 0. At sigma=3, y 5.82e-3: 9 times the relative gap and 81 times the absolute gap.

**[intermediate] Order-3 skewness and kurtosis, measured against a Gaussian ruler** (revise)

At order 3, sol3.theoretical_moments() gives the exact mean, variance and autocorrelations, but reports skewness and kurtosis as NaN. sol3.ergodic_moments(periods=50_000, seed=sd) estimates those two from one pruned simulation; its Mean and StdDev columns are the exact values. Run it for seeds 0-4. Technology a is an exact Gaussian AR(1), so its true skewness is 0 and its kurtosis 3. Use its spread across seeds to measure the Monte Carlo noise. Which shape moments of output stand out from that noise? Next, simulate sol3.simulate(periods=100_000, seed=0, burn=1_000). Form the level of output as m_rbc.steady_state['y'] plus the deviation, and compare the skewness of that level with the skewness of its log. Where does output's skewness come from? Optional: is it a third-order effect? Rerun with sol2 and the same seed.

*Check:* Here se = std across the 5 seeds / sqrt(5). Asserts: |mean skew(a)| < 3 se; |mean kurt(a) - 3| < 3 se; mean skew(y) > 5 se; |skew(log y)| < 0.2*skew(y level). RAN in the modified T02_B copy; all asserts passed and the cell took about 15 s. Printed means over seeds, with standard errors: a skewness 0.0152 (0.0115), kurtosis 2.969 (0.042); y skewness 0.122 (0.0105), kurtosis 2.978 (0.051); c skewness 0.098; k skewness 0.130; l skewness -0.006 (0.013). Skewness of the level of y 0.0990, of its log -0.0083. In a separate script, sol2.simulate with seed 0 gave skew(y) 0.09904 against 0.09898 at order 3, so the skewness is already a second-order effect.

**[stretch] Which term of the variance does order 3 add at sigma^4?** (revise)

The exact order-3 pruned variance is a polynomial in sigma^2. Evaluate sol3.theoretical_moments(sigma=s).moments['Variance'] for s in {0.5, 1, 2, 3, 4}. Fit it by least squares on sigma^2, sigma^4 and sigma^6. Show three things: the fit is exact; the sigma^2 coefficient is the first-order variance V1; and the sigma^4 coefficient differs from the order-2 pruned sigma^4 term you found in the previous exercise. Write x = x1 + x2 + x3, where x_j is of order sigma^j and the shocks are Gaussian. Which covariance enters the variance at sigma^4 at order 3 but not at order 2? What does that mean for reading the order-2 pruned variance as the sigma^4 correction to the first-order variance? Optional: check your answer with the controls_1st, controls_2nd and controls_3rd parts of a long sol3.simulate.

*Check:* Asserts: the fitted polynomial reproduces the variances (rtol 1e-10, atol 1e-22); the sigma^2 coefficient equals V1 (rtol 1e-9); for y, the ratio of the order-3 to the order-2 sigma^4 coefficient is > 2. RAN in the modified T02_B copy; all asserts passed, with a largest relative fit residual of about 9e-14. sigma^4 coefficient, order 2 to order 3: y 8.357e-7 to 2.339e-6 (ratio 2.80); c 3.64; k 3.06; i 2.29; l 1.06. The sigma^6 coefficient for y is 7.93e-10. Expected answer: 2Cov(x1, x3). A separate check with a 400k-period sol3.simulate (seed 3) gave 2Cov(y1,y3) = 1.498e-6 against the exact difference of 1.503e-6, and Var(y2) = 8.34e-7 against 8.357e-7.

## local_llm_uncertainty

**[basic] Model or keyword rule? A negation and a synonym test** (revise)

Your turn: is the uncertainty score coming from a language model or from a keyword rule, and how can you tell? Run `print(provider.name)`. "mock" means the offline fallback MockProvider is answering. "mlx", "llamacpp", "ollama" or "openai" means a real local model. Build two probe records in the same (date, text, url) form as CORPUS: PROBES = [("2020-06-01", "Officials said the outlook is no longer uncertain.", "http://example.test/c"), ("2020-07-01", "Officials warned the outlook is highly unpredictable and could shift abruptly.", "http://example.test/d")]. The first one resolves uncertainty but still uses the word "uncertain". The second one expresses uncertainty without using that word. Score them next to the April warning: p_april, p_resolved, p_synonym = [p for _, p in llm_prob_kernel([CORPUS[1]] + PROBES, provider=provider, category="economic uncertainty")]. Before you run it, predict the three numbers under a keyword rule and under a real model. Then explain what a keyword-built uncertainty index would record in the month uncertainty resolves, and in a month when officials describe it with a synonym. Finally, say which kind of measurement error each failure adds to a downstream regression.

*Check:* I ran this on a modified copy of notebooks/local_llm_uncertainty.py with the jupytext command from the rules. The whole notebook runs in about 2.4 s, and no engine is available in the work env, so MockProvider answers. The appended cell is: `print("provider:", provider.name)`, then the PROBES/llm_prob_kernel call above, then `if provider.name == "mock": assert p_resolved == p_april == 1.0; assert p_synonym == 0.0` and `else: print("resolved below April:", p_resolved < p_april, "| synonym above resolved:", p_synonym > p_resolved)`. ## notebooks/07_local_projections.py (lp_smooth, lp_asymmetric and panel_lp_dk appear only in prose at :287-296)

**[basic] Smooth LP nests Jordà: lambda = 0 switches the penalty off** (revise)

Barnichon–Brownlees (2019) smooth LP writes beta_h = sum_k b_k(h) theta_k on a cubic B-spline basis and fits all horizons jointly. It penalizes lambda * theta' P theta, where P is a second-difference roughness penalty. Using the notebook's `df`, `horizons`, `H` and the section-2 Jordà IRF `lin`, run:
`from puremacro.lp.smooth import lp_smooth`
`full = dict(y="y", x="shock", horizons=horizons, n_lags=2, n_knots=H - 3)`. This gives H+1 basis functions, one per horizon.
`s_zero = lp_smooth(df, **full, lam=0.0)`; `s_inf = lp_smooth(df, **full, lam=1e12)`; `s_gcv = lp_smooth(df, **full, selection="gcv")`.
Before running, predict three things: (a) what `s_zero['beta']` must equal; (b) the effective degrees of freedom `s_inf.df_lambda`, and why that number (what is the null space of a second-difference penalty?); (c) whether the GCV fit's df and roughness (sum of squared second differences of beta over h) land between the two limits.
Prompt: re-run `s_inf` with `penalty_order=1` and predict its df first. It should be 1 because the null space is now a flat line. Smoothing is shrinkage toward a polynomial in h, and lambda chooses how far to shrink.

*Check:* Asserts: `np.allclose(s_zero['beta'].values, lin['beta'].values, atol=1e-10)`; `abs(s_inf.df_lambda - 2.0) < 1e-3`; `2.0 < s_gcv.df_lambda < H + 1`; `rough(s_gcv['beta']) < rough(lin['beta'])` with `rough = lambda b: float(np.sum(np.diff(np.asarray(b), 2)**2))`. RAN in a modified scratch copy through the rule-4 jupytext command. The whole notebook with all three new cells took 4.9 s wall and all asserts passed. It printed: 'lambda=0: max |beta_smooth - beta_Jorda| = 5.0e-16'; 'effective df: lambda=0 -> 17.00, GCV lambda=6.92 -> 11.93, lambda=inf -> 2.000'; 'roughness sum(D2 beta)^2: Jorda 0.8028  GCV-smooth 0.4396'; 'penalty_order=1, lambda=inf -> df 1.000'. The forbidden-module list was still [] after the cells ran, so the notebook stays Pyodide-clean. lp_smooth and lp_asymmetric are already loaded via puremacro/lp/__init__.py:12-13.

**[intermediate] A sign asymmetry, and the average a linear LP reports instead** (revise)

Plant a sign asymmetry, in the spirit of the 'pushing on a string' idea that tightening bites more than easing. The DGP is y_t = 0.5 y_{t-1} + a_pos*max(x_{t-1},0) + a_neg*min(x_{t-1},0) + e_t with `a_pos, a_neg = 1.0, 0.3`. Use `rng_a = np.random.default_rng(7)`, `Ta = 500`, `xa = rng_a.standard_normal(Ta)` and `ea = 0.5*rng_a.standard_normal(Ta)` (draw e as a vector before the loop), and build `dfa = pd.DataFrame({'y': ya, 'shock': xa})`.
Estimate `lp_asymmetric` (from `puremacro.lp.asymmetric`), which splits x_t into x_t*1{x_t>0} and x_t*1{x_t<0}. Also run the linear `lp_hac` on the same data, both with `horizons=range(0, 9), n_lags=2, alpha=0.10`.
(1) Derive the true IRFs: 0 at h=0 and a*0.5^(h-1) for h>=1. Check that the h=1 estimates recover a_pos and a_neg and that their 90% bands separate.
(2) Show that the linear LP is neither response but a weighted average: slope = w*a_pos + (1-w)*a_neg with w = Cov(x, x+)/Var(x), where x+ = max(x, 0). Compute w from the sample.
Prompts: (a) set `a_neg = a_pos` and watch the separation assert fire, because there is no asymmetry to detect. (b) Switch to right-skewed shocks, `xa = rng_a.exponential(1.0, Ta) - 1.0`. Predict first how w moves (about 0.75) and how the linear IRF moves with it. The 'average effect' a linear LP reports depends on the shock distribution, not only on the economics.

*Check:* Asserts at h=1 (r1 = asy[asy['h']==1].iloc[0]; b1, se1 = linear beta and se at h=1; w_pos = np.cov(xa, np.maximum(xa,0))[0,1]/np.var(xa, ddof=1); pred = w_pos*a_pos + (1-w_pos)*a_neg): `abs(r1['beta_pos']-a_pos) < 3*r1['se_pos'] and abs(r1['beta_neg']-a_neg) < 3*r1['se_neg']`; `r1['hi_neg'] < r1['lo_pos']`; `r1['beta_neg'] < b1 < r1['beta_pos']`; `abs(b1 - pred) < 3*se1`. RAN in the jupytext scratch copy and all asserts passed. It printed: 'h=1: beta_pos=0.987 (planted 1.0)  beta_neg=0.318 (planted 0.3)'; 'h=1: linear LP beta=0.616  predicted w_pos*a_pos+(1-w_pos)*a_neg = 0.612 (w_pos=0.45)'. Robustness (plain scripts): the same DGP fails the combined check on 2 of 200 seeds. Skewed-shock variant at seed 7: beta_pos 0.972±0.031, beta_neg 0.449±0.078, linear 0.839±0.028 vs predicted 0.822 (w=0.75), all asserts pass, 4 of 100 seeds fail. With a_neg=1.0 the separation assert fires as intended.

**[stretch] Does smoothing pay? A Monte Carlo bias–variance check on a planted hump** (revise)

The proposal asks how much variance B-spline smoothing removes. A single sample cannot answer that, so run a Monte Carlo. Planted hump: y_t = 0.4 y_{t-1} + sum_{k=0}^{9} w_k x_{t-k} + e_t with `w_hump = [0, .2, .5, .8, 1, 1, .8, .5, .2, 0]`, x_t iid N(0,1), e_t ~ N(0, noise_sd^2), `noise_sd = 2.0`, `T_mc = 200`. Derive the true IRF by recursion, psi_h = 0.4*psi_{h-1} + w_h (peak 1.565 at h=5).
For seeds 0..59, estimate `lp_hac` and `lp_smooth(..., n_knots=4, selection='gcv')` at h = 0..12 with `n_lags=2`. Compute four things: the variance ratio sum_h Var(smooth)/sum_h Var(Jordà); the MSE of each against psi, summed over h; the bias of each at the peak h=5; and the correlation of the Jordà errors at h=5 and h=6.
Prompt: set `noise_sd = 0.5`, the value used in puremacro/examples/lp_smooth_demo.py. Now the first assert fires, because smoothing removes only about 2–3% of the variance and MSE rises. Use the printed error correlation to explain why. When the LP errors at adjacent horizons are almost perfectly correlated, the estimation noise is itself smooth in h. A roughness penalty cannot remove it and only shaves the hump (bias). Smoothing pays when horizon-by-horizon errors are less correlated.

*Check:* Asserts at default noise_sd=2.0: `var_ratio < 0.95`; `bias5_smo < bias5_raw` (smoothing shaves the peak); `mse_smo < mse_raw`. RAN in the jupytext scratch copy; all asserts passed and the Monte Carlo took 1.1 s. It printed: 'noise_sd=2.0: var(smooth)/var(Jorda) = 0.862; MSE Jorda 0.761 vs smooth 0.711'; 'peak (h=5, true 1.565) bias: Jorda -0.057, smooth -0.142; corr of Jorda errors h=5,6: 0.70'. Robustness over 6 disjoint 60-seed blocks at noise_sd=2.0: variance ratio 0.838–0.862, smooth MSE always lower, smooth peak bias always more negative. At noise_sd=0.5 over 3 blocks: variance ratio 0.971–0.977, smooth MSE above Jordà (e.g. 0.444 vs 0.415), error correlation 0.94–0.95, so the prompt's assert fires as described.

## notebooks/17_identification_spec_curve.py (lists non-Gaussian ICA only in prose)

**[basic] Identification up to permutation and sign: a planted test** (revise)

ICA identification promises to recover B0 with no sign or zero restrictions, but only up to the order and sign of its columns. Test that on a model where you know the answer. Simulate a bivariate VAR(1) x_t = A x_{t-1} + B0 eps_t for T=500 periods, with A=[[0.5,0.10],[0.05,0.4]] and B0=[[1,0.5],[0.3,1]]. The shocks are iid Laplace, standardized to unit variance: rng=np.random.default_rng(0); e=rng.laplace(size=(T,2)); e=(e-e.mean(0))/e.std(0). Estimate B0 two ways. First, `non_gaussian_svar(Ys, p=1, horizon=8, seed=0)` from puremacro.var.identify. Second, the Cholesky factor of Sigma from `estimate_var(Ys, 1)` (puremacro.var.estimate). Write match_error(B_hat): the smallest max|B_hat - B0| over all column permutations and column sign flips. Which estimator recovers B0? Look at `.kurtosis`: why does ICA list the true second shock first? And why can no purely statistical method tell you which column is 'the uncertainty shock'?

Kurtosis = [4.91, 2.62]; ICA error 0.022; Cholesky B0 = [[1.115, 0], [0.705, 0.765]], error 0.500. Seed 0 is required: at data seeds 1-4 the ICA error ranges from 0.058 to 0.122.

**[intermediate] Near-Gaussian shocks: ICA's version of the first-stage F** (revise)

Repeat the planted test for 20 samples (data seeds 0-19, T=500) under four shock laws, standardizing each to unit variance: Laplace, Student-t(10), t(30) and Gaussian. For each law, record the median match_error and the share of samples where r.lr_test['p_value'] < 0.05. The LR statistic is a kurtosis test for Gaussian shocks. Where does identification break down? Explain why this test plays the same role for ICA that the first-stage F plays for the proxy SVAR (Prompt 3 above; notebook 14). Optional: rerun at T=2000. Which failure is a small-sample problem, and which one more data cannot fix?

A separate scratch run at T=2000 gave t30 (0.304, 0.60) and gauss (0.571, 0.10). The cell adds about 0.6 s.

Run `non_gaussian_svar(Y, p=P, horizon=H, seed=42)` on the notebook's baseline Y. Do the data reject Gaussian structural shocks? Look at `lr_test` and `kurtosis`. ICA returns four unlabelled shocks, ordered by kurtosis. Label as 'uncertainty' the column j with the largest share of u's impact variance, B0[0,j]^2 / sum_k B0[0,k]^2. Add runs['non-Gaussian'] = unit_effect(r.irf[0,0,j], r.irf[:,1,j], None, None) (unit_effect also flips ICA's arbitrary sign) and re-run the figure cell. Which camp does identification by independence side with: Cholesky/max-share, or sign/narrative/proxy? ## notebooks/26_cycles_and_bandpass

**[stretch] Filters manufacture cycles: HP, Baxter-King and Hamilton on a pure random walk** (revise)

The introduction repeats Hamilton's (2018) warning that a filter can create a 'cycle' the data do not have. Test it on series that have no cycle at all. Use rng = np.random.default_rng(0) to simulate N=200 driftless random walks, x = np.cumsum(rng.normal(0, 1, 400)). Pass each one through hp_filter(x, lamb=1600), baxter_king_filter(x, low=6, high=32, K=12) and hamilton_filter(x, h=8, p=4).
(a) For HP and BK, drop 40 observations at each end of the cycle (n = 320) and demean. Then average the periodograms P = |np.fft.rfft(c)|**2 / n across the 200 draws.
(b) Theory. With sigma = 1, a linear filter with gain G(w) turns a random walk into a cycle whose spectrum, in these periodogram units, is S(w) = G(w)^2 / (2(1 - cos w)).
- For HP, G(w) = 4*lam*(1 - cos w)^2 / (1 + 4*lam*(1 - cos w)^2). Show that S peaks where 4*lam*(1 - cos w)^2 = 3, then give that period in quarters for lam = 1600.
- For BK, recover the 25 weights by filtering a unit impulse (e = np.zeros(101); e[50] = 1; keep the output entries 38:63). Confirm that the weights sum to zero, which is the zero-frequency constraint in the math cell, and form G(w) = sum_k w_k cos(k w).
- Plot the theoretical S against the averaged periodograms at the Fourier frequencies 2*pi*j/n.
(c) In population, regressing x_{t+8} on a constant and x_t, ..., x_{t-3} gives a coefficient of 1 on x_t, so for a random walk the Hamilton cycle is x_{t+8} - x_t. Derive its autocorrelations, (8-k)/8, and its spectrum, which is proportional to (1 - cos 8w)/(1 - cos w). Find where that spectrum is largest and where its zeros are. Compare the autocorrelations with the average autocorrelations of the estimated cycles (drop the first 11 NaNs and demean).
Which filters produce a 'business cycle' whose period comes from the filter rather than from the data?

*Check:* Ran it: I appended the check to a scratch copy of 26_cycles_and_bandpass.py and executed it with the jupytext command. The whole notebook took about 4.5 s and every assert passed. Code:
w = 2*np.pi*np.arange(1, n//2+1)/n; xw = 1-np.cos(w)
S_hp = (4*lam*xw**2/(1+4*lam*xw**2))**2/(2*xw); S_bk = G_bk**2/(2*xw)
peak_hp_theory = 2*np.pi/np.arccos(1-np.sqrt(3/(4*lam)))
assert abs(peak_hp_theory - 30.1) < 0.2
assert abs(bk_w.sum()) < 1e-12
assert np.corrcoef(P_hp[1:], S_hp)[0,1] > 0.98 and 0.9 < P_hp[1:].mean()/S_hp.mean() < 1.1
assert np.corrcoef(P_bk[1:], S_bk)[0,1] > 0.98 and 0.9 < P_bk[1:].mean()/S_bk.mean() < 1.1
assert np.allclose(acf_ham[1:], (8-np.arange(1,9))/8, atol=0.05)
Printed with seed 0:
- HP: closed-form peak 30.14 q; the averaged periodogram peaks at 29.1 q; corr(P_hp, S_hp) = 0.9978; level ratio 0.986.
- BK: weights sum to 6.8e-17; theoretical peak 22.9 q (23.5 q on a fine grid); empirical peak 22.9 q; corr = 0.9980; level ratio 0.987.
- Hamilton autocorrelations, lags 1-8: 0.869, 0.740, 0.614, 0.491, 0.369, 0.249, 0.128, 0.007. Theory: 0.875 down to 0.
Across seeds 0-9 the shape correlations stayed above 0.995 and the level ratios between 0.976 and 1.008. Across seeds 0-19 the Hamilton lag-4 autocorrelation ranged from 0.486 to 0.494 and lag 8 from -0.002 to 0.013. The raw location of the periodogram peak is noisy across seeds (HP 24.6-32 q, BK 20-29.1 q), so the check tests the shape and level of the whole spectrum instead.

## notebooks/51_continuous_projection_collocation_and_fem.py (and 53)

**[intermediate] (nb 51) CRRA curvature changes the speed, not the steady state: Euler vs Bellman collocation vs discrete VFI** (revise)

In notebook 51, keep domain, alpha, beta, delta=1 and eval_k, and set the CRRA curvature to sigma = 2. (a) Solve CollocationProblem(domain=domain, orders=8, params={'alpha': alpha, 'delta': delta, 'sigma': 2.0}, beta=beta) twice, once with method='euler' and once with method='bellman' (the Bellman value collocation of the math section, which the notebook never runs). Find each policy's fixed point with scipy.optimize.brentq. Why is it still k_ss = (alpha*beta)^(1/(1-alpha)), the log-utility steady state? Hint: look at the Euler equation at a steady state. (b) Linearise the Euler equation around k_ss. Show that x_{t+1} = lam*x_t, where lam is the stable root of lam^2 - (1 + 1/beta + A)*lam + 1/beta = 0, with A = -beta*f''(k_ss)*c_ss/sigma, f(k) = k^alpha and c_ss = k_ss^alpha - k_ss. Write lam(sig) and check that lam(1) = alpha, the slope of the closed form alpha*beta*k^alpha at k_ss. Compare lam(2) with the central-difference slope of the Euler policy at k_ss. Higher curvature means slower convergence: explain why in terms of the intertemporal elasticity of substitution. (c) Extend compute_euler_residual to CRRA with the ratio (cp/c)**(-sigma). Compare the two policies and their Euler residuals, and compare their value functions with sol.value. The values agree about 300 times more closely than the policies. Why? Hint: the Bellman objective is flat at its maximum, so a value error eps moves the argmax by about sqrt(eps). (d) Add a third method that shares no code with the other two: solve VFIProblem(a_grid=np.linspace(*domain, 1501), z_grid=np.array([0.0]), P_z=np.array([[1.0]]), return_fn=..., beta=beta) with a CRRA(2) return_fn(kp, k, z, xp=np) that returns -inf when consumption is not positive. Its policy is k_grid[sol.policy_aprime[:, 0]]. (e) Rebuild the Euler problem with params key 'gamma' instead of 'sigma'. A parameter the solver silently ignores is the most common calibration bug, so confirm that this spelling reaches the solver.

*Check:* Ran it: I appended this cell to a scratch copy of notebooks/51_continuous_projection_collocation_and_fem.py and executed the copy with jupytext. The whole notebook took 7 s and the cell 0.60 s; exit 0. Assertions: assert sol_E.converged and sol_B.converged; assert abs(kfix_E - k_ss) < 1e-10 and abs(kfix_B - k_ss) < 1e-4; assert abs(lam(1.0) - alpha) < 1e-12; assert lam(2.0) > alpha and abs(slope_E - lam(2.0)) < 1e-4 (slope_E is a central difference with h=1e-5); assert gap_EB < 1e-3 and eul_E < eul_B (gap_EB = max relative |g_E - g_B| on eval_k); assert vfi_gap <= k_grid[1] - k_grid[0] (vfi_gap = max|g_VFI - sol_E.policy(k_grid)|); assert np.array_equal(sol_E.coefficients, sol_G.coefficients). Printed values: fixed point - k_ss was 5.97e-15 for Euler and -2.77e-05 for Bellman. lam(1) = 0.360000000000 and lam(2) = 0.480725, against an Euler slope of 0.480719. The Euler-Bellman policy gap was 7.57e-05 and the value gap 2.52e-07 (on a value scale of about 45.6). Max CRRA Euler residuals were 8.36e-07 for the Euler policy and 9.84e-05 for the Bellman policy. VFI vs Euler collocation: 8.41e-05, below the grid step of 1.27e-04. The gamma/sigma alias gave max|dc| = 0.0. At sigma=5 (prototype run) lam = 0.62963 against a slope of 0.62963, and the Euler-Bellman gap was 2.2e-4.

**[stretch] (nb 53) Reading the sigma column: why dK*/dsigma = 0 and what dg/dsigma measures** (revise)

Experiment 2 of notebook 53 prints dK*/dsigma = +0.0000. Explain this from the steady-state Euler equation 1 = beta*f'(k*), in which sigma does not appear. Then check the explanation. (a) Take the sigma column of dpol (index params_to_diff.index('sigma')). Show that dg/dsigma is negative below k_ss, positive above it, and (numerically) zero at k_ss. Interpret: a higher sigma means a lower IES and slower convergence toward k*. (b) Near k_ss, g(k) is about k_ss + lam(sigma)*(k - k_ss), so dg/dsigma is about lam'(sigma)*(k - k_ss). Write lam(sig) as the stable root of lam^2 - (1 + 1/beta + A)*lam + 1/beta = 0 with A = -beta*f''(k_ss)*c_ss/sig, using alpha_true, beta_true and delta = 1. Compare the k-slope of the IFT sensitivity at k_ss (a central difference of ift_res.policy_gradient with h = 1e-3) with lam'(sigma_true) (a central difference of lam). (c) Rebuild the problem with the curvature spelled gamma: prob_gamma = replace(prob, params={'alpha': alpha_true, 'delta': delta_val, 'gamma': sigma_true}). Differentiate it with compute_ift_gradients(prob_gamma.solve(), prob_gamma, params=['gamma']) and confirm that it reproduces the sigma column. Differentiate with respect to the key the problem was built with: asking for 'gamma' on the sigma-spelled prob returns a zero column, because 'sigma' takes precedence. (d) Use (a) to explain why a moment placed at k = k* could not identify sigma in Experiment 3's GMM.

*Check:* Ran it: I appended this cell to a scratch copy of notebooks/53_exact_analytic_ift_gradients.py and executed the copy with jupytext; the whole notebook took about 9 s, exit 0. Assertions: assert abs(dK_dtheta[s_idx]) < 1e-5 and abs(dg_kss) < 1e-6; assert np.all(dg[eval_k < 0.9*k_ss] < 0) and np.all(dg[eval_k > 1.1*k_ss] > 0); assert abs(cross - dlam) < 1e-3*abs(dlam) + 1e-5; assert np.max(np.abs(ift_gamma.grad_coefficients[:, 0] - ift_res.grad_coefficients[:, s_idx])) < 1e-10. Printed values: dK*/dsigma = 9.35e-07 (against dK*/dbeta = 0.3104) and dg/dsigma(k_ss) = 9.62e-08. The max of dg/dsigma below 0.9 k_ss was -2.392e-03 and the min above 1.1 k_ss was 2.356e-03. The k-slope of dg/dsigma at k_ss was 0.116617 against dlam/dsigma = 0.116651. The gamma column minus the sigma column was 2.17e-19 (max|dc/dsigma| = 2.428e-02). IFT against re-solved central differences in the sigma column: 1.57e-12. My first draft used a 1e-8 tolerance on dK*/dsigma; it failed at 9.35e-07 (Chebyshev N=8 approximation error on the domain (0.05, 0.5)), so I loosened it to 1e-5.

## probe-exercises

**[intermediate] Fix the Minnesota NIW dummies with the BVAR example gallery and a validation case that can see the bug** (revise)

In bvar.py:244-252 the inner equation loop overwrites the own-lag slot with cross_coeff for every j<n-1. The own-first-lag prior mean therefore becomes lambda2 (0.5) instead of 1, with tightness lambda1*lambda2. This feeds minnesota_gibbs (:319), _minnesota_log_marginal_likelihood (:419) and minnesota_optimal_lambda (:498). minnesota_posterior, the equation-by-equation version, is correct.

No notebook calls these functions. Patched runs of the examples:
- bvar_fan_chart: h=1 median moves from -0.187 to -0.208.
- posterior_predictive_fan and gk_robust_from_gibbs: bands shift.
- mcmc_diagnostics: posterior mean moves from 0.514 to 0.562.
- glp_lambda_search: optimal lambda1 moves from 0.2 to 0.3 at every T. Its lambda2 grid becomes degenerate because the NIW dummy cannot encode lambda2. The header's claim that lambda1 relaxes with T is false both before and after.

validation/cases_var.py:113-131 runs at lambda2=1.0, where the bug vanishes, and uses the defective builder as its own reference. Replace it with an own-lag prior-mean check at lambda2≠1 and document that lambda2 is inert in the NIW paths.

*Check:* With data dropped, i.e. the dummy-only regression of Yd on Xd, the prior mean must equal 1 on own lag 1 and 0 elsewhere for every lambda2. At lambda2=1 the NIW posterior mean must equal minnesota_posterior. In the scratch sanity.py check, the Gibbs diagonal (0.736, 0.761, 0.832) matched the equation-by-equation diagonal (0.737, 0.762, 0.833).

**[intermediate] Write the check that would have caught it: an oracle-design exercise set built from real library defects** (revise)

Four graded exercises for the ITAM course's software-for-macro unit, or as Your-turn cells in 12. Basic: the Minnesota tight-prior limit, where the posterior mean must equal the random-walk prior mean for any lambda2 (HEAD gives 0.5 on A[0,0]). Intermediate: a noiseless two-cohort staggered panel, where the student derives theta_es(0)=4.2 and theta^O_W=3.909 from CS eq. 3.4/3.10 and shows the single-cohort check cannot tell 3.0 from 4.2. Intermediate: Geweke size on stationary AR(1) chains (HEAD rejects 27.9% at a nominal 5%). Stretch: Windmeijer against the Stata manual, or the identification() Jacobian against its AR(1) closed form (HEAD singular values 70.94 and 1.895 vs 284.25 and 1.559). Each exercise asks for the oracle's independence argument, the tolerance and why the existing case missed the defect: circular, blind or absent.

*Check:* Closed forms derived in the exercise: the NIW tight-prior limit; CS eq. 3.4/3.10 on a noiseless DGP; the AR(1) long-run variance sigma^2/(1-phi)^2=100; AR(1) moments m=[0, s^2/(1-r^2), r s^2/(1-r^2)]. Stretch uses the published Stata xtabond Example 4 numbers.

## probe-exercises

**[intermediate] When Callaway-Sant'Anna and Sun-Abraham disagree in sign: a cohort-weight diagnostic on a panel with 6/50/166-unit cohorts** (revise)

This is a short exercise cell. It simulates a staggered panel with the same cohort structure as the case measured in this session: three cohorts of 6, 50 and 166 units plus 629 never-treated. Effects are heterogeneous, with large effects in the smallest, earliest cohort. The unweighted post-cell mean then has the opposite sign to the cohort-size-weighted theta^O_sel. Students compute both from att_gt and compare them with sun_abraham, which weights by cohort shares. They then apply the check to their own panel. This is the empirical case behind the ADVISORY 'What to re-run' line, without shipping the research data. The real case is Pandemics N25_identification_inference_upgrades.py:354-360: CS att_overall is +0.0801 with the current library and -0.0597 with the fix, while Sun-Abraham prints -0.0734 in the same cell. The committed output in N25_identification_inference_upgrades_puremacro.ipynb shows +0.0801, and the cell asserts only len(event_study)>0.

*Check:* The planted cohort-by-period effects give theta^O_sel (CS 2021 eq. 3.11) in closed form. The fixed estimator must recover it within Monte Carlo error, the unweighted mean must miss it with a sign flip, and Sun-Abraham must agree with the weighted value.

**[intermediate] Does your SDID interval agree with your placebo p-value? A single-treated-unit consistency check for rebuilt notebook 29** (revise)

This is an exercise cell for the notebook 29 rebuild. From the same loop that builds the placebo p-value (one pseudo-treated donor at a time), compute se_placebo = std(placebo taus) and a normal CI. Compare them with the donor-bootstrap res.se, res.lo and res.hi, and flag any case where the CI excludes 0 but the placebo p is at least alpha. Also report whether omega or lambda came back uniform. The motivating case is the maintainer's research, re-run read-only in scratch. informal_hours/scripts/xc/colombia_reform_sdid.py:63-92 writes the donor-bootstrap se and CI into reports/xc/tables/colombia_reform_sdid.csv. From there they flow to report_crosscountry.md:154-160, which shows the 44 h headline CI [-2.67,-1.61] next to placebo p 0.125. The same values reach write_report_md.py:132, make_model_figures.py:165 and facts_summary.json. With the placebo variance, COL employees 44h has se 0.291->0.899 and CI [-3.59,-0.64]. COL employees 47h goes from [-0.90,-0.16] to [-1.32,0.54], and COL all-employed 46h from [-1.26,-0.30] to [-2.46,0.67]; both now include 0. SLSQP never failed: 0 of 16 main, 8,000 bootstrap and 128 placebo solves. explorations N23 (Britannia, 7 donors) shows the same pattern: se 0.908->1.508 and 95% CI [-2.95,0.44]->[-4.25,1.66].

*Check:* The placebo SE recomputed in the cell must equal the fixed library se exactly, since both use the same J placebos. The consistency flag must fire on the synthetic example built to have a significant donor-bootstrap CI and an insignificant placebo p. The coverage claim (61% at nominal 90%) is the roadmap's rank 11 and was not re-measured here.

## replication case stylized_facts.okun_law_fred (puremacro/replication/cases_stylized_facts.py); notebooks/course/01_business_cycle_facts

**[basic] Okun en diferencias: de la correlación al coeficiente** (revise)

El caso de replicación stylized_facts.okun_law_fred solo exige corr(crecimiento, Δu) <= -0.5. Convierte esa correlación en un coeficiente. Usa `y_us` (log-PIB x100 de GDPC1.csv, sección 3) y `UNRATE.csv` promediado por trimestre (solo trimestres con sus 3 meses). En 1948-2019Q4 estima Δu_t = α + β·g_t + e_t, con g_t = Δy_t, mediante `from puremacro.regress import ols, add_constant` y `ols(du, add_constant(g), cov_type='HAC', cov_kwds={'maxlags': 4})`. Reporta β, su t HAC, corr(g, Δu) y g* = -α/β, el crecimiento trimestral que mantiene constante el desempleo (anualízalo). ¿Por qué |β| queda tan lejos de 1? Piensa en horas por trabajador, participación y productividad (acaparamiento de mano de obra).

*Check:* assert -0.35 < b1 < -0.20; assert m1.tvalues[1] < -5; assert rho1 < -0.5; assert 0.6 < g_star < 1.0  (b1 = m1.params[1], g_star = -m1.params[0]/m1.params[1]). I ran this in a scratch copy of notebooks/course/01_business_cycle_facts_es.py with the jupytext command, and all asserts passed. It printed: n=287 beta=-0.280 seHAC=0.026 t=-10.6 corr=-0.68 g*=0.78 %/q (3.1 %/yr). As an independent cross-check, statsmodels 0.15 OLS HAC(4) matches puremacro.regress.ols (params and bse agree to within 1e-16).

**[intermediate] Okun en brechas: HP frente a Hamilton, y por qué HAC** (revise)

Con la misma muestra (1948Q1-2019Q4), extrae el ciclo del log-PIB y el de u con `hp_filter` y con `hamilton_filter`. Regresa el ciclo de u sobre el ciclo del PIB. Usa HAC con maxlags=4 para HP y maxlags=8 para Hamilton, y compara además el EE HAC de Hamilton con el EE no robusto (`ols` sin cov_type). (a) ¿La versión en brechas da un |β| mayor o menor que la de diferencias del ejercicio anterior? ¿Por qué, si el desempleo se ajusta con rezago? (b) ¿Cuánto cambia β al pasar de HP a Hamilton? Relaciónalo con la salvedad de la sección 1: no hay filtro gratis. (c) El residuo de Hamilton es un error de pronóstico a h=8 trimestres. ¿Por qué eso obliga a usar HAC?

*Check:* assert -0.50 < b_hp < -0.30 and -0.50 < b_hm < -0.30; assert abs(b_hp) > abs(b1) and abs(b_hm) > abs(b1); assert m_hm.bse[1] / m_hm0.bse[1] > 1.5  (m_hm = HAC(8) fit on Hamilton cycles with NaNs dropped, m_hm0 = the same fit with nonrobust SEs). I ran this in the scratch copy with jupytext, and all asserts passed. It printed: beta_HP=-0.439 (seHAC 0.020), beta_Ham=-0.352 (seHAC 0.039, seOLS 0.016, ratio 2.5).

**[intermediate] ¿Es estable la ley de Okun? Submuestras, ventanas móviles y la pandemia** (revise)

Con la versión en diferencias del ejercicio 1: (a) parte la muestra en 1948Q2-1984Q4 y 1985Q1-2019Q4 y compara β y g*. (b) Estima β por MCO en ventanas móviles de 40 trimestres y localiza la ventana con β más cercano a cero. (c) Extiende la muestra hasta 2026Q1, incluida la pandemia, y compara β y su EE HAC con los de la muestra pre-COVID. Interpreta: ¿qué cambió entre submuestras, la pendiente o el crecimiento necesario para sostener u (una lectura del crecimiento potencial)? ¿Por qué una década sin recesión apenas identifica β? ¿Qué le hacen dos trimestres extremos a la estimación y a su incertidumbre?

*Check:* assert abs(bA - bB) < 0.06; assert gA - gB > 0.3; assert roll.max() > -0.10; assert bC < b1 - 0.1 and seC > 3 * seP  (bA/gA and bB/gB = slope and -α/β in the two subsamples, roll = 40-quarter rolling OLS slopes, bC/seC = HAC fit through 2026Q1, seP = HAC SE through 2019Q4). I ran this in the scratch copy with jupytext, and all asserts passed. It printed: 1948-84 beta=-0.294 g*=0.98; 1985-2019 beta=-0.263 g*=0.56; rolling min -0.395 (window ending 1992Q1), max +0.017 (window ending 2019Q3); with 2020-26 beta=-0.476 seHAC=0.130 vs 0.026 pre-COVID.

**[stretch] México frente a EE. UU.: misma correlación, una fracción del coeficiente** (revise)

Construye la tasa de desempleo de México con `pd.read_parquet(DATA / 'enoe_stocks_monthly.parquet')` (u = 100·U/(F+I+U), promedio trimestral; requiere pyarrow). En la ventana común 2005Q2-2024Q4, elimina 2020Q2 en ambos países (la ENOE se suspendió) y estima Δ4u = α + β·Δ4(100·log PIB) para México y para EE. UU. (UNRATE, GDPC1), con HAC y maxlags=6. ¿Qué significa que las dos correlaciones sean parecidas y las pendientes muy distintas? Relaciónalo con los márgenes de informalidad y de participación de la lección 24. Nota: la u del panel ENOE no coincide en nivel con la oficial del INEGI; aquí solo importa la pendiente.

I ran this in the scratch copy with jupytext, and all asserts passed. It printed: MEX n=73 beta=-0.146 seHAC=0.018 corr=-0.76; USA n=73 beta=-0.718 seHAC=0.096 corr=-0.81.

## short exercise attached to curso T02_F (Gertler-Karadi) or a new small showcase notebook

**[basic] Diamond-Dybvig in closed form: why a solvent bank can fail, and two remedies** (revise)

Section 4.3 claims the central bank 'cannot suffer a run'. Private banks can. In a new cell after the verification suite, build the three-date Diamond-Dybvig economy in numpy. Each depositor has a unit endowment, a fraction pi = 0.25 is impatient, and the long asset returns R = 1.5 at t = 2 or 1 if liquidated at t = 1. Utility is CRRA with gamma = 2. (a) Solve for the optimal deposit contract (c1, c2) from u'(c1) = R u'(c2) and pi*c1 + (1 - pi)*c2/R = 1. (b) Write wait_payoff(f, c1): what a patient depositor who waits receives at t = 2 when a fraction f withdraws at t = 1. The bank liquidates f*c1 units, and if f*c1 >= 1 it fails and waiters get 0. Find the tipping point f_bar at which waiting and withdrawing pay the same. Explain why f = pi and f = 1 are both Nash equilibria. (c) Redo (a) with gamma = 1 and explain why the run equilibrium disappears. What does that say about the link between liquidity insurance and fragility? (d) Suspension of convertibility: the bank stops paying once a fraction pi has withdrawn. Show that waiting becomes a dominant strategy for patient depositors. (e) Deposit guarantee: an authority promises waiters c2 whatever f is. Compute its outlay max(c2 - wait_payoff(f), 0)*(1 - f) and show that it is zero in equilibrium. Why is the promise credible only for an agent with taxing power, as in Section 4.3?

*Check:* Student-facing asserts, which test properties and give no answer key: np.isclose(pi*c1+(1-pi)*c2/R, 1); np.isclose(c1**-gamma, R*c2**-gamma); 1 < c1 < c2; np.isclose(wait_payoff(pi, c1), c2); wait_payoff(1-1e-9, c1) == 0 < 1 (withdrawing in a run pays (1/c1)*c1 = 1); pi < f_bar < 1 and np.isclose(wait_payoff(f_bar, c1), c1); np.isclose(c1_log, 1); under suspension wait_payoff(np.minimum(f_grid, pi), c1).min() > c1; np.isclose(outlay(pi), 0) and outlay(0.9) > 0. RAN: I appended the cell to a scratch copy of curso/notebooks/T02_F_gertler_karadi_macrofinance_local.ipynb and executed it with jupytext --execute --run-path curso/notebooks (whole notebook took 5.5 s). All asserts passed. It printed c1=1.1596 c2=1.4202 f_bar=0.5871 c1_log=1.0000, min wait payoff under suspension=1.4202, outlay(0.9)=0.1420. These numbers are vetting evidence only and must not ship as an answer key.

**[intermediate] How big a fire sale makes a Gertler-Karadi bank run-prone?** (revise)

This bridges the Diamond-Dybvig exercise to the GK model; run it after the verification cell, which defines m_gk, df_no, lambda_results and theta_results. Suppose depositors run and the bank must sell its whole portfolio at a haircut d on its gross value Rk*Q*S. Use the balance sheet Q*S = N + B (Section 1.1) and phi = Q*S/N. The recovery rate on deposits is then x(d) = (1 - d)*Rk*phi / (R*(phi - 1)), and a run is self-fulfilling iff x(d) < 1. (a) Write d_star(phi, R, Rk), the haircut at which x = 1. Evaluate it at every steady state in lambda_results and theta_results, using the steady_state keys 'phi_ss', 'R' and 'Rk'. (b) Evaluate it again on impact of the laissez-faire -5% capital-quality shock. Set phi = m_gk.steady_state['phi'] + df_no['phi'].iloc[0] (phi is a level deviation) and Rk = R + prem_ss + df_no['prem'].iloc[0]. (c) Explain four things: why d* is close to the equity cushion 1/phi; why Section 5's 'paradox of financial development' (lower lambda, lower theta, higher leverage) also governs run fragility; why a run needs a smaller fire sale right after a balance-sheet loss even though the fundamental price fall itself is far from run-sized; and why the zero-equity Diamond-Dybvig bank is run-prone as soon as c1 exceeds the liquidation value.

*Check:* Student-facing asserts: for each lam, np.isclose(recovery(d_star[lam], phi, R, Rk), 1); d_star[0.25] < d_star[0.381] < d_star[0.5]; d_star_theta[0.90] < d_star_theta[0.972]; d_post < d_star[0.381]; abs(df_no['Q'].iloc[0]) < d_star[0.381]; abs(d_star[lam] - 1/phi_ss[lam]) < 0.01 for every lam. RAN on the same scratch copy with jupytext --execute; all asserts passed. It printed phi_ss by lambda {0.25: 5.435, 0.381: 4.098, 0.5: 3.39}, d* {0.1851, 0.2459, 0.2974}, phi_ss by theta {0.9: 9.696, 0.972: 4.098}, d* {0.1104, 0.2459}, 1/phi = 0.2440. On laissez-faire impact: Q -10.78%, N -85.24%, dphi +1.8766, so phi_post = 5.975 and d*_post = 0.1816. Also confirmed lambda_results[0.381] equals df_no. These numbers are evidence only; do not ship them.

**[stretch] Global games: pinning down the run threshold by iterated best responses** (revise)

In the Diamond-Dybvig exercise, sunspots decide which of the two equilibria happens. Remove common knowledge instead. The bank's liquidity buffer theta is unknown, and depositor i observes x_i = theta + sigma*eps_i with eps_i ~ N(0, 1) under a flat prior, so theta | x ~ N(x, sigma^2). The bank fails iff the fraction withdrawing, l, exceeds theta. Waiting pays 1 if the bank survives and 0 if it fails; withdrawing pays r = 0.4 for sure. (a) If all others withdraw iff x < x', then l(theta) = Phi((x' - theta)/sigma). Compute the failure threshold theta'(x') with scipy.optimize.brentq. (b) Derive a depositor's best-response signal threshold given theta'. (c) Iterate the best response from x0 = -1 and from x0 = 2 (iterated deletion of dominated strategies) for sigma in {0.2, 0.05, 0.01}, and report how the iteration count changes as sigma falls. (d) Add partial deposit insurance: a waiter receives s in {0, 0.1, 0.2, 0.3} if the bank fails. Explain why banks with theta in (0, theta*) are solvent yet fail, and what insurance does to that region.

*Check:* Student-facing asserts: from both starting points the thresholds agree to 1e-8, and for s = 0, abs(theta_star - r) < 1e-8 for every sigma (the Laplacian-belief prediction, which students derive). For s > 0, the marginal depositor is indifferent: with p = 1 - norm.cdf((theta_star - x_star)/sigma), np.isclose(p*1 + (1-p)*s, r). theta_star(s) is strictly decreasing in s. RAN in the same scratch notebook; all asserts passed. It printed: sigma=0.2 x*=0.349331 theta*=0.40000000 (63/64 iterations from low/high start); sigma=0.05 x*=0.387333 theta*=0.40000000 (201/207); sigma=0.01 x*=0.397467 theta*=0.40000000 (893/918); theta*(s) for s=0, .1, .2, .3 = [0.4, 0.333333, 0.25, 0.142857]. Evidence only; do not ship.
